## Confidentiality

Definition

Concern for confidentiality exists throughout the social services delivery system. However, system of care emphasizes interagency collaboration. The

purpose of this section is to provide guidance surrounding collaboration with the understanding that the provision of child protective services (CPS) involves responsibility for the sensitive management of confidential information.

The rationale for stringent confidentiality rules in CPS is concern for the protection of the child. CPS records contain detailed information about child

maltreatment, which may include graphic documentation received during CPS assessment activities and verbatim documentation of the child's version of maltreatment. Information provided by the initial reporter, supportive statements from collateral contacts, and other such information is collected because of a thorough CPS assessment by the agency. Families have a right to privacy. More critically, the child has a right to protection from public disclosure of information learned during the CPS assessment. North Carolina General Statute (N.C.G.S.) § 7B-2901, subsections c., d., h., and i., address the confidentiality of protective services records for children in the custody of the Department of Social Services or under placement by the court. N.C.G.S. §108A-80 addresses confidentiality of all social services records.

The North Carolina Administrative Code (10A NCAC 70A .0113) specifically defines the conditions under which a director may allow access to a child's

protective services record.

North Carolina S.L. 2023-14 § 7B-524. Confidentiality of information and records specifies confidentiality of a surrendering parent in a Safe Surrender

case.

Per N.C.G.S. § 7B-302(a3)<br>any individual member of the North Carolina General Assembly (NCGA) or joint legislative<br>oversight committee of the North Carolina General Assembly may request access to confidential<br>information and records necessary for oversight of programs related to child protective services<br>except those protected by federal law, including state plan requirements with federal programs.<br>This, along withN.C.G.S § 7B-302 (e),expands the release of confidential information from<br>county held records to qualified**out-of-state**entities and to situations other than an open CPS<br>assessment.||

## Intensive Family Preservation Services (IFPS Family Service Manual)

Definition

INTENSIVE FAMILY PRESERVATION SERVICES (IFPS FAMILY SERVICE MANUAL)

• Provide intensive, in-home crisis intervention services designed to help families with children at imminent risk of being removed from the home; • Are time-limited (a maximum of six weeks); and • Are characterized by very small caseloads for workers, 24-hour availability of staff, and the provision of services primarily in the child(ren)'s home.

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|**Intensive Family Preservation**<br>**I.**<br>**INTRODUCTION**<br>North Carolina's Family Preservation Services is a model that it is a short-term, intensive, crisis-intervention program<br>with services provided primarily in the family's home or community. North Carolina General Statute §143-150<br>specifies the program design requirements for the Intensive Family Preservation Services (IFPS) Program. They are as<br>follows:<br>1. Each eligible family shall receive intensive family preservation services— beginning with identification of a child<br>who is at imminent risk of out-of- home placement—for an average of four weeks but not more than six<br>consecutive weeks;<br>2. At least one-half of a caseworker 's time spent providing family preservation services to each eligible family shall<br>be provided in the family's home and/or community;<br>3. Family preservation caseworkers shall be available to each eligible family by telephone and on-call for visits 24<br>hours a day, seven days a week;<br>4. Each family preservation caseworker shall provide services to a maximum of four families at any given time.<br>A.**GOALS AND OBJECTIVES**|IFPS should be<br>considered as an<br>option for all cases in<br>which there is a risk<br>rating of "high."<br>Contact between<br>IFPS and the county<br>child welfare worker<br>can either be over<br>the telephone, via e-<br>mail, or in person.|

Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL Page 14 Return to: Cross Function Topics Return to: Manual TOC

<!-- page: 15 -->
# Intensive Family Preservation Services (IFPS Family Service Manual)

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|The goal of North Carolina's Family Preservation Services Program is to prevent unnecessary placement of children<br>away from their families by providing in-home services aimed at restoring families in crisis to an acceptable level of<br>functioning delivered within a System of Care framework. These services are designed to meet the following<br>objectives:<br>• Stabilize the crisis which put the family at imminent risk;<br>• Keep the child, family, and community safe by defusing the potential for violence (physical, sexual, emotional,<br>and/or verbal abuse); and<br>• Help families develop the skills, competencies, and resources they need to handle future crisis more effectively.<br>II. **AGENCY STANDARDS & PROGRAM DESIGN**<br>**A. ELIGIBILITY**<br>Families eligible for IFPS services have at least one child age birth through 17 years who is at imminent risk of<br>placement in out-of-home care. Families with adopted children are eligible for referral to the program when it is<br>determined that a disruption in the adoption placement will occur without intensive services. The priority for case<br>assignment are those children at risk of placement into the child welfare system, and at least 75% of cases served each<br>year shall be county child welfare agency referred cases, but referrals may also come from the mental health,<br>developmental disabilities, substance abuse, or juvenile justice systems.<br>Out-of-home placement means the removal of a child from his or her own home with placement in a non-relative<br>setting for a duration of two weeks or more. Responsibility for his or her supervision, care and essentials of daily living<br>is given to the county child welfare agency, juvenile justice, or public or private mental health/developmental<br>disabilities/ substance abuse systems.<br>At least one parent or another primary caretaker must indicate that he or she is willing and able to participate in IFPS.<br>Note: Primary caretaker means a parent (including adoptive or long-term foster parent), legal guardian, or someone<br>who is acting in loco parentis (has assumed the status and obligation of a parent) in relation to the child(ren).<br>Imminent risk of out-of-home placement is defined as follows:<br>For county child welfare agency referred cases:||

The Community-Based Programs Team of the North Carolina<br>Division of Social Services must approve the tool utilized prior to referrals based on that tool being accepted.<br>**B. AGENCY REQUIREMENTS**<br>NCDSS requirements are as follows:<br>Contract Compliance:<br>•<br>Develop a working knowledge of State policy and procedures regarding IFPS and ensure staff compliance. Supervisors shall directly<br>serve some families each year.<br>•<br>Assign a caseload of two families to each IFPS worker. The Contractor may assign a third family only during<br>the final week of an intervention.<br>•<br>Maintain a supervisory/direct service staff ratio that ordinarily shall be one full-time supervisor to no more<br>than five full-time IFPS workers.<br>•<br>Ensure IFPS workers and supervisors work a flexible schedule determined by the needs of the families<br>(rather than a standard 8am to 5pm schedule). The<br>Contractor must ensure that the supplemental employment and/or educational commitment does not<br>negatively impact services provided by the contract.<br>•<br>Each new IFPS worker shall shadow the supervisor for at least one case and be shadowed by the supervisor<br>for at least one case before being assigned cases. (Shadowing shall be defined as accompanying the person<br>responsible for the case on at least half of the home visits to the family.)<br>•<br>The IFPS supervisor shall accompany experienced staff on home visits, as needed. Feedback on<br>accompanied visits shall be part of the weekly individual conference or team meeting.<br>Supervision:<br>•<br>Conduct regularly scheduled team meetings involving all IFPS staff, including the IFPS supervisor. PROGRAM REQUIREMENTS**<br>Ensure IFPS workers attempt to make face-to-face contact with the family within 24 hours (immediately in the case of<br>an emergency) from time of referral to IFPS. Services may be extended up<br>to a maximum of six weeks when an extension will substantially decrease the chance of an out-of-home<br>placement. # Intensive Family Preservation Services (IFPS Family Service Manual)

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|•<br>Require IFPS workers to develop a Service Plan Report for each family. The IFPS worker shall establish the service<br>plan and goals in collaboration with the family. The family's involvement shall be clearly documented by the<br>signing of the final Service Plan.<br>•<br>The Service Plan shall address safety measures put into place and shall include, but not be limited to:<br>−<br>identifying family strengths;<br>−<br>helping the family define the specific goals of intervention;<br>−<br>showing the family how improvements can occur;<br>−<br>helping the family resolve or improve safety concerns by such techniques as examining positive<br>alternatives for negative behaviors;<br>−<br>teaching skills to prevent the reoccurrence of abuse and neglect and other family conflict; and<br>−<br>connecting with resources to maintain ongoing progress.<br>•<br>The Contractor shall submit the Service Plan to the referring staff within 14 days from the time of the referral to<br>IFPS. An In-Home Family Services Agreement developed as a part of a Child and Family Team Meeting (CFT) will<br>be enough in place of the Service Plan if the IFPS worker is present during the CFT meeting.<br>•<br>Develop a written plan to administer flexible monies averaging $500 per family. These funds will be used to<br>purchase concrete supports, such as furniture, utilities, and respite care. This plan must be available to the IFPS<br>Worker and State IFPS Consultant.<br>Discuss termination recommendations with the referring staff. A termination conference shall occur no later than<br>seven days prior to the anticipated closure of the case. The family may be invited to attend the termination<br>conference.<br>•<br>Conduct a termination meeting with the family to summarize the progress made during the IFPS intervention and<br>options for maintaining progress. The meeting shall occur at the final family session. When possible, a CFT to<br>discuss termination held with the referring staff and family will meet this requirement. The case shall be<br>considered closed as of that date.<br>•<br>Within seven days of case closure, send to the family, and to the referring staff, a Termination Report signed by<br>the appropriate IFPS staff which shall summarize the progress the family made during the IFPS intervention.||

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|

|•<br>Administer the Family Satisfaction Survey and Referring Worker Survey upon termination of each case to<br>determine satisfaction with the IFPS program.<br>•<br>Contact the most involved parent in each family served by the IFPS program and administer a follow-up<br>evaluation at six and twelve months after termination.<br>If a home visit is not possible, a telephone contact shall be attempted. Five telephone attempts to contact shall be<br>made and documented. This evaluation shall determine the status of the family and whether out-of-home placement<br>of a child has occurred.<br>**SERVICE REQUIREMENTS**<br>North Carolina General Statute § 143B-150.6requires:<br>**FAMILY ASSESSMENT**<br>Family assessment is an ongoing process, which continues from intake through termination of services. The purpose of<br>the family assessment is:<br>•<br>To identify the family's strengths, resources, needs, and problems;<br>•<br>To help family members identify measurable, realistic, achievable, and time-limited treatment goals;<br>•<br>To help family members develop strategies for achieving their goals.<br>Consistent with System of Care practice, the family assessment is comprehensive and considers the family's strengths<br>as well as needs within its entire social context (i.e. nuclear family, extended family, school, work, church,<br>neighborhood, etc.). Family assessment information is gathered through:<br>•<br>Ongoing observation of family members in their natural environment;<br>•<br>Family and individual meetings;<br>•<br>A social history gathered over time through interactions with family members;<br>•<br>Reports from other professionals;<br>•<br>Assessment instruments completed by/with family members.||

<!-- page: 23 -->
# Intensive Family Preservation Services (IFPS Family Service Manual)

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|Specialized assessments are considered on a case-by-case basis. These may include neurological, nutritional, speech<br>and language, developmental, offender, and psychological, as well as alcohol and drug screenings, blood chemistry,<br>vocational evaluations, etc.<br>**INTENSIVE FAMILY AND INDIVIDUAL COUNSELING**<br>Counseling in this context means the therapeutic interaction between IFPS workers and family members focused on<br>problem-solving and skill building. Intensity is characterized by small caseloads (averaging two families per worker)<br>and high service hours of up to 20 hours per week per family, within a limited timeframe (four to six consecutive<br>weeks per family). Counseling services may include individual, couple, family, and group counseling. During<br>counseling, the IFPS worker and family members:<br>•<br>Establish a trusting relationship;<br>•<br>Define and clarify family issues and perceptions;<br>•<br>Assess the need for change and develop and implement strategies for making those changes;<br>•<br>Evaluate progress and make appropriate changes to strategies and goals; and<br>•<br>Effectively terminate the relationship.<br>**CLIENT ADVOCACY**<br>Client advocacy means providing the family with information about the services available in the community and<br>helping the family to obtain them. It also means helping families learn to advocate for themselves and to negotiate<br>with bureaucracies and service systems to obtain needed help.<br>**CASE MANAGEMENT**<br>Case management in this context means that the IFPS worker takes responsibility for assuring that the needs of the<br>family as a whole are assessed; that service plans are created with the family and reflect its priorities and goals; and<br>that the efforts of all service providers involved with the family are coordinated and consistent with theService Plan.<br>This is also consistent with the foundational philosophy of System of Care. The goals of family-centered case<br>management are to:<br>•<br>Develop joint service plans which delineate the roles and functions of all agencies involved with the family;||

# Intensive Family Preservation Services (IFPS Family Service Manual)

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|•<br>Ensure that the efforts of all agencies are directed toward common goals;<br>•<br>Ensure that methods and techniques of various service providers do not conflict or confuse family members; and<br>•<br>Ensure assessment of a family's need for follow-up services and to plan with appropriate agencies for follow-up<br>services to be provided.<br>**DEVELOPMENT AND ENHANCEMENT OF PARENTING SKILLS**<br>Through the assessment, counseling, and case management processes, parenting skill deficits will be identified. The<br>IFPS worker offers instruction in these skills and provides support as parents implement newly learned skills. This<br>involves a wide range of parenting activities and functions such as nurturing, age-appropriate expectations, adequate<br>supervision, acceptable discipline, behavior management, communication, anger control, etc.<br>**REFERRAL TO OTHER SERVICES AS APPROPRIATE**<br>In keeping with the System of Care, the IFPS worker provides family members with information on the range of useful<br>community resources and helps the family access necessary services. The family makes its own decisions about which<br>services it will use and participates in meetings with service providers.<br>**RECORDKEEPING AND DOCUMENTATION OF SERVICES**<br>Documentation on the case includes completion of all required forms as appropriate. Regardless of the referral source,<br>it is also important to include any other documented information pertinent to service delivery (e.g., behavior plans,<br>letters written on behalf of the client/family, etc.). # Family Reunification Services

| Purpose | |
|---|---|
|**FAMILY REUNIFICATION SERVICES (SOCIAL SECURITY ACT AND FAMILY FIRST PREVENTION SERVICES ACT)**<br>The eligible services and activities described below that are provided to a child who is removed from the child's home and placed in a foster<br>family home or a child care institution or a child who has been returned home and to the parents or primary caregiver of such a child, in order<br>to facilitate the reunification of the child safely and appropriately within a timely fashion and to ensure the strength and stability of the<br>reunification (Social Security Act- Promoting Safe and Stable Families Definitions)<br>•<br>Reunification is defined as placement of the juvenile in the home of either parent or placement of the juvenile in the home of a<br>guardian or custodian from whose home the child was removed by court order (N.C.G.S. § 7B-101)<br>•<br>In the case of a child who has been returned home, the services and activities shall only be provided during the 15-month period that<br>begins on the date that the child physically returns home.<br>•<br>The child must have the goal of reunification on the North Carolina Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement - DSS-5240.|**FAMILY REUNIFICATION SERVICES (SOCIAL SECURITY ACT AND FAMILY FIRST PREVENTION SERVICES ACT)**<br>The eligible services and activities described below that are provided to a child who is removed from the child's home and placed in a foster<br>family home or a child care institution or a child who has been returned home and to the parents or primary caregiver of such a child, in order<br>to facilitate the reunification of the child safely and appropriately within a timely fashion and to ensure the strength and stability of the<br>reunification (Social Security Act- Promoting Safe and Stable Families Definitions)<br>•<br>Reunification is defined as placement of the juvenile in the home of either parent or placement of the juvenile in the home of a<br>guardian or custodian from whose home the child was removed by court order (N.C.G.S. § 7B-101)<br>•<br>In the case of a child who has been returned home, the services and activities shall only be provided during the 15-month period that<br>begins on the date that the child physically returns home.<br>•<br>The child must have the goal of reunification on the North Carolina Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement - DSS-5240.|
|Protocol - Whatyou must do|Guidance - Howyou should do it|
|**POLICY AND LEGAL BASIS:**<br>Effective June 1, 2018, the Family First Prevention Services Act<br>(FFPSA):<br>•<br>Eliminated the previous 15-month time-limit on the use of<br>Title IV-B funds for family reunification services for children<br>in foster care;<br>•<br>Provided a child returning home with access to 15-months<br>of family reunification services, beginning on the date the<br>child returns home; and<br>•<br>Changed the name of the program from "Time-Limited<br>Family Reunification Services" to "Family Reunification<br>Services."<br>(PI-18-07- Public Law 115-123, the Family First Services<br>Act)||

<!-- page: 27 -->
# Family Reunification Services

|N.C.G.S. §7B-906.2(d) Permanent Plans; concurrent planning<br>requires the court to make written findings regarding reunification:<br>• Whether the parent is making adequate progress within a<br>reasonable period of time under the plan.<br>• Whether the parent is actively participating in or<br>cooperating with the plan, the department, and the<br>guardian ad litem for the juvenile.<br>• Whether the parent remains available to the court, the<br>department, and the guardian ad litem for thejuvenile.<br>• Whether the parent is acting in a manner inconsistent with<br>the health or safety of the juvenile<br>ELIGIBILITY:<br>Eligibility for Family Reunification Services must be certified<br>through documentation of the following referral/acceptance<br>criteria:<br>Parent or Caretaker Criteria:<br>• At least one of the child's parents/caregivers whom the<br>child was removed from must be willing to participate in<br>reunification on a voluntary basis; AND<br>• The parent/caregiver must be willing to work to achieve<br>goals of the North Carolina Permanency Planning Family<br>Services Agreement (DSS-5240)<br>Child(ren) Criteria:<br>• The child must be in the custody or placement authority of<br>a county child welfare agency; AND<br>• The child must either be in a current out of home<br>placement or recently moved from an out of home<br>placement to the home of the parent/caregiver to be<br>reunified; AND|GOALS AND OBJECTIVES<br>The primary goal of family reunification services is to support the family<br>in eliminating the conditions which led to the child's removal and to build<br>protective factors that enable the parent(s) to keep the child safe.<br>These services are designed to help families develop the skills,<br>competencies and resources necessary to provide the child(ren) with a<br>safe and nurturing environment and access personal and community<br>supports to case of crisis.<br>Reunification must occur as soon as possible when concerns that<br>precipitated the child's removal have been alleviated and parents can<br>demonstrate their ability to provide a minimum sufficient level of care<br>and ensure safety. Division of Social Services. (2020). Permanency<br>Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance|

<!-- page: 31 -->
## Family Reunification Services

Definitions Child Placement Services are designed to:

• Strengthen, preserve and/or reunite families after children have come into agency custody or placement responsibility by helping families improve the conditions in the home that caused agency intervention; • Ensure a single, stable, safe, nurturing, and appropriate temporary living arrangement for children removed from their homes; • Achieve an alternative safe, permanent home for all children in agency custody or placement responsibility who cannot return home.

Entering Foster Care A child shall be considered to have entered foster care on the earlier of:

• The date of the first judicial finding that the child has been subjected to child abuse or neglect; or • The date that is 60 days after the date on which the child is removed from the home (Sec. 475. [42 U.S.C. 675]). (http://www.ssa.gov/OP_Home/ssact/title04http://www.ssa.gov/OP_Home/ssact/title04/0475.htm/0475.htm)

Family Foster Care "Family Foster Care" means a planned, goal-directed service in which the temporary protection and care of children take place in a family foster home. Family foster care is a child welfare service for children and their parents who must live apart from each other for a period due to abuse, neglect, dependency, or other circumstances necessitating out-of-home care.

Foster Care "Foster Care" means the continuing provision of the essentials of daily living on a 24-hour basis for dependent, neglected, abused, abandoned, destitute, orphaned, undisciplined or delinquent children or other children who, due to similar problems of behavior or family conditions, are living apart from their parents, relatives, or guardians in a family foster home or residential child-care facility. The essentials of daily living include but are not limited to shelter, meals, clothing, education, recreation, and individual attention and supervision [N.C.G.S. §131D-10.2].

Primary Caregiver A legal guardian or an adult who is acting in loco parentis and has assumed the status and obligation of a parent in relation to the child(ren).

Residential Child-Care Facility (Child Care Institution) "Residential Child-Care Facility" means a staffed premise with paid or volunteer staff where children receive continuing full-time foster care. Residential child-care facility includes child-caring institutions, group homes, and children's camps which provide foster care (N.C.G.S. §131D- 10.2).

## Safety

|Policy|Legal Basis|
|---|---|
|The primary concern of Child Welfare Services is protecting children. When a<br>safety threat (present or impending) is identified, the county child welfare<br>services agency must respond and develop a plan of safety. At no time should<br>a county child welfare worker leave a child(ren) in unsafe circumstances. The<br>intent of safety planning is to reach an agreed upon plan with the family that<br>imposes the lowest level of intrusiveness possible while assuring a child(ren)'s<br>safety.|Non-secure custody will only be granted when one or more criteria<br>exist as specified in N.C.G.S. § 7B-503.<br>North Carolina statuteN.C.G.S. § 7B-101 (19) defines a safe home as<br>"a home in which the child is not at substantial risk of physical or<br>emotional abuse or neglect."|

Definitions

Safety Threat Defined A safety threat exists when there are conditions or actions within the child(ren)'s home that represent the likelihood of imminent serious harm to the child(ren). There are two types of safety threats: present and impending.

1. "Present safety threat" refers to an immediate, significant, and clearly observable family condition (severe harm or threat of severe harm)

occurring to a child(ren) in the present. Present danger is easier to detect because it is transparent and is occurring now. If present danger is observed, the child(ren) is not safe.

2. "Impending safety threat" refers to threatening conditions that are not immediately obvious or currently active but are out of control and

likely to cause serious harm to a child(ren) soon. Impending danger is covert. Impending danger is a threat that can be reasonably expected to result in serious harm if safety action is not taken and/or sustained. These threats may or may not be identified at the onset of involvement by a county child welfare services agency but are understood upon a more complete evaluation and understanding of the individual and family conditions and functioning.

To be classified as a safety threat, a situation, condition, or behavior must meet the "safety threshold." The safety threshold is the point when a parent's behaviors, attitudes, emotions, intent, or circumstances create conditions that fall beyond mere risk of future maltreatment and have

Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL Page 31 Return to: Cross Function Topics Return to: Manual TOC

<!-- page: 33 -->
## Safety

Definitions become an actual imminent threat to the child(ren)'s safety. These conditions could reasonably result in the serious and unacceptable pain and suffering of a vulnerable child(ren).

Safety Agreement Defined A safety agreement/plan is made between a parent and a county child welfare services agency when a child(ren) is in immediate danger in their own home because of a safety threat. A safety agreement/plan must be all the following:

1. Enough to manage safety;
2. Tailored to the address the child(ren)'s safety issues that exist within the family;
3. Immediately available so that it is capable of being in operation the same day it is created; and
4. A plan that includes actions and goals that are specific and measurable.

Due Process Considerations Under the United States Constitution, parents have a fundamental right to the care, custody, and control of their child(ren). Safety actions that require the separation or restriction of a parent's access to their child(ren) affect a parent's custodial rights. When a county child welfare services agency interferes with this right, reasonable procedural protections must be in place. This procedural protection often takes the form of a hearing in juvenile court. In certain situations, to protect a child(ren), a county child welfare worker conducting an assessment may be required to perform actions that affect a parent's custodial rights without first providing procedural due process. These instances should be used only to the extent necessary to protect the child(ren) and should not continue longer than necessary to assure safety. Procedural protection must be provided within a reasonable period, even when a parent agrees to the infringement on the parent's own custodial right.

Guardians, custodians, caretakers, and other relatives do not have these same constitutional rights; this due process consideration only applies to birth and adoptive parents.

<!-- page: 40 -->
## Candidacy for Foster Care

Definition

Candidacy for foster care is defined in the ACF Children's Bureau's IV-E Policy manual based on requirements in Title IV-E of the social security act. Federal policy allows counties to claim reimbursement for specific allowable costs when serving children who meet the definition and documentation requirements to be candidates for foster care.

Candidate for Foster Care: a child at imminent or serious risk of removal from the home into foster care as evidenced by the county child welfare agency's making efforts to either prevent removal or pursuing removal.

Documentation and Evidence of Candidacy: a written case plan that clearly indicates that, absent effective preventive services, foster care is the planned arrangement for the child. The case plan must be developed together with the family, describe the services offered and provided to prevent removal of the child from the home, and document why the child is at serious or imminent risk of removal absent effective preventive services. In the absence of a case plan, evidence of court proceedings for removal can also serve as documentation and evidence of foster care candidacy. The evidence can be in the form of a petition requesting removal or a court transcript showing the county has requested removal into foster care.

Length of candidacy: A child will only be a candidate for as long as the child is at serious risk of entering foster care and the county child welfare agency is either pursuing removal or making reasonable efforts to prevent such removal. Federal policy does not set a maximum length of time a child can be a candidate, but candidacy is expected to be finite. A written justification is needed to retain a child in candidate status longer than six months.

Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL Page 39 Return to: Cross Function Topics Return to: Manual TOC

# Candidacy for Foster Care

|Protocol-What you must do|Guidance-What you should do|
|---|---|
|**Establishing and documenting candidacy.**<br>•<br>To establish candidacy for a child at serious risk of entering foster<br>care, a worker must develop a services plan with the family that:<br>•<br>clearly states why the child is at serious or imminent risk of<br>removal to foster care absent effective preventive services (being<br>at imminent risk of removal from the home to a treatment or a<br>juvenile justice facility does establish foster care candidacy)<br>•<br>describes services to be offered or provided to the family<br>specifically to prevent removal into foster care.<br>When services are provided to a child who is not a candidate (either<br>because the child has never been determined to be a candidate or<br>because the child is no longer a candidate), IV-E funding cannot be used<br>to reimburse costs. SeeAppendix 3.4 Child Welfare Funding-CPSfor<br>additional information on costs that can and cannot be claimed to IV-E<br>and on other sources of funding.<br>Note: A child at imminent risk of removal for placement in a treatment or<br>Juvenile Justice facility does establish foster care candidacy.<br>During the development of a case plan that establishes candidacy, the<br>worker must communicate with the family that the child is at serious or<br>imminent risk of entering foster care without effective services and<br>clearly outline what changes are required to prevent removal.<br>All efforts made to prevent the removal of the child from the home must<br>be documented in the client record.|Every child who has been maltreated and found in need of CPS In-Home<br>Services does not meet the federal requirements to be considered<br>candidates for foster care.<br>In North Carolina, referrals to CPS In-Home Services are based on a<br>moderate to high risk of repeat maltreatment. Candidacy for foster care<br>is determined based on imminent or serious risk that a child will enter<br>foster care absent effective preventive services and must be documented<br>in a plan of services developed together with the family. The<br>development of a case plan is best done in the context of a Child and<br>Family Team meeting.<br>When developing the case plan, a conversation with the family should<br>include why the child is at serious risk of entering foster care without<br>effective services. These are potentially uncomfortable conversations but<br>are crucial and should be entered into honestly and in a non-threatening<br>way. If it would be untrue to tell a family that the plan is foster care<br>absent effective preventive services, that is an indication the child may<br>not meet the candidacy definition.|

<!-- page: 42 -->
# Jurisdiction in Child Welfare

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|PURPOSE<br>Child Protective Services are federally mandated; however, North Carolina remains one of the few states that administers its child<br>welfare system at the county level. These federally mandated services are applicable to the entire state without consideration to<br>county lines. The Division of Social Services (the Division) recognizes that all children in North Carolina have the right to live free of<br>abuse and neglect without regard to where they live or with whom they live. Therefore, all 100 counties in North Carolina bear the<br>responsibility for ensuring the safety and well-being of all children and their families.<br>This policy provides guidance but does not cover every conceivable situation that may arise. Therefore, counties must remember that<br>some basic premises exist that guide this policy.<br>The safety of North Carolina's children and their families is paramount. Although North Carolina is a county-administered system, the<br>protection of children knows no county lines.<br>The county of residence is the primary county responsible for the delivery of services to a family.N.C.G.S. § 153A-257provides guidance<br>to the Division to determine which county is responsible for a needy person who meets the eligibility requirements for public assistance<br>offered by the county or for social services required by the person. This statute includes a provision about the residence of a minor and<br>further provides that the Division is responsible for determining which county shall be provide child protective services when two or<br>more counties cannot agree. Although the statue does not define the term "reside," in this context the term has its ordinary meaning. A<br>child "resides" where:<br>•<br>He or she is receiving public assistance,<br>•<br>He or she is enrolled in school or childcare,<br>•<br>He or she works,<br>•<br>He or she receives mail, or<br>•<br>The location listed on the child's medical records, driver's license, or library card.<br>The child resides in the place where he or she sleeps most often, lives, and has the greatest number of daily contacts unless the child is<br>in a licensed foster care placement, a hospital, or other remedial facility.<br>This policy defines a process that addresses the complicated issues presented when more than one county is engaged in the delivery of<br>services to a family.||

Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL Page 41 Return to: Cross Function Topics Return to: Manual TOC

<!-- page: 43 -->
# Jurisdiction in Child Welfare

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|With the implementation of the Multiple Response System, county child welfare agencies utilize the principles of both family-centered<br>practice and System of Care. Both recognize that the protection of children is a collaboration between the county child welfare agency,<br>the family, and other professionals that are involved with the family. It is also a partnership between county child welfare agencies<br>where collaboration and accountability model a desired behavior to the families that are served.<br>When the family-centered practice and System of Care principles are applied to the interactions with families and other professional,<br>there is the expectation to see greater accountability which leads to better outcomes for children and families.<br>When the delivery of child welfare services involves multiple counties, it is expected that each county child welfare agency work in a<br>collaborative and coordinated manner in order to provide greater continuity of services to families while providing stability and ensuring<br>safety for children. Each community is best able to identify, build, and make the most of existing resources to assist families.<br>This section will provide guidance on conducting Child Protective Services (CPS) Assessments that involve out-of-home placement<br>providers, as well as, when there are multiple counties involved. Guidance is also provided for situations when multiple counties are<br>involved during the provision of CPS In-Home Services, Child Placement Services and Adoption Services that involve disruptions and<br>dissolutions.<br>When a CPS report indicates more than one county will be involved with the delivery of services, the county where the child resides is<br>responsible for conducting the CPS Assessment except for specific circumstances identified in this section. Whenever possible, face-to-<br>face meetings should occur when discussing case decisions and case plans by all county child welfare agencies involved.<br>Note: When a county child welfare agency requests any assistance from another county child welfare agency, that request shall be<br>honored.<br>LEGAL BASIS<br>North Carolina laws regarding the provision of protective services are in Chapter 7B of the North Carolina General Statutes. Please refer<br>to "Legal Basis" in Assessments in theNC Child Welfare manualfor additional information regarding the laws that govern the provision<br>of child protective services in North Carolina. Additional guidance is provided in North Carolina's Administrative Code Title 10A- Health<br>and Human Services- Chapter 70- Children's Services.<br>Laws regarding the provision of child placement services are also located in Chapter 7B of the North Carolina General Statues. Please<br>refer to the Permanency Planning section of theNC Child Welfare manual.||

# Jurisdiction in Child Welfare

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|Chapter 48of the North Carolina General Statues establishes a clear judicial process for adoptions.<br>Please refer to Adoptions in theNC Child Welfare manualfor additional guidance regarding the delivery of adoption services.<br>Determining the County Child Welfare Agency Responsible for Providing Social Services<br>When determining the county child welfare agency responsible for providing services, the guiding principle is where the child(ren)<br>resides.<br>To determine the county with the responsibility to provide social services,N.C.G.S. §153A-25states:<br>Legal residence in a county determines which county is responsible (i) for financial support of a needy person who meets the eligibility<br>requirements for a public assistance or medical care program offered by the county or (ii) for other social services required by the<br>person.<br>1.)<br>Legal residence in a county is determined as follows:<br>(a.)<br>Except as modified below, a person has legal residence in the county in which he or she resides.<br>(b.)<br>If a person is in a hospital, mental institution, nursing home, boarding home, confinement facility, or similar institution or<br>facility, he or she does not, solely because of that fact, have legal residence in the county in which the institution or facility is located.<br>(c.)<br>A minor has the legal residence of:<br>(i)<br>Theparent or other relative with whom he or she resides.||

Page 43 Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

# Jurisdiction in Child Welfare

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|(ii)<br>If the minor does not reside with a parent or other relative and is not in a foster home, hospital, mental institution, nursing<br>home, boarding home, educational institution, confinement facility, or similar institution or facility, he or she has the legal residence of<br>the person with whom he resides.<br>(iii)<br>Any other minor has the legal residence of his or her mother,<br>A.<br>If the mother's residence is not known, then the legal residence of his or her father,<br>B.<br>If the residence of either parent is unknown, the minor is a legal resident of the county in which he or she is found.<br>If the minor is in the legal custody of the father, then the minor's residence shall be that of the father.<br>2.)<br>A legal residence continues until a new one is acquired, either within or outside this State. When a new legal residence is<br>acquired, former legal residences terminate.<br>3.)<br>This section is intended to replace the law defining "legal settlement." Therefore, any general law or local act that refers to<br>"legal settlement" is deemed to refer to this section and the rules contained herein.<br>B. When Two or More County Child Welfare Agencies Disagree<br>Furthermore, N.C.G.S. §153A-257states: "If two or more county departments of social services disagree regarding the legal residence of<br>a minor in a child abuse, neglect, or dependency case, any one of the county departments of social services may refer the issue to the<br>Department of Health and Human Services, Division of Social<br>Services, for resolution. The Director of the Division of Social Services or the Director's designee shall review thepertinent background||

"<br>It is the expectation of the Division that prior to seeking the intervention of the Division that the respective county directors become<br>involved in these disagreements and work towards a resolution.<br>III.<br>CPS ASSESSMENTS<br>NCGS §7B-302defines the responsibility of the director when receiving reports of child abuse, neglect, and dependency. North Carolina<br>Administrative Code10A NCAC 70A.0105further clarifies the responsibility of counties when receiving a report of alleged abuse or<br>neglect.<br>A. Intake/Screening<br>Policy for screening reports of alleged abuse, neglect, and/or dependency can be found in Intake in theNC Child Welfare manual.<br>N.C.G.S. §7B-302 directs county child welfare agencies that upon receipt of any report, a prompt and through assessment shall be<br>conducted. The county that receives the report is responsible for screening the report based on the available information, except for<br>reports involving conflicts of interest for the county child welfare agency or reports involving children that reside in another county.<br>Please see Conflict of Interestfor details of how to screen reports involving conflicts of interest.<br>Note: Receipt of a report refers to the first time that the reporter contacts the agency even if that county child welfare agency will not be<br>the county responsible for conducting the CPS Assessment. When the report involves a conflict of interest or a child that resides in<br>another county, the county who receives the report must take all the information from the reporter. The county that took the report<br>must make verbal notification to the child's county of residence who must make a screening decision and, if accepted, determine the<br>assessment track and response time.||

The county child welfare agency that receives a<br>request to complete an activity in a CPS Assessment does NOT make a determination regarding the acceptance of the report.<br>Definitions: Out of Home CPS<br>Assessments are those involving children in foster homes, public and private group homes, other child caring institutions, boarding<br>schools, Department of Health and Human Services facilities, and Division of Juvenile Justice facilities.<br>Definitions: A request for assistance shall be made when the activity required in a CPS Assessment cannot be performed by the county<br>child welfare agency conducting or coordinating the CPS Assessment. The exception is for reports involving residential is childcare facilities and boarding schools.||

That county child<br>welfare agency will determine the counties of residence for each child and will make referrals to the child welfare agency in each child's<br>county of residence to complete the CPS Assessment for those children.<br>(ii.)<br>If during CPS Intake, the children alleged to have been abused, neglected, and/or dependent have returned to their counties of<br>residence from the county where the abuse, neglect, and/or dependency occurred, the resident county child welfare agencies will be<br>notified of the report, and those agencies will be responsible for conducting the CPS Assessments for the children residing in their<br>counties.<br>(iii.)<br>When there are multiple county child welfare agencies involved in conducting CPS Assessments for multiple children resulting<br>from a report of abuse, neglect, and/or dependency, it is the responsibility of each county child welfare agency of residence to<br>determine if it will conduct a family assessment or an investigative assessment for the child that is a resident of its county.<br>(iv.)<br>Each county child welfare agency will complete its assessment and make its case decision based on that assessment. Each<br>county child welfare agency will submit oneDSS-5104for the child that is the resident of that county.||

# Jurisdiction in Child Welfare

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|(a.)<br>When a resident county child welfare agency needs another county child welfare agency to conduct CPS Assessment, the<br>resident county child welfare agency is responsible for contacting the other county child welfare agency directly. Direct contact by<br>telephone is preferable. The resident county child welfare agency is responsible for ensuring that the CPS Assessment is initiated within<br>the required timeframes to assess safety of the child(ren) and to develop a safety agreement with the parents/caretakers, if needed<br>prior to the other county beginning the ongoing assessment.<br>(b.)<br>It is the responsibility of the resident county child welfare agency to respond and cooperate in every way possible, providing all<br>necessary assistance as may be requested by the county child welfare agency conducting the CPS Assessment.<br>(c.)<br>It is the responsibility of the resident county child welfare agency to make available the Structured Intake Form, case record,<br>Central Registry report, and other pertinent information. It may be asked to conduct local criminal records checks and make any<br>necessary appointments with individuals pertinent to the assessment within the community.<br>(d.)<br>It is also the responsibility of the resident county child welfare agency to coordinate with the other county child welfare<br>agency to discuss the information learned during the CPS Assessment prior to the county child welfare agency conducting the<br>assessment making the case decision. While the county child welfare agency conducting the CPS Assessment has the sole<br>responsibility for making the case decision, both counties will discuss the finding before the case decision is made.<br>(e.) The resident county child welfare agency is responsible for completing and sending all required notices. This includes delivering<br>personal notice to the perpetrator in substantiated cases when their name will be included on the Responsible Individuals List (RIL). See<br>Assessments in theNC Child Welfare manual for the procedures in providing this notice.<br>If multiple attempts to deliver the notice are unsuccessful or if distance is an issue, it is permissible for the county child welfare agency<br>conducting the CPS Assessment to request the resident county child welfare agency to deliver the notice. All required timeframes must<br>be met as outlined in statute and in Assessments in theNC Child Welfare manual.<br>(f.) The resident county child welfare agency is responsible for ensuring all documentation is received from the county child welfare<br>agency that conducted the CPS Assessment andplaced in the case record.||

|CPS Assessments of an Out-of-Home Placement<br>When the CPS Assessment is related to an out-of-home placement provider, there are additional required activities.<br>(i.) The agency responsible for supervising the home shall be notified verbally within two hours that a report was received and accepted<br>for a CPS Assessment.<br>For facilities licensed by Division of Health Services Regulation (DHSR), verbal notification shall be to the intake/complaint unit at 1- 800-<br>624-3004. Should this occur after-hours, it is permissible to leave a message.<br>A written report shall follow the verbal notification within five business days of receiving the CPS report. Please refer to theDSS-5282.<br>Note: When notifying the agency responsible for supervising the placement, refer the agency to its internal protocol for responding to<br>allegations of child abuse and/or neglect.<br>(ii.)<br>Depending on the nature of the report, and if appropriate, it is permissible for the agency responsible for supervising the<br>placement provider or licensing staff to notify the provider that a report has been received. When notifying the agency that a report has<br>been received, it will be important to advise the agency on what to expect during the CPS Assessment.<br>(iii.)<br>Identifying information regarding the alleged victim child shall not be included in any written documents found in the<br>placement provider's record.<br>(iv.)<br>All children living in the home are considered victim children. All children named as victims that are no longer living in the home<br>are still required to be seen.<br>(v.)<br>A visit to the home where the alleged incident occurred is required.<br>(vi.)<br>All required activities must be completed even if the child(ren) has been removed from the home.||

|come to a consensus with a focus on family-centered practice.<br>2.)<br>If the staff from the county child welfare agencies involved and the respective supervisors cannot reach a consensus, the<br>program managers/administrators from each county child welfare should confer, attempt to resolve the issues, and come to a<br>consensus with a focus on family-centered practice.<br>3.)<br>If the issues cannot be resolved at the program manager/administrator level, the directors of the involved child welfare<br>agencies will be notified of the issues and the fact that the issues cannot be resolved by their staff and management. The directors will<br>confer, attempt to resolve the issues, and come to a consensus with a focus on family-centered practice.<br>B. Consultation with the Division of Social Services<br>In the event that the county child welfare agencies involved cannot resolve the issues and come to a consensus at the directors' level,<br>the appropriate Regional Child Welfare Specialist (RCWS) will be contacted. If multiple RCWSs are assigned to the different counties<br>involved in the situation, each county's RCWS should be included in the discussion. The Local Support Team Manager may also be<br>contacted as a means of resolving the disagreement between the county child welfare agencies. In the event that the RCWS or Local<br>Support Team Manager is unavailable, contact the Division's CPS Policy Team at (919) 527-6340.<br>N.C.G.S. §153A-257(d)provides the Division of Social Services with the authority to make a decision when disagreements occur and the<br>decision of the Division shall be final.<br>NOTE: At no time shall a CPS Assessment be allowed to go un-assessed due to a disagreement between county child welfare agencies.<br>Although nothing should prevent a county child welfare worker from crossing into another county to conduct a CPS Assessment activity<br>if necessary, a request from the other county should be made first.<br>VI.<br>CHILD PLACEMENT SERVICES||

That county child welfare agency may request that the county child welfare agency where the child(ren) and<br>adoptive family reside assist by actually removing the child(ren) from the placement.<br>However, the county child welfare agency that has custody of the child(ren) is responsible for transporting the child(ren) to the new<br>placement. In many instances, the county child welfare agency that had been supervising the adoptive placement may have provided<br>significant contact and services to the child(ren) during the placement. The two county child welfare agencies may jointly decide that<br>both county child welfare workers need to accompany the child(ren) to the new placement in order to minimize further trauma. This<br>decision should be based on the needs of the child(ren).<br>The county where the child(ren) resides with the adoptive family is responsible for ensuring the child(ren)'s safety until the county child<br>welfare agency with legal custody moves the child(ren) to the new placement.<br>The DSS-1797shall detail the responsibility of each county child welfare agency involved should a disruption occur. |1.)<br>Before filing a motion to change venue of a juvenile court case, the involved county child welfare agencies must discuss the<br>case. When it becomes necessary to request a change of venue, county child welfare agencies must collaborate with one another to<br>achieve a resolution that is in the best interest of the child(ren) without interrupting services to the family. The original county child<br>welfare agency shall ensure that notice of the hearing to transfer venue is served on the county child welfare agency proposed to<br>receive venue so that it is afforded an opportunity to be present at the hearing and to present any arguments against changing venue or<br>send written consents.<br>2.)<br>An order changing custody of the child(ren) from one county child welfare agency to another should be entered only after that<br>county child welfare agency is given notice and an opportunity to be heard or consented to the transfer.<br>North Carolina Rule of Civil Procedure 13(h)states, "Parties may be brought in. When the presence of parties other than those to the<br>original action is required for the granting of complete relief in the determination of a counterclaim or cross-claim, the court shall order<br>them to be brought in as defendants as provided in these rules, if jurisdiction of them can be obtained."<br>North Carolina Rules of Civil Procedure 19(b)states, "The court may determine any claim before it when it can do so without prejudice<br>to the rights of any party or to the rights of others not before the court; but when a complete determination of such claim cannot be<br>made without the presence of other parties, the court shall order such other parties summoned to appear in the action."<br>3.) If a county child welfare agency fails to appear or respond after having received notice, that county child welfare is considered as<br>having forfeited its right to be heard, and venue may be properly transferred.<br>B. When a Parent Moves<br>When a parent moves and a decision is made to transfer venue, the following should be considered:<br>1.)<br>The permanent plan for the child.<br>2.)<br>The current placement of the child.<br>3.)<br>Visitation issues.<br>4.)<br>Any other relevant facts.<br>IX.<br>INTERSTATE COMPACT ON THE PLACEMENT OF CHILDREN<br>The Interstate Compact on the Placement of Children (ICPC) regulates the interstate movement of children. The Compact is a uniform<br>law that has been enacted by all the fifty states, the District of Columbia, and the US Virgin Islands. The North Carolina legislature<br>enacted the Compact on July 1, 1971. The Compact is the best means to ensure protection and services to children who are placed<br>across state lines for child placement services or adoption.<br>The Compact is a legally and administratively sound means of placing children across states lines with the same safeguards and services<br>as are available when they are placed within their own state. The Compact provides the means for securing an evaluation of a<br>prospective placement before the child is sent outside the state and provides assurance that the sending state retains jurisdiction over<br>the child sufficient to ensure that the child receives adequate care and protection. See Interstate Compact on the Placement of Children<br>in theNC Child Welfare manualfor more information.||

<!-- page: 84 -->
# Conflict of Interest

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|**PURPOSE**<br>Child Protective Services are federally mandated; however, North Carolina (NC) remains one of the few states that administer its child<br>welfare system at the county level. These federally mandated services are applicable to the entire state without consideration to county<br>lines. The North Carolina Division of Social Services (Division) recognizes that all children in NC have the right to live free of abuse and<br>neglect without regard to where they live or with whom they live. Therefore, all 100 counties in NC bear the responsibility for ensuring<br>the safety and well-being of all children and their families.<br>The primary purpose of this policy is to avoid conflicts of interest or the potential for the appearance of conflicts of interest. It was<br>developed in collaboration with the NC Association of County Directors of Social Services Children's Services Committee to provide<br>guidance to county child welfare agencies on managing conflicts of interest.<br>County child welfare agencies should develop Memoranda of Understanding (MOU) between partner county child welfare agencies, as<br>well as case-specific Memoranda of Agreement to address the needs of individual county child welfare systems. An example of a MOU<br>can be found at the end of this policy.<br>When the delivery of child welfare services involves multiple counties, it is expected that each county child welfare agency work in a<br>collaborative and coordinated manner to provide greater continuity of services to families while providing stability and ensuring safety<br>for children. Each community is best able to identify, build, and make the most of existing resources to assist families.<br>All efforts should be made to resolve disagreements related to the identification of and the provision of services to families where a<br>Conflict of Interest (COI) or a perceived Conflict of Interest (COI) exists. These efforts should include involving county child welfare<br>agency management up through and including directors in the attempt to reach a resolution.<br>This policy addresses COI cases only. It does not cover every conceivable situation that may arise and nothing in this policy alters any of<br>the required activities to complete the child welfare services activities that are outlined in NC child welfare law, rule, and policy.||

Intake/Screening**<br>1.<br>Screening<br>a.<br>When there is an immediate safety concern for a child, the resident county child welfare agency should<br>screen and initiate the report, regardless of whether a COI exists. The resident county child welfare agency shall<br>immediately request assistance from a partner county child welfare agency due to COI and notify the partner<br>child welfare agency of the need to immediately address a safety concern.<br>b.<br>After determining if there are any immediate safety issues, the resident county child welfare agency<br>shall determine whether a COI exists.<br>c.<br>When the resident county has identified a report as a COI and there is not an immediate safety concern<br>for a child, the narrative collected from the reporter along with their contact information is referred<br>immediately to a partner county where the report will be screened for acceptance, and when accepted, a<br>response track and time must be determined within two hours of receipt. Screening decisions stand regardless<br>of which county child welfare agency ultimately conducts the CPS Assessment. ## Risk & Use of Assessment Tools

Policy

The primary concern of Child Welfare Services is protecting child(ren) from maltreatment.

Definition

Risk is the likelihood that a child(ren) will be harmed (abused or neglected). Safety threats are a subset of risk that represent the likelihood of immediate or imminent serious harm to the child(ren).

Risk:

• Occurs on a continuum from mild to severe; • Includes family situations and behaviors from onset progressing into seriously troubled; • Applies to aspects of family life relevant to understanding the likelihood of maltreatment; • Impacts child(ren) well-being and safety; • Is based on an unlimited time frame (could occur any time in the future); • Is associated with family functioning and behaviors that need to be managed or treated; and • Requires a judgement about the negative effects on the child(ren) from future maltreatment.

Risk assessment is an ongoing process to determine the probability of future harm to the child(ren). It does not predict when or how serious the harm may be, but rather the likelihood that harm will occur. Risk assessment, based on an examination of factors, attempts to address whether the harm may continue and whether the harm is acute or chronic in nature. It is used as a vehicle for decision making in child maltreatment cases. The risk scales are based on research on cases with "substantiated" abuse or neglect or "services needed" findings that examined the relationships between family characteristics and the outcomes of subsequent abuse and/or neglect. The scales do not predict recurrence; simply the likelihood that a family will have another incident without intervention by the county child welfare services agency.

"Protective capacity" is defined as the ability and willingness to mitigate or ameliorate the identified safety and risk concerns. Protective capacity can be demonstrated by a parent through their statements, actions, and reactions. Protective capacity exists both within the parent/caretaker and within the family environment.

Page 103 Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

<!-- page: 105 -->
# Risk & Use of Assessment Tools

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|**MONITORING AND ASSESSING RISK**<br>Risk assessment is an ongoing process that<br>starts at the time a case is accepted for a CPS-<br>A and continues until case closure.<br>County child welfare efforts must assess the<br>risk and develop a plan to reduce the risk to an<br>acceptable level with a focus on maintaining<br>the child(ren) in the family home.<br>When assessing for risk, county child welfare<br>workers must observe and document the<br>impact of maltreatment on the child(ren). The<br>worker must use objective language to<br>document the child(ren)'s behavior or<br>condition and relate that behavior or<br>condition to the identified maltreatment.<br>**ASSESSMENT TOOLS**<br>The following assessment tools must be<br>completed**accurately and thoroughly**, <br>approved, and signed within the timeframes<br>indicated in the appropriate functional<br>protocol:<br>•<br>Safety Assessment DSS-5231<br>(Assessments),<br>•<br>Risk Assessment DSS-5230<br>(Assessments),<br>•<br>Risk Reassessment DSS-5226(In-Home),|When assessing risk, a county child welfare services agency should consider:<br>•<br>CPS history, how long has risk been occurring;<br>•<br>Parent/caretaker's reaction to and/or explanation regarding the risk (what was the<br>parent/caretaker's intent?);<br>•<br>Related criminal history;<br>•<br>Parent/caretaker's willingness to engage and/or agreement with safety and risk planning,<br>(what is the parent's/caretaker's attitude?);<br>•<br>How severe the potential risk is to the child(ren);<br>•<br>What is the impact of the potential risk on the child(ren)?<br>•<br>What is the degree of change needed by the parent(s)/caretaker(s) to remediate the risk?<br>•<br>What is the timeframe within which the risk is likely to occur?<br>•<br>What is the protective capacity of the parents/caretakers to address the identified risk?<br>Families should be involved in the completion of all the assessment tools used by the county child<br>welfare services agency.<br>While the approach is family-centered, decisions regarding the risk, needs, and strengths are the<br>responsibility of the county child welfare services agency. The outcome of any decision should not be<br>surprising to the family if the county child welfare worker has successfully involved family members.<br>**IMPACT ON CHILDREN**<br>When assessing for risk, county child welfare workers should be alert for conditions, behaviors, and<br>reactions in child(ren) that indicate an impact from maltreatment. In some cases, the impact may be<br>directly and clearly related to the maltreatment, including but not limited to:<br>•<br>Bruising, burns, bites or broken bones from abuse or neglect;<br>•<br>Medical conditions caused by a lack of medical care; and/or<br>•<br>Exposure to an unsafe condition (e.g., young child running across busy street due to lack of<br>supervision).|

Questions to consider include:<br>•<br>Is the parent/caretaker oriented to time, place, and space? (i.e., reality orientation)|

# Collateral Contacts

Legal Basis

N.C.G.S. § 7B-302(c) states: "in performing any duties related to the assessment of the complaint or the provision or arrangement of social services, the director may consult with any public or private agencies or individuals, including the available state or local law enforcement officers who shall assist in the assessment and evaluation of the seriousness of any report of abuse, neglect, or dependency when requested by the director. The director or the director's representative may make a written demand for any information or reports, whether confidential, that may, in the director's opinion, be relevant to the protective services case. Upon the director's or the director's representative's request and unless protected by the attorney-client privilege, any public or private agency or individual shall provide access to and copies of this confidential information and these records to the extent permitted by federal law and regulations."

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|A collateral contact is any person(s) identified as having information relevant to the CPS case or other<br>persons or agencies known to be currently involved with the family or known to have knowledge of<br>the situation. This includes, but is not limited to:<br>•<br>Medical and mental health providers. When a child(ren) is alleged to have a medical or mental<br>health condition, disease, or illness relevant to the allegation, the county child welfare<br>services agency must consult the medical or mental health provider treating the condition.<br>This consultation must be focused on determining the family's assertions about that medical<br>or mental health condition, or there must be justification for why this was not done.<br>•<br>Educational providers.<br>•<br>Collateral sources provided by the family. The county child welfare worker should ask the<br>family for collateral information sources. These contacts should be people who can<br>provide reliable information concerning the child(ren) and family (i.e., not simply<br>character references). This would include, but not be limited to:<br>`o` Extended family members,<br>`o` Friends,<br>`o` Community members, and<br>`o` Faith community members.<br>•<br>Reporters/intake form collaterals.|Professional Collateral Contacts<br>It is expected that professional service<br>providers and agencies will share concerns<br>about the family with the family members<br>themselves. When a professional collateral is<br>to be contacted, whether provided by the<br>reporter, the family, or the county child<br>welfare worker, the parent/caretaker should<br>be given the option to be present for this<br>collateral contact. In those instances, when<br>the parent chooses not to be present, the<br>county child welfare worker should advise<br>the parent of the information gathered from<br>that collateral source.<br>Non-Professional Collateral Sources<br>The parent will be with the county<br>child<br>welfare worker when contact is made if the|

The county<br>child welfare worker should contact the non-<br>professional collateral information source to<br>determine whether that individual has any<br>concern about their own personal safety if<br>the parent and county child welfare worker<br>contact them together. ## Diligent Efforts

Legal Basis

10A NCAC 70A.0105 regarding children states: "The director shall make diligent efforts to locate the alleged victim child or children until such efforts are successful or until the director concludes that the child or children cannot be located. Diligent efforts shall include, but not be limited to, visits to the child's or children's address at different times of the day and on different days. All efforts to locate the child or children shall be documented in the case record."

N.C.G.S.§ 7B-505(b) : The department of social services shall use due diligence to identify and notify adult relatives and other persons with legal custody of a sibling of the juvenile within 30 days after the initial order removing custody. The department shall file with the court information regarding attempts made to identify and notify adult relatives of the juvenile and persons with legal custody of a sibling of the juvenile.

Performance Measurement - CPS Assessment The Child and Family and Family Services Review (CFSR) and the On-Site Review Instrument (OSRI) measure whether the county child welfare worker met state requirements for making face to face contacts with children during agency involvement with the family throughout the life of the case Specifically, Safety Outcome, Item 1 looks at if the county child welfare agency worker initiated a CPS Assessment according to state policy, and if not, why the initiation did not occur timely. Documentation for timely initiation is through entry of the DSS-5104, Field 6A - DILIGENT EFFORTS REASON. Failure to document a valid reason for not making timely initiation may impact the achievement of safety. Valid reasons for untimely initiation are:

• Unable to Initiate Timely Despite Daily Diligent Efforts, through a variety of methods • Unable to Initiate Timely, Diligent Efforts not met • Unable to Initiate Timely Despite Daily Diligent Efforts, due to family out of State • Unable to Initiate Timely Due to Natural Disaster

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|

Contact numbers and addresses for<br>specific prisons can be found on the NC Division|

# Juvenile Court

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|**INTRODUCTION**<br>The provision of child welfare services is frequently enhanced by the involvement of the<br>court system. However, invoking the jurisdiction of the court system into a child<br>welfare case is not a measure to be taken by the agency without careful consideration<br>and planning. This careful consideration and planning must take into account that the<br>juvenile court process can be seen as an adversarial process and so it requires strong<br>collaboration between the agency, the family, the service providers, and the court<br>system. This duality of adversity and collaboration exists to serve the best interest of all<br>parties involved by protecting the rights of both children and their parents and by<br>holding the community accountable to serve the best interest of its families.<br>Protecting the safety, well-being, and permanence of children is not only the role of the<br>agency but of the community, including the court system. The court system also plays a<br>critical role in meeting the eligibility requirements of several funding sources that are<br>essential to meeting the needs of North Carolina's children. Even though the juvenile<br>court process can be adversarial in nature, staff from county child welfare agencies are<br>strongly encouraged to use these principles to guide every interaction with every<br>person the staff member encounters throughout the court process. This includes the<br>judge, the family and the children, the agency's attorney, the parent's attorney, the<br>Guardian ad Litem volunteer and the attorney advocate, the bailiff or sheriff's deputy,<br>and any other court personnel. Specifically, maintaining professional relationships with<br>parents' attorneys and attorney advocates in this process models for families and<br>demonstrates to the court that partnerships are valued despite differences each group<br>may have. Albeit only a small piece of the equation, these interactions can become a<br>part of the teaching process to help families successfully navigate the child welfare<br>system.||

Page 115 Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

<!-- page: 117 -->
# Juvenile Court

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|To solidify these principles into everyday practice, agencies are strongly encouraged to<br>develop a Memorandum of Agreement (MOA) or a Memorandum of Understanding (MOU)<br>with their local court system to help establish a collaborative relationship between all the<br>participants within the court process and can be used as the basis to establish local court<br>rules and procedures. These memoranda can also help to improve outcomes of safety,<br>permanence, and well-being of abused, neglected, and/or dependent children in the court<br>system by increasing timely decisions and resolution of juvenile court cases and to conduct<br>more meaningful, thorough hearings. When used as a "living document" these memoranda<br>can help to identify trends which impact outcomes for children and their families and<br>develop responsive strategies and highlight those training activities needed to enhance<br>overall best practices in child welfare and juvenile court.<br>The court process, from the first hearing to the final disposition, is critical to the<br>achievement of a timely, appropriate, permanent plan for children.<br>**II.**<br>**PURPOSE AND PHILOSOPHY**<br>The juvenile court is a branch of the district court whose primary function is the hearing,<br>evaluation, and disposition of cases involving children alleged to be delinquent,<br>undisciplined, abused, neglected, and/or dependent. In abuse, neglect, and dependency<br>proceedings involving county child welfare agencies, juvenile court represents the power<br>of the State to act in the best interests of children under 18 years of age. Thorough<br>respect for this power and an understanding of the juvenile court's complex process is<br>critical to the child welfare professional's success and efficacy in keeping children and<br>their families safe. Within North Carolina's 100 counties, there are 42 separate judicial<br>districts. Thus, several counties may be encompassed within a single court jurisdiction. A<br>map overlaying North Carolina's 42 District Courts with its 100 counties may be found at<br>http://www.nccourts.org/Courts/Trial/District/Documents/DistrictCourtmap15.pdf.||

Page 116 Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

<!-- page: 118 -->
# Juvenile Court

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|This chapter is intended to be a comprehensive examination of the role of the juvenile<br>court in North Carolina's child welfare cases. However, recognizing that each case<br>presents its own unique characteristics, workers are strongly encouraged to seek<br>clarifications when needed from their supervisor, from their agency's child welfare<br>leadership, from their agency's legal counsel, or from the North Carolina Division of<br>Social Services.<br>THE "LIFE" AND "TIMES" OF A JUVENILE COURT CASE<br>From the first filing of a petition through permanence, each case takes on a certain "life" and while<br>each jurisdiction may have practices that are specific to that court, in general juvenile court cases<br>follow a prescribed path. Just as with juvenile court terminology, developing an understanding of<br>the path of the case will assist child welfare staff in becoming more comfortable and confident in<br>working with the court system. Perhaps one of the best examinations of the "life" of a juvenile<br>court case is found in Chapter 4 of the text entitled Working with the Courts in Child Protection<br>found at: http://www.childwelfare.gov/pubs/usermanuals/courts/courts.pdf. Written by The<br>Honorable William G. Jones, a retired family court judge who served 25 years for the<br>Mecklenburg County District Court, that manual (along with the entire Child Abuse and Neglect<br>User Manual Series) is available online for viewing, download and printing from the Child Welfare<br>Information Gateway at: https://www.childwelfare.gov/pubs/usermanuals/. Counties are strongly<br>encouraged to have all workers that participate in juvenile court proceedings read and regularly<br>refer to Judge Jones' text.<br>Additionally, counties shall, throughout the life of an agency's involvement with families,<br>consistently explore with the family any American Indian heritage that family may have. It is critical<br>for agencies to understand that at any point a family identifies the child as an American Indian Child<br>of a federally recognized tribe, the agency is required to follow all provisions clearly outlined in the<br>Indian Child Welfare Act of 1978. In discussing the tribe's jurisdiction over child custody proceedings<br>involving an Indian child the Act reads:||
