|An Indian tribe shall have jurisdiction exclusive as to any State over any child custody proceeding<br>involving an Indian child who resides or is domiciled within the reservation of such tribe, except<br>where such jurisdiction is otherwise vested in the State by existing Federal law. Where an Indian<br>child is a ward of a tribal court, the Indian tribe shall retain exclusive jurisdiction, notwithstanding<br>the residence or domicile of the child.<br>In discussing the tribe's ability to intervene in the court's proceeding, the Act goes on to read, "In<br>any State court proceeding for the foster care placement of, or termination of parental rights to, an<br>Indian child, the Indian custodian of the child and the Indian child's tribe shall have a right to<br>intervene at any point in the proceeding."<br>A tool that can assist counties in following these provisions is the ICWA Checklist (DSS-5291). This<br>tool is useful in documenting the ongoing efforts made in determining<br>American Indian Ancestry, the efforts made to prevent the breakup of an American Indian Family,<br>tribal notification and involvement as well as the family's placement preferences.<br>Just as with the "life" of a juvenile court case, from the first filing of a petition through permanence<br>each case has certain "times" in which the various parts of the case must take place. In trainings<br>provided to GAL Attorney Advocates, Deana Fleming, Associate Legal Counsel with the<br>Administrative Office of the Courts, provides this examination of the timeframes that a typical court<br>case follows:<br>Juvenile Court Case Statutory Timeline<br>•<br>Day 0 - Juvenile Petition (AOC-J-130) and Nonsecure Custody Order (AOC-J-150)filed.<br>•<br>Day 7 - Initial hearing to determine need for continued nonsecure custody as per<br>N.C.G.S. § 7B-506(a)which may be continued for up to ten business days by consent;<br>subsequent hearings within seven business days and then 30 calendar day intervals as<br>per N.C.G.S. § 7B-506(e).<br>•<br>Day 60 -Adjudicatory hearing no later than 60 days from filing as perN.C.G.S. § 7B-801<br>unless continued as per N.C.G.S. § 7B-803.||

|IV-E: An Overview is a one-day specialized curriculum focusing on the laws and policies regarding<br>IV-E funding for children in custody or placement responsibility of county child welfare agencies.<br>Topics covered include the importance of IV-E funding, court requirements, required court<br>language, documentation, and eligibility and redetermination requirements. Due to the nature of<br>this one-day training event, county staff are strongly encouraged to direct any case and county<br>specific questions related to IV-E to the Regional Child Welfare Specialist (RCWS) or the Local<br>Business Liaison (LBL). This course is open to staff and supervisors employed in a NC county child<br>welfare agency in the areas of Income Maintenance, Work First, CPS Intake,<br>CPS Assessments, CPS Occasional On-Call, CPS In-Home Services, Child<br>Placement including Independent Living (LINKS), Foster Home Licensing, or Adoptions or individuals<br>or agencies contracting with a county child welfare agency to provide any of those services (this<br>does not include staff from private foster care / adoption agencies or private residential care<br>agencies). Completion of this course counts towards the annual requirement of 24 hours of<br>continuing education.<br>VI.<br>INVOKING THE JURISDICTION OF THE JUVENILE COURT<br>As stated previously, the act of filing a petition is a serious decision. Except for cases in which the<br>immediate safety of the child(ren) would be compromised, a Child and Family Team (CFT) meeting<br>shall be held before any court action is taken. If this is not possible due to an urgent need to<br>remove the child(ren) in order to maintain safety, then the CFT meeting shall be held as soon after<br>the removal as possible. This is an opportunity to bring the family, along with both formal and<br>informal supports to the table to make a plan to keep the child(ren) safe, in his or her community,<br>without court intervention. For more detailed information on conducting CFT meetings, refer to<br>Child and Family Team Meetings.<br>A county child welfare agency may invoke the jurisdiction of the juvenile court in these situations<br>related to child welfare: refusal to cooperate with or obstruction of a CPS Assessment, and request<br>to adjudicate a child as abused, neglected, and/or dependent. The filing of a petition requesting<br>adjudication of abuse, neglect, and/or dependency can be done for various reasons including the<br>family's unwillingness to accept critically needed services, safety-related circumstances that||

|necessitate the need for immediate removal, and despite agency efforts to provide services the<br>family has made no progress towards providing adequate care for the child. This chapter will<br>examine each of these three areas in general and will explore some of the issues related to the filing<br>of a petition.<br>A.<br>REFUSAL TO COOPERATE WITH / OBSTRUCTION OF A CPS ASSESSMENT<br>If, at any point during the CPS Assessment process, any person obstructs or interferes with the CPS<br>Assessment, the director may file a Petition: Obstruction of or Interference with Juvenile<br>Investigation (AOC-J-120) and a Juvenile Summons and Notice of Hearing for Obstruction of or<br>Interference with Juvenile Investigation (AOC-J-121) naming that person as a respondent and<br>requesting an order directing that person to cease such obstruction or interference. For purposes<br>of this section, the person obstructing the CPS Assessment is not limited only to a parent or family<br>member. The petition must first provide sufficient evidence to demonstrate that the allegations<br>outlined in the report would meet one or more of the definitions under NCGS § 7B-101 before<br>proceeding to evidence of the person's conduct that constitutes obstruction or interference. When<br>this evidence is established, then evidence of the person's obstruction or interference should be<br>specifically stated and verified. At this point, the court may issue an Ex Parte Order to Cease<br>Obstruction of or Interference with Juvenile Investigation (AOC-J-122).<br>It is vitally important that a thorough interview is conducted during CPS Intake, and that the<br>interview solicits sufficient information to make a clear-cut screening decision as to whether the<br>allegations reported meet at least one of the legal definitions of abuse, neglect, and/or<br>dependency. The use of the structured CPS Structured Intake Form (DSS-1402) ensures that<br>thorough, pertinent information is obtained, while the Structured Intake screening (refer to Intake<br>in theNC Child Welfare manual) tools serve to guide screening decisions that are based on statutory<br>definitions.<br> N.C.G.S. § 7B-303prohibits any person from obstructing or interfering with a CPS Assessment.<br>Interference means refusing to disclose the whereabouts of the juvenile, refusing to allow access to<br>the juvenile, refusing to allow the agency representative to observe or interview the juvenile in<br>private, refusing to allow the agency representative to arrange for an evaluation of the juvenile by a||

|physician or other expert, or other conduct that makes it impossible for the agency representative<br>to carry out his duty to assess the child's safety.<br>When a person or entity interferes with the CPS Assessment process, it is the worker's obligation to<br>adequately explain the need to thoroughly complete the assessment in order to ascertain the safety<br>and well-being of the child(ren).<br>It is the Division's position that the provision of Child Protective Services, including visiting and<br>interviewing the child in his or her home, with the parent's permission does not infringe upon<br>Fourth Amendment rights. Securing parental consent to interview the child is vital. Efforts to secure<br>voluntary consent shall never be coercive. The worker shall explain his or her role and express a<br>desire to interview the child in order to assess safety, risk, and the strengths and needs of the<br>family. It is important to remember that the ability to interview children at school or at childcare<br>centers has not been compromised - schools and childcare centers are not private residences.<br>Family-centered practice and the concept of involving parents and both their formal and informal<br>supports in decision-making throughout service provision can be challenging at times. It is<br>important to remember that every interaction with the family and those supports is an opportunity<br>to make a connection. Take the time to engage the family and the individuals that comprise the<br>family's support network, recognize the strengths, help with transitions, provide choices, pay<br>attention to the words used when interacting with families, and make an effort to act as a change<br>agent, rather than an authority figure. Family members and their supports should be made to feel<br>their involvement and participation throughout the life of the case is crucial and that their feedback<br>is valued.<br>Despite a worker's best efforts towards family centered practice, there will be instances where a<br>county child welfare agency must file an obstruction or interference petition in order to proceed<br>with the CPS Assessment required in the provision of Child Protective Services. If the family or<br>person interfering with or obstructing the CPS Assessment is still unwilling to cooperate, he or she<br>should be informed of the law and the potential outcomes of the filing of a petition in court. If<br>necessary, the agency director or the designee can file apetition so that the court can hear the||

|allegations and order the cessation of the obstruction. At the obstruction or interference hearing,<br>all relevant information from the CPS report should be presented. The agency must demonstrate to<br>the judge that the decision to initiate a CPS Assessment met the statutory threshold for abuse,<br>neglect, and/or dependency. In determining what information from the CPS report is relevant, it is<br>helpful to think in broad terms. The reporter's identity remains confidential. However, the judge<br>may order disclosure of the reporter during the hearing. If the director believes the child(ren) is in<br>immediate danger, he or she may seek an ex parte order. A detailed description of this procedure is<br>outlined below.<br>B.<br>REQUEST TO ADJUDICATE A CHILD AS ABUSED, NEGLECTED, AND/ORDEPENDENT<br>1.<br>Unwillingness to Accept Critically Needed Services<br>When an allegation is substantiated or a finding of Services Needed is made, some families may<br>refuse to follow through with services that are needed to keep the family intact. Other families may<br>participate only marginally, receiving virtually no benefits from the process. In such situations, the<br>agency shall document its efforts to gain cooperative participation from the family. If such efforts<br>are not successful, this documentation shall be included with a petition to the court to order family<br>participation in services. Just as when considering an obstruction or interference petition, agencies<br>shall use the family-centered practice Principles of Partnership to engage the family in a discussion<br>of the need for the parents or caretakers to accept these critical services for either themselves or<br>their children. Listening to the parents or caretakers about their thoughts, feelings, and concerns<br>related to receiving these services and providing an open and honest discussion aimed at assisting<br>the parents or caretakers in working through these issues may eliminate the need to proceed with<br>filing a juvenile petition. If filing a juvenile petition is necessary and upon an adjudication finding<br>that the juvenile is abused, neglected, and/or dependent,N.C.G.S. § 7B-904provides for a special<br>dispositional hearing to determine whether the court shall order the parents, custodian, or guardian<br>to participate with the child or children in medical, psychiatric, psychological, or other treatment.<br>The statute outlines the authority of the court to require the caretaker to undergo treatment or<br>counseling aimed at remediating or remedying behaviors or conditions that led or contributed to||

|the juvenile being adjudicated as abused, neglected, and/or dependent or that led or contributed to<br>the court's decision to remove custody from the caretaker.<br>2.<br>Safety Related Circumstances Indicating the Need for Immediate Removal<br>Sometimes the risk to the safety of a child is so great that the agency must protect the child by<br>removing the child from the home. Voluntary Placement Agreements are not appropriate for use in<br>this situation. Removal of the child is most often accomplished through the filing of a Juvenile<br>Petition (AOC-J130) with either the local clerk of court's office or when the clerk's office is closed,<br>with the local magistrate's office (as perN.C.G.S. § 7B-404). <br> N.C.G.S. § 7B-500(a)permits the county child welfare worker to take temporary physical custody<br>without a court order if there are reasonable grounds to believe that the juvenile is abused,<br>neglected, and/or dependent and that the child would be injured or could not be taken into custody<br>if it were first necessary to obtain a court order. This same statute permits the county child welfare<br>worker to arrange for the placement, care, supervision, and transportation of the juvenile while in<br>the temporary custody of the worker.<br>Upon notification by the county child welfare worker, the agency director or the designee<br>determines whether to file a petition for nonsecure custody in accordance withN.C.G.S. § 7B-502.If<br>no petition is filed, the child(ren) must be returned to the parent from whom he or she was<br>removed. When taking a juvenile into temporary custody, the agency shall:<br>•<br>Notify the parent, guardian or custodian that the juvenile has been taken into custody and<br>advise the caretaker of the right to be present with the juvenile until a determination is<br>made of the need for nonsecure custody (Worker must make every reasonable effort to<br>notify the caretaker. However, failure to notify the caretaker that the juvenile is in<br>temporary custody shall not be grounds for release of the juvenile.),<br>•<br> Release the juvenile to the parent, guardian or custodian when the need for custody no<br>longer exists, or||

All information regarding attempts must be filed with the court.<br>3.<br>Despite Agency Efforts to Provide Services, No Progress Has Been Made Toward Providing<br>Adequate Care for the Child<br>Despite an agency's best efforts to engage the family and coordinate the necessary remedial<br>services during the provision of CPS In-Home Services, families may not make sufficient and timely<br>progress in addressing the issues that led to the child abuse, neglect, and/or dependency. In these<br>cases, the agency can file a petition alleging that the child is abused, neglected, and/or dependent.<br>It is important to note that a petition may be filed asking for an adjudication of abuse, neglect,<br>and/or dependency even when the agency is not seeking removal of the child(ren) from the legal<br>custody of the parent, guardian, or custodian. This may be the case when dealing with families who,<br>despite diligent efforts made by the agency, remain uncooperative and refuse to work with the<br>agency but the child(ren) is determined to remain safe in his or her home. The Juvenile Petition<br>(AOC-J-130) filed by county child welfare agencies in this situation is the same petition filed when<br>the agency is requesting custody. The petition is filed without an Order for Nonsecure Custody<br>(AOC-J-150). This is a matter that is outlined inN.C.G.S. § 7B-302(c)which reads:<br>If the assessment indicates that abuse, neglect, or dependency has occurred, the director shall<br>decide whether immediate removal of the juvenile or any other juveniles in the home is necessary<br>for their protection. If immediate removal does not seem necessary, the director shall immediately<br>provide or arrange for protective services. If the parent, guardian, custodian, or caretaker refuses to<br>accept the protective services provided or arranged by the director, the director shall sign a<br>complaint seeking to invoke the jurisdiction of the court for the protection of the juvenile or<br>juveniles.||

Failure to comply with the order of the court may lead to the parent(s) being found in<br>civil or criminal contempt.<br>VII.<br>STEP-BY-STEP LEGAL PROCESS (NOT INCLUDING TPR)<br>The information that follows in this section is a general step-by-step examination of the most typical<br>juvenile court actions that county child welfare workers encounter. Because Termination of<br>Parental Rights (TPR) is such a complex issue, that process will receive a closer examination in a<br>separate section within this chapter. While every effort has been made to accurately reflect the<br>overall juvenile court process, the process each agency experiences may vary from district to district<br>and possibly among cases within the same district. Frequently, local rules play a definitive role in<br>determining how cases are handled within the court district. The impact of these local rules may<br>cause similar cases to appear very dissimilar from district to district. County agencies are strongly<br>encouraged to learn more about the local court rules that may be applicable for their district. The<br>local rules for each county can be found on the Administrative Office of the Courts website:<br>http://www.nccourts.org/courts/crs/policies/localrules/default.asp.||

For more detailed information on this type of a petition, refer toRefusal to<br>Cooperate with / Obstruction of a CPS Assessment.<br>When information supports a determination that a petition is needed for the protection of the<br>child(ren) alleged to be abused, neglected, or dependent, the petition shall be drawn by the<br>director or his designee, verified before an official authorized to administer oaths, and filed by the<br>clerk, recording the date of filing.<br>When preparing the Juvenile Petition alleging abuse, neglect, and/or dependency,N.C.G.S. § 7B-<br>402states, "The petition shall contain the name, date of birth, address of the juvenile, the name<br>and last known address of the juvenile's parent, guardian, or custodian, and allegations of facts<br>sufficient to invoke jurisdiction over the juvenile."<br>Using the Administrative Office of the Courts (AOC) Juvenile Petition (AOC-J-130) ensures all the<br>information required is captured. Petitions should also state the<br>severity of harm and explain how the behavior of the caretaker has resulted in the children's<br>condition. The petition should also state the ability and willingness of the caretaker to adequately<br>care for the child and, if appropriate, any services the parents have been offered but have refused.<br>Petitions should also specifically state the efforts the agency made with the family to prevent the||

The allegations shall also contain enough information to make a legally valid<br>case. These allegations should be broad enough to allow introduction of all evidence that the<br>agency considers important to the case. Allegations should only include what the agency believes<br>to be facts in the case, not observations or opinions held by others. Workers should be aware that<br>while only those allegations that rise to level of abuse, neglect, and/or dependency are to be<br>included on the petition, some allegations might fit in more than one category. The mediation process usually is called 'dependency mediation' and is<br>similar in many ways to settlement conferences, except that there is a skilled and trained mediator<br>facilitating the discussion."<br>As with Child Planning Conferences, not all 100 counties will operate in court districts where<br>mediation is used. Like CFT meetings, these meetings are facilitated and involve the family and<br>their supports (which, in this situation may include their attorney, the Attorney Advocate for the<br>GAL and the agency's attorney). The North Carolina General Assembly enacted legislation in 2006 to<br>provide for mediation services in juvenile court to resolve issues in cases in which a juvenile is<br>alleged or has been adjudicated to be abused, neglected, and/or dependent, or in which a petition<br>or motion to terminate a parent's rights has been filed. This service is not currently available in all<br>counties, but the legislation calls for the Administrative Office of the Courts to establish<br>permanency mediation in phases in all judicial districts in the state.<br>Mediation is a valuable tool for judges in juvenile court. Mediation is a process whereby a neutral<br>third party--a mediator--acts to encourage and facilitate the resolution of a dispute between<br>parties. It is informal and non- adversarial processes with the objective of helping the disputing<br>parties reach a mutually acceptable and voluntary agreement. The mediator is not a judge, lawyer,<br>or counselor, rather the mediator is a trained, skilled facilitator who assists in defining and clarifying<br>issues, helps reduce obstacles to communication, and provides the opportunity for exploring<br>possible solutions to reaching agreement.<br>Local court rules in each judicial district determine the specific process that the judge will use to<br>order the parties to participate in permanency mediation. The parties in permanency mediation<br>consist of, at a minimum, each parent, the parent(s) attorney, county child welfare worker assigned<br>to the case, county child welfare supervisor, agency attorney, attorney advocate, and GAL staff||

|and/or volunteer. North Carolina uses a co-mediation model (two mediators) which takes into<br>account the large number and diversity of people present, the complexity of the work, and the<br>demands of negotiating a petition and producing agreements to be written and signed off on in the<br>session.<br>Mediation provides the opportunity for increased parental involvement in the development of a<br>case plan tailored to the family's specific needs, as well as an opportunity for the agency to clearly<br>articulate its expectations early in the case. By assisting to move a case to permanence more<br>quickly, permanency mediation provides cost savings to both the foster care and court systems.<br>Perhaps more importantly, moving to permanence provides emotional and physical stability for the<br>child(ren).<br>A thorough explanation is provided byN.C.G.S. § 7B-202which explains:<br>(a)<br>The Administrative Office of the Courts shall establish a Permanency Mediation Program to<br>provide statewide and uniform services to resolve issues in cases under this Subchapter in which a<br>juvenile is alleged or has been adjudicated to be abused, neglected, or dependent, or in which a<br>petition or motion to terminate a parent's rights has been filed. |The adjudicatory hearing shall be a judicial process designed to adjudicate the existence or<br>nonexistence of any of the conditions alleged in a petition.<br>N.C.G.S. § 7B-802explains, "The adjudicatory hearing shall be a judicial process designed to<br>adjudicate the existence or nonexistence of any of the conditions alleged in the petition."<br>Adjudication means making a judgment (i.e., legally deciding whether something did or did not<br>occur) based on either the evidence presented or either party agreeing (stipulating) to the existence<br>or non-existence of the conditions of abuse, neglect, and/or dependency. In the adjudicatory<br>hearing, the court shall protect the rights of the juvenile and the juvenile's parent to assure due<br>process of law (N.C.G.S. § 7B-802). The adjudicatory hearing must be held within 60 days of the<br>filing of the petition unless the judge orders that it be held later (N.C.G.S. § 7B-801). <br>The allegations stated in the petition must be proven by clear and convincing evidence (N.C.G.S. §<br>7B-807). The judge may continue the hearing, for good cause, for as long as is reasonably required<br>to receive additional evidence, reports, or assessments requested by the Court or other information<br>needed in the best interest of the juvenile. The rules of evidence in civil cases apply in these<br>proceedings. All adjudicatory and dispositional hearings shall be recorded.<br>If the judge finds that the allegations in the petition have been proven, he or she shall so state. If<br>the judge finds that the allegations have not been proven, he or she shall dismiss the petition with<br>prejudice and the juvenile will be released from nonsecure custody.<br>The Order on Adjudication shall be reduced to writing, signed, and entered no later than 30 days<br>following the completion of the hearing. If the order is not entered within 30 days following<br>completion of the hearing, the clerk of court for juvenile matters shall schedule a subsequent<br>hearing at the first session of court scheduled for the hearing of juvenile matters following the 30-<br>day period to determine and explain the reason for the delay and to obtain any needed clarification<br>as to the contents of the order. The order shall be entered within ten days of the subsequent<br>hearing required by this subsection.<br>I.<br>DISPOSITIONAL HEARING||

# Juvenile Court

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|

|Reunification means that the biological/adoptive parent(s) or caretaker from whom the child(ren)<br>was removed regains custody of the child. In most cases, reunification is the primary permanent<br>resolution sought, and reasonable efforts to reunify the child(ren) with the parent must be<br>demonstrated and documented to the court. Reunification is appropriate when the parent is<br>capable of providing minimum sufficient level of care, as long as the safety of the child(ren) can be<br>ensured.<br>Reunification should be considered when:<br>•<br>The issues that precipitated the child(ren)'s removal has been addressed and resolved, and<br>•<br>Risk to the child(ren) has been reduced to a reasonable level, and<br>•<br>The parents have made changes in their behavior and circumstances that were identified as<br>needing to change before the child(ren) could be returned safely to the home, and<br>•<br>The parent has demonstrated capacity and willingness to provide appropriate care for the<br>child(ren), and<br>•<br>The child(ren)'s safety and care in the home is reasonably expected to remain secure, and<br>•<br>Supports from the agency and community are in place to assist the family to remain intact.||

|Reunification should not be considered when the court has found that such efforts would be futile<br>or would be inconsistent with the juvenile's need for a safe, permanent home within a reasonable<br>timeframe.<br>The agency is responsible for working with the parents to help them gain capacity to provide care<br>for their children. |relatives to consider include foster parents or adults who have a kinship bond with the child, even if<br>they are not related by blood.<br>4.<br>Legal Custody<br>Legal custody is another permanent option for children, although legal custody is less "legally<br>secure" than adoption or guardianship. Custody may be terminated based on a change in<br>circumstances, regardless of the fitness of the custodian. The judge can order legal custody of a<br>child to a relative, foster parent, or other adult person deemed suitable by the court. Legal custody<br>has most of the same advantages and disadvantages as legal guardianship. The specific rights and<br>responsibilities of a legal custodian, however, are defined by the court order rather than being fully<br>defined in law. The custodian must show the court order to prove his or her right to act in a<br>parental role.<br>Questions to consider when determining the recommendation for legal guardianship or legal<br>custody are as follows:<br>•<br>What pre-placement relationship existed between the child and the potential guardian or<br>custodian, and does the child have any attachment to them?<br>•<br>Have the potential guardians or custodians been carefully evaluated? Is there a written<br>assessment or home study using both the Initial Provider Assessment (DSS-5203) and the Kinship<br>Care Comprehensive Assessment (DSS-5204)? (For complete instructions on completion of the<br>Kinship Care Comprehensive Assessment, refer to DSS-5204ins).||

Page 163 Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|

|•<br>Have the potential guardians or custodians been included in the shared decision-making<br>process?<br>•<br>Have both maternal and paternal relatives been considered?<br>•<br>Is placement with relatives or fictive kin a way to protect the child's roots in<br>the community?<br>•<br>Will placement with a particular relative mean that the child must leave the community?<br>•<br>Have sibling attachments been considered? Will placement with siblings be positive for this<br>child?<br>•<br>Is this potential guardian or custodian related to all the siblings, and are they willing to take<br>all the siblings whether related or not?<br>•<br>Will this placement support the child's ethnic and cultural identity? For additional<br>information, refer toMulti-Ethnic Placement Act and Indian Child Welfare Act. <br>•<br>Is the potential guardian or custodian willing to provide a home for this child through the<br>child's minority?||

|•<br>Are there the same issues in the extended family that existed with the parents?<br>•<br>What will be the on-going relationship with the child's parents? Will there be lifelong<br>conflicts with the child's parents or is there a possibility of an unofficial return to the child's<br>parents?<br>While legal custody is not well defined in statute, it implies responsibility for the oversight of the<br>child's care, protection, training, and personal relationships. Neither guardianship of the person nor<br>custody confers authority over the disposition of the child's estate. If the child either has an estate<br>or receives income, such as through the Social Security<br>Administration (SSA), separate court action should be initiated to establish guardianship of the<br>estate. For additional information on the appointment and responsibilities of the guardian of<br>the estate, refer toN.C.G.S. § 35A-1250, N.C.G.S. § 35A-1252, andN.C.G.S. § 1253.<br>The court must order a specific visitation plan in any custody order (unless the specific findings<br>support no visitation). When seeking custody with a court-approved caretaker, the county child<br>welfare agency is strongly encouraged to make a recommendation to the court regarding specific<br>visitation plan as many custody orders have been remanded on this issue since visitation is one of<br>the specific rights that parents retain if custody becomes the permanent plan.<br>Agency Requirements Towards Legal Custody||

# Juvenile Court

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|•<br>The county child welfare agency shall conduct and demonstrate diligent efforts to locate a<br>suitable person who is willing to assume custody of the child. Both in-state and out-of-state options<br>must be considered when making reasonable efforts to place the child in accordance with the<br>permanent plan and to finalize that plan. Such efforts shall be documented in the case record and<br>the court report,<br>•<br>If the county child welfare agency is unsuccessful in locating a person willing to assume<br>custody of the child within one year, the permanent plan shall be changed unless the agency is able<br>to justify to the court why the plan should remain "custody". Justification will include the county<br>child welfare agency's progress toward locating a suitable person willing to assume legal<br>responsibility for the child.<br>•<br>The county child welfare agency shall provide information to the potential custodian about<br>more permanent and legally secure options, including adoption and legal guardianship.<br>•<br>The Kinship Care Comprehensive Assessment (DSS-5204) shall be conducted to assure that<br>the placement is physically appropriate.<br>•<br>Potential conflicts with the birth parent shall be evaluated and discussed with the<br>custodian.<br>5.<br>Reinstatement of Parental Rights||

# Juvenile Court

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|Reinstatement of Parental Rights (RPR) became a permanency option whenN.C.G.S. § 7B-1114<br>went into effect. Circumstances that would allow this permanent option are very narrow. Only the<br>youth, the county child welfare agency, or the youth's guardian ad litem attorney advocate may file<br>a motion to reinstate parental rights. Three conditions must be met in order to consider filing a<br>motion for RPR:<br>1.<br>The youth is at least 12 years of age or if under the age of 12, extraordinary circumstances<br>exist that warrant consideration of reinstatement of parental rights,<br>2.<br>The youth does not have a legal parent, is not in an adoptive placement, and is not likely to<br>be adopted within a reasonable time period, and<br>3.<br>The order terminating parental rights was entered at least three years prior unless the<br>youth's plan is no longer adoption.<br>When the county child welfare agency with custody of the youth is considering RPR, it is important<br>to hear from the youth and explore his or her thoughts, concerns, needs, etc. Care should be taken<br>to ensure that the former parent is interested and appropriate, as well as, what the impact might be<br>for the youth. A youth may be afraid to freely speak in front of a parent for fear of being disloyal or<br>hurtful, therefore, the youth should be given an opportunity to express himself or herself without<br>the former parent present prior to any Child and Family Team (CFT) Meeting. A CFT is required to<br>make a change in the permanent plan.<br>Flexibility in who should attend the CFT to discuss RPR is also required. The youth should be able to<br>invite any supports or connections that are important to him or her to the CFT. The CFT should<br>assist the youth in making an informed decision and provide him or her with an understanding of<br>any possible repercussions. If a youth does not currently have a guardian ad litem, one should be<br>requested and will be required if a motion to reinstate parental rights is filed. Finally, it is important||

Has the<br>county child welfare agency actively worked toward other permanent plan options?<br>•<br>Has the former parent remedied the conditions that led to the youth's removal and<br>placement in foster care and subsequent termination of parental rights? Is there any<br>reason to believe that the youth is receiving pressure from the former parent to choose this plan?<br>•<br>Is the former parent willing to resume contact with the youth and have rights reinstated?<br>•<br>Is the youth willing to resume contact with the former parent and have rights reinstated?||

# Juvenile Court

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|While emancipation is not a permanent placement option, and while very few youth pursue this<br>(fewer still achieve), the process of emancipation is an issue that county child welfare agencies may,<br>at some point, be required to address.<br>Emancipation occurs in a very few cases, whether through marriage, joining the armed forces, or<br>emancipation by the court. Some youth will leave the custody of the county child welfare agency<br>without ever achieving a permanent family. It is the responsibility of the county child welfare<br>agency to prepare these children for self-sufficiency. It is also the responsibility of the county child<br>welfare agency to assist the youth in forming a positive attachment to adults who can be a resource<br>to that youth through the rest of his or her childhood and into adulthood. Efforts to achieve a<br>permanent plan for the youth should always remain active.<br>If the director of the county child welfare agency is asked to consent to marriage of a child in the<br>legal custody of the agency, or to give permission for that child to join the armed services,<br>exceptionally careful consideration shall be given to ensure that the child's safety and needs will be<br>provided. UnderN.C.G.S. § 7B-3500children ages 16-18 may petition the court for a judicial decree<br>of emancipation whileN.C.G.S. § 7B-3504states that in determining the best interests of the<br>petitioner, the court will make the following considerations:<br>•<br>The parental need for the earnings of the petitioner,<br>•<br>The petitioner's ability to function as an adult,<br>•<br>The petitioner's need to contract as an adult or to marry,||

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|•<br>The employment status of the petitioner and the stability of his or her living arrangements,<br>•<br>The extent of family discord which may threaten reconciliation of the petitioner with his or<br>her family,<br>•<br>The petitioner's rejection of parental supervision or support, and<br>•<br>The quality of parental supervision or support.<br>After the final decree of emancipation, the petitioner has the same right to make contracts and<br>conveyances, to sue and be sued, and to transact business as if he or she was an adult. The parent is<br>relieved of legal duties and obligations owed to the petitioner and are divested of all rights with<br>respect to the petitioner. The decree of emancipation is irrevocable.<br>M.<br>ADOPTION RELATED COURTISSUES<br>For adoption policies relative to court issues, please refer to "Legal Guide" in Adoptions in theNC<br>Child Welfare manual.<br>VIII.<br>TERMINATION OF PARENTAL RIGHTS(TPR)||

# Juvenile Court

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|Termination of Parental Rights is the legal severing of all rights and obligations of the parent to the<br>child and of the child to the parent (except the child's right to inheritance from the parent which<br>continues until a final decree of adoption has been issued). This type of court action comes under<br>the jurisdiction of the Juvenile Division of the District Court. The North Carolina General Statutes<br>governing the legal framework for<br>Termination of Parental Rights can be found in the following statutes:N.C.G.S. § 7B-1100, N.C.G.S. §<br>7B-1101, N.C.G.S. § 7B-1101.1, N.C.G.S. § 7B-1102, N.C.G.S. § 7B-1103, N.C.G.S. § 7B-1104, N.C.G.S.<br>§ 7B-1105, N.C.G.S. § 7B-1106, N.C.G.S.. § 7B-1106.1, N.C.G.S. § 7B-1107, N.C.G.S. § 7B-1108,<br>N.C.G.S. § 7B-1109, N.C.G.S § 7B-1110, N.C.G.S. § 7B-1111, N.C.G.S. § 7B1112.<br>As with any other juvenile court action, this particular action is initiated by the filing of a motion or<br>petition for Termination of Parental Rights of the parent or parents whose consents have not been<br>obtained. The Notice of Motion Seeking Termination of Parental Rights (AOC-J-210) may be used<br>when a TPR action is filed by motion. If a TPR is filed by petition, then the Summons in Proceeding<br>for Termination of Parental Rights (AOC-J-208) must be used. As with other petitions for Juvenile<br>Court action, termination petitions are filed with the Clerk of Court. Due to the complex legal<br>nature of termination proceedings, county child welfare agencies seeking to have parental rights<br>terminated should coordinate all aspects of this court action in close consultation with their legal<br>counsel.<br>A.<br>GROUNDS FOR TPR<br>N.C.G.S. § 7B-1111provides that the first requirement for terminating the parental rights is a<br>finding by clear, cogent and convincing evidence that one or more of the following conditions exist:||

# Juvenile Court

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|1.<br>The court has found that the parent has abused or neglected the child in accordance with<br>the definitions of N.C.G.S § 7B-101, <br>2.<br>The parent has willfully left a child in foster care for more than 12 months without showing<br>to the satisfaction of the court that "reasonable progress under the circumstances has been made<br>within 12 months in correcting those conditions that led to the removal of the child,"<br>3.<br>The child has been placed in the custody of a county child welfare agency, a licensed child<br>placing facility, a child caring institution, or a foster home, and the parent, for a continuous period<br>of six months immediately prior to filing of TPR petition, has willfully failed to pay a reasonable<br>portion of the cost of care for the child although physically and financially able to do so,<br>4.<br>One parent has been awarded custody of the child by judicial decree, or has custody by<br>agreement of the parents, and the other parent whose parental rights are sought to be terminated<br>has for a period of one year or more preceding the filing of the petition willfully failed without<br>justification to pay for the care, support and education of the child, as required by said decree or<br>custody agreement,<br>5.<br>The father of a child born out of wedlock has not prior to filing of petition to terminate his<br>parental rights: (a) established paternity, (b) legitimated the child, (c) married the mother of the<br>child or (d) provided substantial financial support or consistent care with respect to the child and<br>mother, (e) established paternity through G.S. 49-14, 110-132, 130A-101, 130A-118, or other<br>judicial proceeding,||

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|

|6.<br>The parent is incapable of providing for the proper care and supervision of the child, such<br>that the child is a dependent child, and there is a reasonable probability that such incapability will<br>continue for the near future. Incapability under this subdivision may be the result of substance<br>abuse, intellectual/developmental disability, mental illness, organic brain syndrome, or any other<br>similar cause or condition,<br>7.<br>The parent has willfully abandoned the child for at least six consecutive months<br>immediately preceding the filing of the petition. # Juvenile Court

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|TPR is a two-part process. First, the agency must show by clear, cogent, and convincing evidence<br>the existence of one or more grounds for TPR. Second, even if these grounds exist, the decision to<br>terminate the parent's rights must be determined by the court to be in the child's best interest.<br>Prior to the filing of a TPR petition, agencies shall thoughtfully consider the following questions. The<br>answers to these questions should be thoroughly discussed with the agency's legal counsel in<br>consideration of preparing evidence for the court:<br>•<br>Have all appropriate services been offered to the parents in a timely manner?<br>•<br>Have the parents responded to these services in a way that demonstrates they are now able<br>to provide a minimally sufficient level of care for their children?<br>•<br>If the child has special needs, are the parents able, at the time of the TPR hearing, to meet<br>those needs?<br>•<br>Is there a reason to believe that the parents could materially improve the conditions or<br>behavior that led to the removal of their child in the next three months if given the opportunity?<br>Can any improvement be expected to last?<br>•<br>What type of relationship have the parents maintained with their child since he or she was<br>removed?||

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|

|•<br>What progress or problems has the child experienced while receiving Child Placement<br>Services?<br>The county child welfare agency must also present evidence that addresses "best interest" issues<br>for the court. The following additional questions will help develop this evidence:<br>•<br>What relatives or other kin have been considered for permanent placement? Are there<br>members of the kinship network who are willing and able to adopt the child? Is there an existing<br>emotional attachment between the adult and child? Can the child maintain contact with them if<br>they do not adopt the child? Will they be supportive of an adoptive placement? Will siblings remain<br>together? Should siblings remain together?<br>•<br>What is the potential for adoption by non-relatives? Will the foster parents adopt this child?<br>Are there other adoptive parents waiting? Will the agency conduct a child specific recruitment<br>effort?<br>•<br>Is there a balance between what the child wants against what the child needs? Does the<br>child understand the issues? What has the child said about his or her preferences? Who has talked<br>to the child? Are there conflicting loyalty issues?<br>•<br>How long has the child been waiting for a permanent home?||

|•<br>What will happen to this child if TPR is not granted? Who will "parent" this child when he or<br>she is an adult? Who will be his or her "forever" family?<br>Additionally,N.C.G.S. § 7B-1110(a)requires that the court must consider factors such as:<br>•<br>The age of the juvenile,<br>•<br>The likelihood that the juvenile will be adopted,<br>•<br>Whether TPR will aid in accomplishing the juvenile's permanent plan,<br>•<br>The bond between the juvenile and the parent,<br>•<br>The quality of the relationship between the juvenile and any proposed placement, and<br>•<br>Any other relevant consideration.<br>B.<br>PROCEDURES FOR FILING A TPR PETITION<br>In agency placements, the agency having placement authority has the responsibility for legally<br>clearing the child and directly filing the documents with the Clerk of Court. These documents||

# Juvenile Court

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|contain identifying information and shall not be given to the adoptive parents or their attorney. In<br>all other placements, the adoptive parents' attorney has the responsibility to legally clear the child<br>and file the documents with the Clerk of Court. The agency need not maintain legal clearance<br>documents in its files except for children for whom it has placement authority.<br>C.<br>TPR PROCESS<br>Adoption may become the plan for children whose parents may not be available to give consent or<br>whose parents may be unable or unwilling to care for the children. In these circumstances, one<br>method of assuring that the children are legally cleared for adoption is by obtaining a court<br>adjudicated termination of parental rights.<br>1.<br>Petition for TPR<br>A petition for termination of parental rights of a child's parent or parents may be filed by the<br>Director of the county child welfare agency or by a licensed private child-placing agency, if the<br>agency has court ordered custody of the child or if the child has been relinquished for adoption to<br>the agency by one of the parents or by the guardian of the person of the child. Others who may file<br>a petition include either parent, seeking termination of the other parent's parental rights, a<br>judicially appointed guardian of the person of the child, any person with whom the child has lived<br>for two or more years, and a Guardian ad Litem appointed to represent the minor child pursuant to<br>NCGS § 7B-601, who has not been relieved of this responsibility, and any person who has filed a<br>petition for adoption.||

Page 182 Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

<!-- page: 184 -->
# Juvenile Court

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|The petition must contain information that is specified inN.C.G.S. § 7B-1104, including the facts<br>that are considered sufficient to warrant a determination that one or more of the grounds for<br>terminating parental rights exists. If the identity and/or whereabouts of the parent are unknown,<br>an affidavit should<br>be attached to the petition indicating both this fact and the efforts undertaken to ascertain the<br>identity and whereabouts of the parent. The affidavit is to be that of the petitioner. If the petitioner<br>is not one of the child's parents, the affidavit should include information furnished to the petitioner<br>by the parent, if available, such as a mother's sworn statement of circumstances that resulted in her<br>pregnancy without her knowledge of the father's identity. Alternatively, the affidavit may be based<br>on personal information or absence thereof about the child's parent or parents.<br>2.<br>Preliminary Hearing in the Case of Unknown Parent(s)<br>If it appears to the court before which the petition for Termination of Parental Rights is filed that<br>the identity of the child's parent or parents is unknown, the court shall, within ten days of the filing<br>of the petition (or during the first available court period), conduct a preliminary hearing in an effort<br>to ascertain the identity of the unknown parent. Though notice of this hearing is required only to<br>the petitioner, the court may summon other persons to the hearing if it is felt that others may be<br>apt to provide the identifying information.<br>If the identity and whereabouts of the parent are not ascertained, notice of a hearing to terminate<br>parental rights shall be served upon the unknown parent by publication, as set forth inN.C.G.S. §<br>7B-1105. If the unknown parent served by process of publication fails to answer the petition within<br>the 30-day time period prescribed in the notice, the statute mandates the court to issue an order<br>terminating the unknown parent's parental rights.||

|3.<br>Summons<br>In cases in which the identity of the parent whose rights are sought to be terminated is known,<br>diligent attempts must be made to provide personal service of summons and petition. If personal<br>service is not possible, the statute provides for alternate methods of service. The summons shall<br>notify the parent to file written answer to the petition within 30 days after service of summons and<br>petition.N.C.G.S. § 7B-1106outlines the parties that should be recipients of the TPR summons. It<br>should be noted that this statute indicates that the juvenile will receive service of the summons but<br>goes on to also say, "Except that the summons and other pleadings or papers directed to the<br>juvenile shall be served upon the juvenile's guardian ad litem if one has been appointed, service of<br>the summons shall be completed as provided under the procedures established byN.C.G.S. 1A-1,<br>Rule 4(j)." Termination of Parental Rights matters have been vacated by the North Carolina Court of<br>Appeals based on the lack of summons on the juvenile or his or her appointed Guardian ad Litem.<br>4.<br>TPR Hearing<br>After the time prescribed in the summons has expired, and whether there has been written answer<br>to the petition from the parents served with the summons, the court shall conduct a hearing to<br>determine the issues regarding termination of parental rights.N.C.G.S. § 7B-1107directs that:<br>Upon the failure of parent to file written answer to the petition or written response to the motion<br>within 30 days after service of the summons and petition or notice and motion, or within the time<br>period established for a defendant's reply byN.C.G.S. § 1A-1, Rule 4(j1)if service is by publication,||

|the court may issue an order terminating all parental and custodial rights of that parent with<br>respect to the juvenile.<br>The court must appoint a Guardian ad Litem for the child to represent the child's best interests only<br>if an answer is filed denying material allegations. If a Guardian ad Litem has already been<br>appointed, pursuant toN.C.G.S. § 7B601, then the GAL and the Attorney Advocate continue to<br>represent the juvenile. The court must also schedule a special hearing to determine the issues<br>raised by the petition and the answer, and must provide notice of that hearing to the petitioner,<br>parent, and GAL. The hearing shall not be scheduled less than ten days or more than 30 days from<br>the time of serving notice and is often held just before the termination of parental rights hearing<br>(N.C.G.S. § 7B-1108). <br>a.<br>Adjudicatory Portion of Hearing<br>The burden at this stage is on the petitioning agency to show by clear, cogent, and convincing<br>evidence the existence of one or more grounds for termination of parental rights. The court may<br>request additional evidence, reports, and information and may continue the hearing for the time<br>required for the receipt of such informationN.C.G.S. § 7B-1109. In addition, pursuant to this<br>statute, the adjudicatory hearing on termination shall be held no later than 90 days following the<br>filing of the petition or motion unless the court has entered a continuance for up to 90 days for<br>good cause. Continuances beyond 90 days shall only be granted in extraordinary circumstances for<br>the proper administration of justice.<br>In cases in which a county child welfare agency petitions the court for termination of parental rights<br>of a child in its care and/or custody, it is essential that the agency case records contain well<br>documented entries that indicate the type and frequency of agency attempts to involve the parent,||

|to correct or better conditions that led to the child's removal, to establish paternity, to obtain<br>reasonable support for the child, etc.<br>The length of time the child has received Child Placement Services, dates of contacts, kinds of<br>contacts and quality of contacts, and contents of communication with the parent will be essential in<br>establishing the county child welfare agency's justification for termination. If the court finds that grounds<br>do not exist, the court shall dismiss the petition or deny the motion after making the appropriate<br>facts and conclusions (N.C.G.S. § 7B1110). At this stage of the proceeding, the petitioner does not<br>have the burden of proof, the court hears all evidence and makes a discretionary determination of<br>best interest. Should the court determine that despite the existence of circumstances warranting<br>termination of parental rights, such action would not be conducive to the child's best interests, the<br>court may dismiss the petition after first setting forth facts and conclusions upon which the decision<br>for dismissal is based.<br>5.<br>Appeals||

|NCGS § 7B-1001 sets forth the provisions for an appeal of an adjudication or order of disposition by<br>any person who has been a party to a termination of parental rights proceeding. The appeal is made<br>to the North Carolina Court of Appeals.<br>6.<br>Effects of TPR Order<br>A termination order has the effect of completely and permanently severing all parent/child rights<br>and obligations between the parent and the child, except that the child's right to inherit from his or<br>her parents will not be terminated until a Final Decree of Adoption has been issued. If the child is in<br>the custody of a county child welfare agency or a licensed private child placing agency at the time<br>the termination is filed, upon entry of the termination order that agency shall have all placement<br>rights to the child, including the right to consent to the adoption. The agency's consent in such<br>cases should be executed in writing. It is possible for the agency to be vested with consenting<br>authority in some situations in which it was not the petitioner for termination of parental rights,<br>such as if foster parents or a GAL petition for termination regarding a child in agency custody. In<br>the case of foster parent petitioners, it is anticipated that usually the agency will feel that adoption<br>by such long-term caretakers is in the child's best interests and will agree for the foster parents to<br>adopt. In cases in which the child had not been in agency custody or relinquished by one parent to<br>an agency for adoptive placement and the petition for termination was filed by someone other than<br>an agency director or representative, the court, upon terminating parental rights, may place<br>custody of the child with the petitioner, or some other suitable person, or with an agency, according<br>to the child's needs and interests.<br>7.<br>Post-Termination Reviews||

|PerN.C.G.S. § 7B-909, reviews are required following termination of parental rights in order to<br>ensure that every reasonable effort is being made to finalize a permanent plan for the child who has<br>been placed in the custody of a county agency or licensed child-placing agency.<br>Placement reviews shall be held no later than six months from the date of the termination of<br>parental rights. Subsequent reviews shall be held every six months until the juvenile is placed for<br>adoption and a final decree of adoption is entered. Additionally, the appointment of the GAL<br>continues until the decree of adoption is filed. At each review hearing, the court may consider<br>information from the agency, the licensed child-placing agency, the Guardian ad Litem, the child,<br>the foster parent, and any other person or agency the court determines is likely to aid in the review.<br>The court shall consider at least the following in its review:<br>•<br>The adequacy of the plan developed by the county child welfare agency or licensed child-<br>placing agency for a permanent placement,<br>•<br>Whether the child has been listed with the NC Adoption Resource Exchange, the North<br>Carolina Photo Adoption Listing Services (PALS), or any other specialized adoption agency,<br>•<br>The specific recruitment efforts made by the agency to find a permanent home for the<br>child,<br>•<br>Whether the agency's efforts toward achieving the permanent plan for the child are<br>sufficient or reasonable.||

The review hearing shall be<br>cancelled, and the clerk shall give notice of the cancellation to all persons previously notified.<br>The process of selection of specific adoptive parents shall be the responsibility of and within the<br>discretion of the county child welfare agency or licensed child-placing agency. For<br>additional information on IV-E eligibility refer to Appendix 3.5 in theNC Child Welfare manual. The<br>order must say that continuation in the home is contrary to the welfare of the child or removal is in<br>the best interest of the child. This must be in the very first court order pertaining to the child's<br>removal from the home. A finding that there were no other reasonable means to protect the||

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|

|juvenile meets this requirement. This must be in the initial removal order which must be the Order<br>on Nonsecure Custody if the child is removed immediately by a nonsecure order.<br>In addition, the court order must state that the county child welfare agency has placement and care<br>responsibility. The removal order must result in the immediate departure of the child from the<br>home unless the court order specifies an alternative timeframe that assures the safety of the child.<br>In instances when a child is removed as a result of a hearing on the merits of the petition, rather<br>than a nonsecure order, or pursuant to undisciplined or delinquent actions, or in a motion for<br>review when the county child welfare agency has custody but not placement authority, then the<br>court order that results in placement must contain these findings because that would be the initial<br>removal order. If this language is not in the very first court order, even if the findings are made later<br>and included in a subsequent order, IV-E eligibility cannot be established for this removal period.<br>B.<br>REASONABLE EFFORTS<br>Within 60 days of the removal of the child from the home, there must be a judicial determination<br>that the efforts made by the county child welfare agency to prevent the removal of the child were<br>reasonable or sufficient. The court may also meet this requirement by finding that under the<br>circumstances (such as imminent danger to the child), making no efforts was reasonable. The court<br>may also find that no efforts were required if certain specific situations exist (N.C.G.S. § 7B-507). In<br>cases where the county child welfare agency is unable to locate the child, details of diligent efforts<br>should be provided to the court. While the federal regulations allow a finding of reasonable efforts<br>within 60 days,N.C.G.S. § 7B-506(a) requires a finding of reasonable efforts at the nonsecure<br>custody hearing (as well as all subsequent nonsecure, dispositional, and review hearings), thus a<br>judicial determination regarding reasonable efforts must be obtained at the earliest opportunity.||

The court report and the county child welfare worker's testimony<br>must each accurately reflect the county child welfare agency's knowledge of the child and family.<br>The contents of court reports may differ according to the requirements of the juvenile court district.<br>However, the purpose of the report is consistent-- to document the agency's findings,<br>determinations of risk of further harm, plan goals and activities intended to correct the conditions<br>that led to the request for custody and/or removal, and recommendations to the court for judicial<br>action. Permanency Planning<br>The majority of court reports that will be written are for permanency planning hearings. These shall<br>inform the court whether thePermanency Planning Family Services Agreement (DSS-5240)has<br>been followed and whether the family has made progress on the goals of the plan. |E.<br>POST-TERMINATION OF PARENTAL RIGHTS REVIEW<br>As required under (N.C.G.S. § 7B-908), Post-Termination of Parental Rights Reviews are designed to,<br>"ensure that every reasonable effort is being made to provide for a permanent placement plan for<br>the juvenile who has been placed in the custody of a county director or licensed child-placing<br>agency which is consistent with the juvenile's best interests." Thus, these court reports shall<br>contain:<br>•<br>Description of the efforts that the agency has made toward achievement of the permanent<br>plan,<br>•<br>The current status of the child,<br>•<br>The steps taken by the county child welfare agency to find an adoptive family for the<br>child(ren), including what recruitment efforts have occurred on behalf of the child(ren) which<br>listings have been posted to on state, regional, and national exchanges, and any completed or<br>pending referrals made to private agencies,<br>•<br>The results of the recruitment efforts, and<br>•<br>The plans for the child and possible barriers to the implementation of the plans.||

# Juvenile Court

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|XI.<br>SPECIAL ISSUES<br>A.<br>CONTINUANCES<br>North Carolina Supreme Court Rules require that each judicial district adopt policies on limiting<br>continuances. Rule 4 of the State of North Carolina District Court Model Continuance Policy (1997)<br>states the following regarding juvenile abuse and neglect cases:<br>The goal of a case management plan for juvenile court is to put the courts in the best position to<br>ensure the safety of children, and give them the best possible chance of living in stable, permanent<br>families. Therefore, continuances should be allowed only when it serves the child's best interest.<br>Participants must come to court prepared to meet each statutory obligation that is required for<br>resolution of these matters. Accordingly, juvenile cases, including motions for review in neglect and<br>abuse matters, should be disposed at the earliest opportunity, including the first setting for hearing.<br>In resolving court conflicts, juvenile cases shall take precedence over all other matters.<br>Continuances should not be allowed for the convenience of attorneys or parties.<br>Continuances should be granted in extraordinary circumstances such as the illness<br>of an attorney, inability to locate witnesses, or service of process has not yet been completed.<br>When cases are calendared, parties should be prepared for, and expect, a meaningful event to<br>occur. When cases are continued as a matter of practice, usually upon request and agreement of<br>both parties and their counsel, valuable court time is lost, and time is lost towards achieving<br>permanence for children.||

# Juvenile Court

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|

|N.C.G.S. § 7B-803states:<br>"The court may, for good cause, continue the hearing for as long as is reasonably required to<br>receive additional evidence, reports, or assessments that the court has requested, or other<br>information needed in the best interests of the juvenile and to allow for a reasonable time for the<br>parties to conduct expeditious discovery. Otherwise, continuances shall be granted only in<br>extraordinary circumstances when necessary for the proper administration of justice or in the best<br>interests of the juvenile."<br>Often, how the local court districts have complied with Supreme Court's requirement of the<br>adoption of policies limiting continuances is to include this information in the court district's local<br>rules. It should be noted that for IV-E eligibility requirements, continuances are not sufficient to<br>meet the requirement unless the court actually hears evidence related to the agency's efforts to<br>prevent removal.<br>B.<br>CHANGING VENUE<br>N.C.G.S. § 7B-400states:<br>"A proceeding in which a juvenile is alleged to be abused, neglected, and/or dependent may be<br>commenced in the district in which the juvenile resides or is present. When a proceeding is<br>commenced in a district other than that of the juvenile's residence, the court, on its own motion or<br>upon motion of any party, may transfer the proceeding to the court in the district where the<br>juvenile resides. A transfer under this section may be made at any time."||

Page 199 Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

# Juvenile Court

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|Transfers of the physical juvenile court case are made by the Clerks of Court and are not as simple<br>as sending one file from one district to another. In lay terms, a motion (in the sending county) to<br>transfer a case from one district to another is made and notice is provided to the receiving district<br>and the receiving county child welfare agency. A hearing is held on the motion, at which the<br>receiving agency should be permitted to be heard.<br>C.<br>FAMILY COURT<br>Family Court is a group of specially trained judges and staff in a district who seek to quickly respond<br>to family and child concerns. In Family Court, a case coordinator assigns one judge to one family to<br>hear the matters that occur when a family breaks apart, such as divorce, child custody and<br>visitation, property sharing and alimony. In districts without Family Court, each of these family<br>matters is assigned separately to whichever judge is holding court on the date of the hearing, so he<br>or she may not be familiar with all of the family's issues.<br>Case coordinators also assign a specially trained judge to one family to hear cases of child abuse,<br>neglect, dependency, and juvenile delinquency. Judges and staff work to make sure that these<br>cases are heard in court regularly, based on time standards in the law.<br>One of the goals of family court is to encourage families to reduce conflict and solve problems<br>through mediation outside of court, rather than having the judge conduct a trial. Mediation can be<br>used to settle money matters such as child support, and/or to reach an agreement on child custody<br>and visitation. Another goal of Family Court is to complete hearings on family matters within one<br>year. Family court staff keeps the legal issues moving through the court process by scheduling<br>hearings according to time standards and organizing court calendars so that meaningful events<br>occur at each court session.||

Page 200 Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

<!-- page: 202 -->
# Juvenile Court

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|D.<br>PETITION AMENDMENT<br>As per N.C.G.S. § 7B-800, "The court may permit a petition to be amended when the amendment<br>does not change the nature of the conditions upon which the petition is based." A Juvenile Petition<br>may need to be amended by a county child welfare agency if additional information becomes<br>available. As with any court related action, the decision to amend a petition to add to or remove<br>information from the original petition must be carefully weighed by the county child welfare agency<br>in close consultation with its legal counsel. There is no specific Juvenile Petition Amendment form<br>available. Agencies may simply file the Juvenile Petition for Abuse / Neglect / Dependency (AOC-J-<br>130) with the amended information included.<br>E.<br>MODIFYING OR VACATING ORDERS<br>The court has the authority to modify or vacate any previous orders perN.C.G.S. §7B-1000. The<br>statute reads, "Upon motion in the cause or petition, and after notice, the court may conduct a<br>review hearing to determine whether the order of the court is in the best interest of the juvenile,<br>and the court may modify or vacate the order in light of changes in the circumstances or the needs<br>of the juvenile." The ability of the court to modify or vacate any order or disposition continues until<br>the juvenile reaches the age of 18, until the juvenile is otherwise emancipated, or until the court<br>case is terminated by order of the judge.<br>F.<br>OBTAINING SUBSTANCE ABUSE RECORDS BY COURT ORDER||

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|Alcohol and drug abuse patient records are protected under the federal regulations, 42 CFR Part 2<br>and cannot be disclosed or re-disclosed without the patient's written consent unless otherwise<br>provided for in the regulations. 42 CFR Part 2 allows for substance abuse records to be released<br>without written consent under the following conditions:<br>1.<br>To medical personnel to meet a bona fide medical emergency,<br>2.<br>To qualified personnel for the purpose of conducting scientific research, audits or program<br>evaluation, and<br>3.<br>If authorized by an appropriate order of a court of competent jurisdiction granted after<br>application showing good cause, therefore. See below for instructions on how to obtain this<br>court order.<br>However, no State law may either authorize or compel any disclosure prohibited by the federal<br>regulations. The Guardian ad Litem's general appointment letter does not allow access to<br>information about the diagnosis or treatment of substance abuse for the child or parent/caretaker.<br>If the GAL does not have a court order explicitly allowing for the release of alcohol or drug abuse<br>patient records for the child or parent/caretaker, those records and any references to them would<br>have to be removed from the record prior to the GAL's review.<br>Please note that a violation of any provision of 42 CFR Part 2 is subject to a criminal penalty, a fine<br>of not more than $500.00 for the first offense and not more than $5,000.00 for any subsequent<br>offense.||

## Filing a Petition

FILING A PETITION

This section specifies how to file a petition for adjudication for abuse / neglect / dependency with or without filing for non-secure custody. See CPS Assessment policy for petitioning regarding Refusal to Cooperate with / Obstruction of a CPS Assessment.

When the local county child welfare agency determines that a petition is needed for the protection of a child(ren) alleged to be abused, neglected, or dependent, the petition must be drawn by the director or their designee, reviewed by the county's legal counsel, issued by a judge or delegated magistrate, and filed by the clerk, recording the date of filing. Whether removal of the child(ren) is required, the Juvenile Petition (form AOC-J-130) is filed with either the local clerk of court's office or, when the clerk's office is closed, with the local magistrate's office. Using the Administrative Office of the Courts (AOC) Juvenile Petition (form AOC-J-130) ensures that all of the information required is captured.

When a child(ren) is placed outside their county of residence as the result of a Safety plan developed on the Safety Assessment or Conflict of Interest (COI), whether during a CPS-A or In-Home Services, this does not change the original venue (resident county) when filing a Juvenile Petition.

Along with Juvenile Petitions, a Juvenile Summons and Notice of Hearing must also be filed (form AOC-J-142). The Juvenile Summons also contains the following information:

• A parent's rights to legal representation; • In many districts, information relative to the date, time, and location of a prehearing conference or child planning conference; • If the agency has assumed custody of the child(ren) when filing a petition, information related to the hearing on need for continued nonsecure custody (7-day hearing); • Information that the dispositional order (or any subsequent order) may require certain activities of either the parent or the juvenile or may even remove the juvenile from the parent's custody; • Information related to the local law enforcement officer's ability or inability to serve the summons, petition, affidavit as to the status of a minor child, and order for non-secure custody (if applicable) on the persons identified within the summons; • A notice to parents, guardians, or caretakers that they may be held in contempt of court if they fail to show, without reasonable cause, at the hearing specified; and

Page 211 Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

<!-- page: 214 -->
# Filing a Petition

• An additional notice that with the service of the summons on the parents, guardians, or caretaker the court system has obtained jurisdiction over them and that their failure to comply with any court order may result in the court issuing a show cause order for contempt.

Juvenile Petitions should also include information relative to the agency's knowledge about issues including:

• Paternity or information on absent / missing parent(s); • Known relatives able and willing to provide care for the child(ren); • Any person with legal custody of the victim child(ren)'s sibling. Adoptive parents of siblings are not considered relatives of the legal child; however, they must be considered in the identification and notification of individuals who have legal custody of a sibling.

• Child(ren)'s Mexican heritage; • ICWA related issues; • MEPA related issues; • Information regarding the parent's military affiliation (http://www.nccourts.org/Forms/Documents/1664.pdf) Service Members Civil Relief Act Affidavit; and • Siblings or other juveniles remaining in the home and any specific findings of the assessment of the juveniles or any actions taken to secure the protection of the juveniles.

# Placement (or Placement Change) Preparation and Follow-Up

<!-- page: 228 -->
## Temporary Safety Providers & Kinship Providers

Policy

When during provision of child welfare services the child(ren) is placed in the care of a provider other than the parent or caretaker, the county child welfare services agency must remain involved and maintain the required contacts with the child(ren), the family providing placement, and the family until safety can be assured and the child(ren) can be reunified with the family or until the child(ren) is in a legally secure placement, whether it be custody or guardianship or adoption. Parents must be involved, as well as the safety provider, with the county child welfare services agency in planning at every stage of the case.

Definitions

Safety Provider: Any person or persons (either Temporary Safety Provider or Kinship Provider) that is not the parent or caretaker but is providing care for a child(ren) and is required for child safety.

Temporary Safety Provider (TSP): A voluntary, temporary intervention made between a parent and a county child welfare services agency during the delivery of child protective services. TSPs are used to address immediate safety threats to a child(ren) when a child(ren) is found unsafe in the care of their parents/caretakers during child protective services. TSP's must only be used when less intrusive safety interventions are not sufficient. TSPs may care for the child(ren) outside of their home or provide supervision of the parent's contact with the child(ren) in or outside of their home.

Kinship Provider: A relative or fictive/nonrelative kin identified or in place during Permanency Planning Services. Kinship is the self-defined relationship between two or more people and is based on biological, legal, and/or strong family-like ties. Identification of a Kinship Care Provider by a parent is desired; however, a parent may not always agree with the decision to evaluate or place the child(ren) with a specific kinship care provider. Placement with a Kinship Care Provider often lasts for months or years, must have court oversight, and addresses safety and/or risk factors.

Nonrelative Kin: An individual having a substantial relationship with the juvenile. In the case of a juvenile member of a state-recognized tribe as set forth in N.C.G.S. 143B-407(a), nonrelative kin also include any member of a state-recognized tribe or a member of a federally recognized tribe, whether or not there is a substantial relationship with the juvenile.
