SeeInitiating Use of<br>Safety Provider.<br>**Monitoring Kinship Providers**<br>For Permanency Planning cases, the Comprehensive Provider Assessment (DSS-5204)<br>must be completed for Kinship Providers:<br>•<br>When use of the Kinship Provider continues over 29 days after the case is<br>transferred to Permanency Planning, or<br>•<br>When use of the Kinship Provider initiates during Permanency Planning<br>Services and continues in use over 29 days after it was initiated.<br>The Comprehensive Provider Assessment must be completed within 30 days of the<br>child(ren) entering custody if the child(ren) is already placed out of the home, or<br>within 30 days of the child(ren)'s placement in the home of the kinship provider (if<br>placed after entering custody).<br>In addition to completing the initial and comprehensive assessment, agency staff must<br>maintain sufficient contact with the kinship care provider and the child(ren) to assure<br>that the basic physical and emotional needs of the child(ren) are being met and that<br>the kinship care provider is receiving adequate informal and formal support to meet<br>those needs. Whether the home is licensed as a foster home, county child welfare<br>workers must maintain contact as designated in Permanency Planning protocol.|USE OF KINSHIP PROVIDERS<br>Providing all information to a kinship provider is a very<br>important part of concurrent planning and provides the<br>kinship care provider with valuable information that has<br>implications later, especially as plans for permanency<br>change for the child(ren) as time goes on.<br>It is critical that the agency demonstrate sensitivity to the<br>unique issues that are present when relatives and other kin<br>are assessed for their suitability to parent child(ren).<br>Assessment should be based on an understanding of the<br>kinship family's culture and community, child rearing<br>approaches, and family dynamics, and should focus on the<br>ability of the family to meet the immediate and ongoing<br>needs of the child(ren).<br>If kin are determined to be suitable for the care and<br>nurturing of the child(ren), but the home cannot meet all<br>foster care licensing requirements, the agency may submit<br>justification for a waiver to the Regulatory and Licensing<br>office, NCDSS. In North Carolina, many licensure<br>requirements may be considered for waiver if approval is in<br>the best interest of the child(ren), if the health, safety and<br>protection of the child is assured, and if the county child<br>welfare services agency recommends that the waiver(s) be<br>granted. There are no waivers for training requirements, for<br>well inspection requirements, or for placements that have<br>outside toilet facilities.|

<!-- page: 238 -->
## Domestic Violence

Purpose

Following are the six principles developed through the Child Well-Being and Domestic Violence Task Force to address the intersection of child safety, permanence, well-being, and domestic violence.

• Enhancing a non-offending parent/adult victim's safety enhances their child(ren)'s safety. • Domestic violence perpetrators may cause serious harm to the child(ren). • Domestic violence perpetrators, not their victims, should be held accountable for their actions and the impact on the well-being of the non-offending parent/adult victim and child victims. • Appropriate services, tailored to the degree of violence and risk, should be available for non-offending parent/adult victims leaving, returning to, or staying in abusive relationships. These services should also be available for child victims and perpetrators of domestic violence. • Child(ren) should remain in the care of the non-offending parent/adult victim whenever possible. • When the risk of harm to the child(ren) outweighs the detriment of being separated from the non-offending parent/adult victim, alternative placement should be considered.

The primary focus in cases involving domestic violence is the assessment of risk posed to the child(ren) by the presence of domestic violence. The goals of CPS interventions in cases involving domestic violence are:

• Ensure the safety of the child(ren). • All family members will be safe from harm. • The non-offending parent/adult victim will receive services designed to protect and support them. • The child(ren) will receive services designed to protect, support, and help them cope with the effects of domestic violence. • The alleged perpetrator of domestic violence will be held responsible for their abusive behavior. • The incidence of child maltreatment co-occurring with domestic violence will be reduced.

The challenge in providing CPS interventions in domestic violence situations is to keep the child(ren) safe without:

• Penalizing the non-offending parent/adult victim and • Escalating the violent behavior of the alleged perpetrator of domestic violence.

Page 235 Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

<!-- page: 239 -->
# Domestic Violence

|Definition|Legal Basis|
|---|---|
|Domestic violence is defined as the establishment of control and fear in<br>an intimate relationship using violence and other forms of abuse<br>including but not limited to:<br>•<br>Physical abuse,<br>•<br>Emotional abuse,<br>•<br>Sexual abuse,<br>•<br>Economic oppression,<br>•<br>Isolation,<br>•<br>Threats,<br>•<br>Intimidation, and<br>•<br>Maltreatment of the children to control the non-offending<br>parent/adult victim.<br>While victims and families may experience and be affected by domestic<br>violence in different ways, there are still core aspects of domestic<br>violence that are consistent across racial, socio-economic, educational,<br>and religious lines:<br>•<br>The primary goal of a domestic violence perpetrator is to obtain<br>and maintain power and control over their partner.<br>•<br>While domestic violence may "present" as an incident of violence<br>or neglect, it is rather a pattern of abuse, which may include<br>violent incidents.<br>•<br>Domestic violence is not simply discord between intimate<br>partners but rather a progressive, intentional, patterned use of<br>abusive behaviors.|TheN.C.G.S. § Chapter 50-B also defines domestic violence according to<br>the relationship between the parties and behaviors or actions that<br>constitute domestic violence, as well as its available relief. North Carolina<br>General Statutes also identify certain misdemeanor and felony criminal<br>offenses that often occur in the context of domestic violence, such as<br>assault, stalking, violation of a Domestic Violence Protection Order,<br>domestic criminal trespass, harassing telephone calls, communicating a<br>threat, and strangulation.|

Page 236 Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

<!-- page: 240 -->
# Domestic Violence

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|**INTERACTION WITH NON-OFFENDING**<br>**PARENT/CARETAKER**<br>The non-offending parent/adult victim must never<br>be placed in danger by having to be interviewed,<br>develop safety plans, or meet with the perpetrator<br>of violence against them.|Each parent or caretaker is only responsible for their own actions to provide safe, nurturing<br>care for their child(ren).<br>INTERACTION WITH NON-OFFENDING PARENT/CARETAKER<br>The Non-Offending Parent/Adult Victim Domestic Violence Assessment Tool (DSS-5235)<br>contains scaled assessment questions and should be used to support the determination of<br>safety and risk factors.<br>The inability to speak with the non-offending parent/adult victim alone may be an indication of<br>the level of control the perpetrator of domestic violence exerts over the family, and an<br>indication of high risk. The presence of relatives or friends may also affect disclosure and<br>safety.<br>Information concerning resources and referrals to services should immediately be given to the<br>non-offending parent/adult victim and child(ren) (as appropriate).<br>With cases involving domestic violence, the safety of child(ren) is closely linked to the safety of<br>the non-offending parent/adult victim. So, domestic violence cases also include a secondary<br>focus on the safety of the adult victim. The non-offending parent/adult victim of domestic<br>violence is the expert at predicting the domestic violence perpetrator's reactions. Therefore,<br>development of the family safety plan or services agreement is driven by the non-offending<br>parent/adult victim based on what they think they are capable of and willing to do to ensure<br>safety for their child(ren) and themselves.<br>A Safety Plan is a tool used by domestic violence advocates in providing services to<br>non-offending parents/adult victims. The Personalized Domestic Violence Safety Plan<br>(DSS-5233)contains suggested steps that may be useful for county child welfare agencies in:<br>•<br>Safety planning with the non-offending parent/adult victim and<br>•<br>Assisting in the development of service agreements.|

Page 237 Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

# Domestic Violence

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|**INTERACTION WITH THE CHILD(REN)**<br>The child(ren) must not be interviewed in the<br>presence of the alleged perpetrator of the domestic<br>violence incident. It is appropriate to interview the<br>child(ren) in the presence of the non-offending<br>parent/adult victim as circumstances allow, and<br>when the safety of the child(ren) is not<br>compromised.<br>Information obtained from the non-offending<br>parent/adult victim or child(ren) that may jeopardize<br>their safety must not be shared,especially with the<br>alleged perpetrator of domestic violence. Sharing|Keep in mind that a perpetrator (or their legal representative) can subpoena the contents of a<br>case file. For the protection of the victim, the county child welfare services agency should<br>make decisions on where and how domestic violence safety plans are maintained.<br>To develop and monitor a coordinated services plan for every case with domestic violence, the<br>county child welfare worker should:<br>•<br>Seek out and utilize the consultation of a domestic violence expert throughout the life<br>of the case.<br>•<br>Communicate with a domestic violence perpetrator's probation or parole officer<br>regarding any current abuse.<br>•<br>Reach out and make connections with school social workers and teachers to gain<br>information about the child(ren)'s day-to-day functioning.<br>•<br>Work closely with Work First to create plans together. This is especially true when<br>Work First may already be providing or can assist in referring a family for domestic<br>violence services.<br>INTERACTION WITH THE CHILD(REN)<br>The Children's Domestic Violence Assessment Tool,DSS-5237, contains scaled assessment<br>questions and should be used to support the determination of the safety and risk factors.<br>Every child reacts differently when exposed to domestic violence. Some child(ren) develop<br>debilitating conditions, while others show no negative effects from the exposure to violence.<br>As a result, it is important to interview the child(ren) regarding their involvement and/or<br>exposure to domestic violence, as well as their general safety and well-being. It is important to<br>recognize that older child(ren) are more likely to minimize reports of parental fighting. Younger<br>child(ren) may be more spontaneous and less guarded with the information they share. See<br>Impact on Childrensection of the Cross Function topic of Risk.|

|information that may seem inconsequential—<br>specifically information about the non-<br>offending/adult victim's whereabouts and/or<br>schedule if they have left the home/relationship—<br>can place the child(ren) and non-offending<br>parent/adult victim in danger.|**INTERACTION WITH THE ALLEGED PERPETRATOR**<br>The Domestic Violence Perpetrator Assessment Tool (DSS-5234) contains scaled assessment<br>questions and should be used to support the determination of the safety and risk factors.<br>Interaction with the alleged perpetrator of domestic violence provides the opportunity to<br>observe and document behaviors relative to the allegations, both positive and "concerning."<br>This observation supplements information obtained from:<br>•<br>Police reports;<br>•<br>Criminal records;<br>•<br>Hospital/medical records;<br>•<br>The child(ren); and<br>•<br>The non-offending parent/adult victim.<br>It is important to note that the alleged perpetrator of domestic violence may attempt to:<br>•<br>Present themselves as the "victim";<br>•<br>Charm the county child welfare worker;<br>•<br>Gain control of the interview; and/or<br>•<br>Deny any domestic violence, insisting that the relationship is "perfect."<br>During interaction with the perpetrator, the county child welfare worker should:<br>•<br>Focus on information from third party reports such as law enforcement, medical<br>providers, or the Administrative Office of the Courts.<br>•<br>Follow up on legal accountability and/or treatment and other service referrals for the<br>allegedperpetrator of domestic violence.|

# Domestic Violence

|||•<br>Convey to the alleged perpetrator of domestic violence that based on what happened<br>(citing as much information as possible without compromising confidentiality or safety<br>of the child(ren), non-offending parent/adult victim, and/or the reporter) they will be<br>required to take steps to stop the violence and ensure that the child(ren) are safe.<br>•<br>Avoid debates and arguments with the alleged perpetrator of domestic violence. This<br>is crucial. The focus of CPS is not to convince the alleged perpetrator of domestic<br>violence to admit violent behavior but discuss how to ensure the child(ren)'s safety<br>with them.<br>•<br>Set limits within the interaction with the alleged perpetrator of domestic violence and<br>document the behaviors that make setting limits necessary and their capacity to<br>respect those efforts.<br>**COLLATERAL CONTACTS**<br>•<br>It should be remembered that domestic violence usually occurs in private and<br>collaterals may not always be aware of the violence.<br>•<br>Collateral contacts being unaware of the occurrence of violence does not mean that it<br>is not happening.|

|**CHILD WELL-BEING**<br>All child well-being needs or**any lack of medical, dental, mental health, or other**<br>**care**of the child must be:<br>•<br>Assessed during the provision of all child welfare services,<br>•<br>Addressed by the county child welfare services agency or through service<br>referrals,<br>•<br>Reviewed during development and review of Family Services Agreements,<br>and<br>•<br>Documented (all the above).<br>Any physical, dental, developmental, psychological, and educational needs must be<br>addressed, and appropriate assessments scheduled within one week from the<br>identification of the need.<br>To facilitate meeting the above, all open In-Home and Permanency Planning<br>Services cases must include, at a minimum, current copies of:<br>•<br>Medical exam(s) current within the last year,<br>•<br>Dental exam(s) current within the last year,<br>•<br>Mental health or substance abuse treatment plan(s) current within the last<br>year if the child has an associated need,<br>•<br>Educational reports (academic and behavior) within the last year if the child<br>is of school age, and/or<br>•<br>Diligent efforts to obtain the above documentation.<br>Requests for this documentation must occur within the first month of ongoing (In-<br>Home and Permanency Planning) cases. Confirmation of these documents and<br>review of the documents, as appropriate, must occur during all updates of Family<br>Services Agreements. |Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|

|•<br>Assessment of trauma and the impact on a child's well-being**. **<br>Child social/cultural/community relationship needs include:<br>•<br>Whether the child has social/community connections and if not, what<br>social/community connections could support the child;<br>•<br>Whether the child is engaged in community (school, church, social groups)<br>activities and if not, identify community activities that the child may<br>benefit from; and<br>•<br>Whether the child has a network for emotional, social, cultural, and/or<br>other needs and if not, how one could be developed.||
|**EARLY INTERVENTION**<br>A referral must be made to the local Children's Developmental Services Agency<br>(CDSA) for early intervention when:<br>•<br>There is the appearance or presence of any of the North Carolina Infant<br>Toddler Program eligibility conditions of "Established Conditions" or<br>"Developmental Delay", or<br>•<br>There is the likelihood that a child has a mild developmental delay in the<br>areas of:<br>`o` Cognitive development;<br>`o` Physical development, including fine and gross motor function;<br>`o` Communication development;<br>`o` Social-emotional development; or<br>`o` Adaptive development.<br>Any child under age three who has been identified as a substance affected infant<br>(SAI) must be screened for referral to the North Carolina Infant Toddler Program<br>(NC ITP) through the local CDSA for early intervention services. ## Child and Family Team (CFT) Meetings

Purpose

Child and Family Teams (CFT) are family members and their community support that come together to create, implement, and update a plan for the child(ren), youth, and family. The purpose of a CFT meeting is to:

• Reach agreement on which identified child welfare issues will be addressed and how they will be addressed throughout the life of the case; • Develop a Family Service Agreement or safety plan that is created using the best ideas of the family, informal, and formal supports that the family believes in, the agency approves of, and lessens risk and heightens safety for the child/youth and family; and

• Plan for how all participants will take part in, support, and implement the Family Service Agreement or safety plan developed by the team.

A CFT meeting is a way to engage and partner with all the people who surround a family and to support the family in building a support network that will eventually sustain it after the case is closed. A CFT meeting is a way for county child welfare agencies to share responsibility for protecting children/youth with their families and the community.

Definition

CFT meetings are structured, guided discussions with the family, their natural supports, and other team members about family strengths, needs, and problems and the impact they have on the safety, permanence, and well-being of the family's child(ren) and youth. The meetings share the following components:

• A clear but open-ended purpose; • An opportunity for the family to be involved in decision-making and planning; • Options for the family to consider and decisions for the family to make; • The family's involvement in the development of specific safety or permanent plans and in the development of services and supports; and • The outcome of the meeting will be reflected in the development or revision of a Family Services Agreement or a safety plan.

The primary focus must always be the safety and well-being of the child(ren) and youth.

A meeting is not a CFT meeting:

• When a decision or plan has been made and there is no room for input from family and natural supports; • When the family (either biological or fictive) are not present; and • When the goal of the meeting is primarily information gathering, rather than case planning. While these fact-finding meetings are important and useful, they are not CFT meetings, and they may hinder the family's trust in county child welfare staff and services and ultimately negatively affect child welfare outcomes.

## Parent Engagement (including Non-Resident Parents) and Needs Assessment

Policy

Efforts to locate and engage all maternal and all paternal parents must occur during all phases of child welfare, unless the court has terminated parental rights.

Definitions

Birth Parent: genetic, biological, or natural parents.

Residential Parent: In child welfare it is the parent with whom the child(ren) primarily resides.

See Juvenile Court for definitions regarding juvenile court. Also, refer to the course "Legal Aspects of Child Welfare in North Carolina" for information regarding both NC Statute Chapter 7B and North Carolina Administrative Code 10 N.C.A.C.70A through 10 N.C.A.C.70O for the rules most relevant to child welfare.

Keep in mind that definitions in North Carolina statute can vary from statute to statute, depending on the context with which the term is applied. For example, in North Carolina guardianship law, Chapter 35A, the clerk of the court will decide if the person is incompetent and requires a guardian. This is a different context of the term guardianship than what is used in juvenile court in awarding guardianship. For this reason, county child welfare workers must be careful in their use of terminology and consult with their county attorney as needed. During provision of Permanency Planning Services, Chapter 7B should be the primary statute for consideration. During provision of CPS-A or In-Home Services, county child welfare workers may need to refer to Chapter 50 regarding child custody and/or domestic violence court.

# Parent Engagement (including Non-Resident Parents) and Needs Assessment

PATERNITY

All the following information comes from Child Support Services Manual and is provided as information only. Child Support policy and Child Welfare policy (and Juvenile Court) vary on key aspects, especially in defining when paternity testing is required. When collaborating with Child Support Services, this information may be of value.

ESTABLISHING PATERNITY If paternity has not been determined (either voluntarily or by court order) and no judicial action to establish paternity has been filed with the court, paternity testing can be initiated by agreement of the parties involved.

Testing by agreement is NOT appropriate if:

• An Affidavit of Parentage has been signed and has not been rescinded;

• A judicial action for paternity has been filed; or

• A court order of paternity has been entered.

For more information, specifically regarding establishment of paternity and paternity testing, see also CSS Paternity Policy.

PATERNITY TESTING RESULTS These results have the following effect if ordered under N.C.G.S. § 8-50.1:

• Probability of paternity is less than eighty-five percent (85%) - The alleged father is presumed NOT to be the parent.

• Probability of paternity is between eighty-five (85%) and ninety-seven percent (97%) - The results have same weight as other evidence.

• Probability of paternity is ninety-seven percent (97%) or higher - The alleged father is presumed to be the parent.

USING PREVIOUSLY COLLECTED TEST SAMPLES Paternity test samples and test results for individuals who are tested under the state contract that are maintained by the testing laboratory can be used in subsequent testing conducted under this contract. Rather than collecting new samples, using existing samples or results can reduce the time and cost of subsequent testing. DNA samples or results obtained for testing under the state contract can be reused for subsequent tests, including:

• New testing for the same mother/ child/ father group. • Testing of an individual in a different mother/ child/ father group.

• Testing that was conducted by the county which requested the initial test sample.

• Testing that was conducted by a different county than the initial requesting county.

Page 253 Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

<!-- page: 257 -->
# Parent Engagement (including Non-Resident Parents) and Needs Assessment

EXCLUSION OF ALLEGED FATHER Paternity testing that results in the exclusion of a man as the biological father of a child does not constitute a legal determination of non-paternity. However, test results are evidence that the court can consider in making such a determination.

If paternity testing excludes the alleged father as the biological father of a child, the appropriate course of action is based on whether a judicial or voluntary determination of paternity has been made, a marital presumption of paternity exists, or the child has no legally responsible father.

If a judicial or voluntary determination of paternity has been entered:

• Exclusionary test results do not void that determination.

• Test results can be used as evidence in a motion or independent action to disestablish paternity.

• The paternity determination remains in effect until a court makes a ruling.

If a marital presumption of paternity exists:

• Exclusionary test results do not invalidate the presumption of paternity.

• Test results can be presented as evidence in a judicial challenge of the presumption by the legal father.

• The presumption of paternity remains in effect until a court makes a ruling.

## Identifying, Locating, & Engaging Extended Family Members

Legal Basis

42 U.S. Code § 671(a)(19) provides that the state shall consider giving preference to an adult relative over a non-related caregiver when determining a placement for a child(ren), provided that the relative caregiver meets all relevant state child protection standards.

42 U.S. Code § 671(a)(19) provides that, within 30 days after the removal of a child from the custody of the parent or parents of the child, the state shall exercise due diligence to identify and provide notice to the following relatives: all adult grandparents, all parents of a sibling of the child, where such parent has legal custody of such sibling, and other adult relatives of the child (including any other adult relatives suggested by the parents), subject to exceptions due to family or domestic violence, that:

a. Specifies that the child has been or is being removed from the custody of the parent or parents of the child;
b. Explains the options the relative has under federal, state, and local law to participate in the care and placement of the child, including any

options that may be lost by failing to respond to the notice;

c. Describes the requirements to become a foster family home and the additional services and supports that are available for children placed in

such a home; and

d. If the state has elected the option to make kinship guardianship assistance payments, describes how the relative guardian of the child may

subsequently enter into an agreement with the state to receive the payments.

N.C.G.S. §7B-505, Requirements for Placement with Relatives:

(b) The court shall order the department to make diligent efforts to notify relatives and other persons with legal custody of a sibling of the

juvenile that the juvenile is in non-secure custody and of any hearings scheduled to occur pursuant to G.S. 7B-506, unless the court finds the notification would be contrary to the best interests of the juvenile. In placing a juvenile in non-secure custody under this section, the court shall first consider whether a relative of the juvenile is willing and able to provide proper care and supervision of the juvenile in a safe home. If the court finds that the relative is willing and able to provide proper care and supervision in a safe home, then the court shall order placement of the juvenile with the relative unless the court finds that placement with the relative would be contrary to the best interests of the juvenile. (c) If the court does not place the juvenile with a relative, the court may consider whether nonrelative kin or other persons with legal custody of

a sibling of the juvenile are willing and able to provide proper care and supervision of the juvenile in a safe home. The court may order the department to notify the juvenile's state-recognized tribe of the need for non-secure custody for the purpose of locating relatives or nonrelative kin for placement. The court may order placement of the juvenile with nonrelative kin if the court finds the placement is in the juvenile's best interests.

Page 255 Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

<!-- page: 259 -->
## Identifying, Locating, & Engaging Extended Family Members

Legal Basis

N.C.G.S. §7B-903 Requirements for Placement with Relatives

(a1) In placing a juvenile in out-of-home care under this section, the court shall first consider whether a relative of the juvenile is willing and able

to provide proper care and supervision of the juvenile in a safe home. If the court finds that the relative is willing and able to provide proper care and supervision in a safe home, then the court shall order placement of the juvenile with the relative unless the court finds that the placement is contrary to the best interests of the juvenile.

N.C.G.S. § 7B-505(b) states "The department of social services shall use due diligence to identify and notify adult relatives and other persons with legal

custody of a sibling of the juvenile within 30 days after the initial order removing custody. The department shall file with the court information regarding attempts made to identify and notify adult relatives of the juvenile and persons with legal custody of a sibling of the juvenile."

|Protocol|Guidance|
|---|---|
|County child welfare agencies must makediligent<br>effortsto identify and locate extended maternal and<br>paternal family members as soon as a county child<br>welfare services agency becomes involved with a<br>child/youth and continue throughout the case.<br>Relative is defined as an individual directly related<br>to the juvenile by blood, marriage, or adoption. This<br>includes but is not limited to the following<br>examples: a grandparent, sibling, aunt, or uncle.<br>Adoptive parents of siblings are not considered<br>relatives of the legal child; however, they must be<br>considered in the identification and notification of<br>individuals who have legal custody of a sibling.|County child welfare workers should consider their beliefs on what is possible for youth<br>through connections with extended family members. Finding extended family members<br>encourages workers to view case planning to address safety, risk, and permanence beyond one<br>route.<br>Identification- Requirements to Find and Locate<br>The goal of identification of an extended family member or other "kin" is to promote<br>connections for child(ren)ren/youth and to create more options for support and planning for<br>the family, parent(s) or child(ren). Techniques to use include:<br>•<br>Record Review: Closely review case record to identify and record names;<br>•<br>Interview all known family members, maternal and paternal, including child(ren),<br>and fictive kin and/or close friends; and<br>•<br>Internet and social media searches:<br>`o` www.Zabasearch.com<br>`o` www.msn.com(White Pages)<br>`o` www.USSEARCH.com|

# Identifying, Locating, & Engaging Extended Family Members

|Protocol|Guidance|
|---|---|
|At least once a month throughout the case, county<br>child welfare workers must inquire with parents and<br>child(ren)ren about extended family members to<br>include:<br>•<br>Knowledge of (names, when last seen);<br>•<br>Location (address, contact information);<br>•<br>Contact with (telephone, Facebook, etc.); or<br>•<br>Relationships (history with that relative,<br>support that relative may be able to<br>provide, etc.).<br>For states to meet the requirements of IV-E federal<br>funding for foster care and adoption assistance,<br>states must "consider giving preference to an adult<br>relative over a nonrelated caregiver when<br>determining placement for a child(ren), provided<br>that the relative caregiver meets all relevant state<br>child protection standards." IV-E requires states to<br>apply due diligence to identify and provide notice to<br>all grandparents and other adult relatives of the<br>child(ren) regarding:<br>• The fact that the child(ren) has been or is<br>being removed from the custody of their<br>parents;<br>• The options the relative available to<br>participate in the care and placement of the<br>child(ren); and<br>• The requirements to become a foster parent<br>to the child(ren).|`o `https://www.facebook.com/<br>**RECONNECTING WITH A RELATIVE**<br>Consideration must be given to the impact of any new or renewed connection to an extended<br>relative. Although this connection is in general a positive event, there may be other<br>extenuating circumstances that should be understood, including the following:<br>a. Families have a primary need to know what happened to "lost" relatives. While youth<br>in foster care are not considered to be lost child(ren), the child(ren), their relatives, or<br>individuals who had a close relationship to the child(ren) continue to have a desire to<br>know how their loved ones are doing. A majority of youth who've been adopted report<br>a desire to find or be found by their birth parents and/or other extended family<br>members.<br>b. Identification and location of extended family won't solve the psychological problems<br>that can affect youth in foster care. Being in foster care has a life-altering effect on<br>youth and families. The loss of connections, disruption to the life cycle, and number of<br>transitions can be very traumatic even to resilient child(ren)/youth. Maintaining or<br>reestablishing family connections may provide support to help youth and families heal.<br>c. The process of locating and engaging families can open family wounds, rekindling the<br>problems surrounding the child(ren)'s birth or removal. Numerous issues and<br>questions may arise. It is the role of the county child welfare services agency to<br>facilitate all contact to avoid situations involving blame, to ensure that all interaction<br>acknowledges the youth's current situation and need for wraparound support.<br>d. Each person, conservatively, has about 100-300 living relatives.<br>e. Permanence for youth in care is more than a legal goal. It involves the stability<br>and continuity of relationships that are meaningful to individuals. Permanence<br>incorporates a sense of belonging and cultural and social connections to a<br>child(ren)/youth's background and permanent home.|

# Special Legal Considerations (MEPA, ICWA, Mexican Heritage)

MULTIETHNIC PLACEMENT ACT

|Policy and Definitions|Legal Basis|
|---|---|
|All state and county agencies that use federal funds must comply with the<br>Multiethnic Placement Act (MEPA) of 1994 as amended in 1996 by the<br>Interethnic Adoption Provisions (MEPA-IEP).<br>The Act prohibits states or agencies that receive federal funds from<br>delaying or denying the placement of any child for adoption or into foster<br>care based on the race, color, or national origin of the individual, or the<br>child involved. Therefore, failing to place a child with a prospective parent<br>because the parent is a specific, or different race, color, or national origin<br>from that of the child; or removing a child from a prospective parent<br>because the parent or child is a specific race, color or national origin is a<br>direct violation of MEPA-IEP. The Act also prohibits denying any individual<br>the opportunity to become an adoptive or foster parent, on the basis of<br>the race, color, or national origin of the individual, or of the child<br>involved. Therefore, training shall be offered to all individuals in<br>consideration of foster home licensure.<br>Agencies are prohibited from acting on a child or parent's preference for<br>a same race placement. Any consideration of race, color, or national<br>origin must be done in the context of individualized needs of the child<br>and must advance the best interest of the child, with the rationale<br>specifically documented in the placement record.<br>All state and county agencies using federal Title IV-E funds must comply<br>with MEPA-IEP. This includes contract and sub-recipient child-placing<br>agencies who receive federal funding for the placement of child in foster<br>care.|MEPA<br>MEPA is designed to "prevent discrimination in the placement of<br>child(ren) in foster care and adoption on the basis of race, color, or<br>national origin; decrease the length of time that child(ren) wait to be<br>adopted; and facilitate the identification and recruitment of foster and<br>adoptive parents." The best strategy for full compliance with the MEPA is<br>a comprehensive recruitment strategy that targets the general public and<br>also specifically targets those communities that reflect the racial and<br>ethnic diversity of your foster care population.<br>An agency may not rely on generalizations about the needs of child(ren)<br>of a particular race or ethnicity, or on generalizations about the abilities<br>of prospective parents of one race or ethnicity to care for a child(ren) of<br>another race or ethnicity. Any violation of MEPA-IEP will be deemed a<br>violation ofTitle VI of the Civil Rights Act.|

Page 259 Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

<!-- page: 263 -->
# Special Legal Considerations (MEPA, ICWA, Mexican Heritage)

Protocol - What you must do

### MEPA-IEP PLACEMENT REQUIREMENTS (INITIAL AND PLACEMENT CHANGES)

Every agency must have a diligent recruitment and retention plan to comply with MEPA-IEP. The major thrust of MEPA-IEP's recruitment requirements is that agencies provide for the diligent recruitment of potential foster and adoptive families that reflect the ethnic and racial diversity of children in the state or county for whom foster and adoptive homes are needed. Federal guidelines specifically call for a thorough diligent recruitment and retention plan that includes both general and targeted campaigns and encompasses the following features:

• A description of the characteristics of waiting children (i.e., age, race, time in care, special needs, etc.); • Specific strategies to reach all community members through a general media campaign such as radio, television, and print;

• Diverse methods of disseminating both general and child-specific information to targeted communities through community organizations such as churches and other religious institutions.

• Strategies for assuring that all prospective foster and adoptive families throughout the community have equitable access to the home study process, including location and hours of services that facilitate access by all members of the community.

• Strategies for training foster and adoptive staff in cultural, racial and economic diversity. • Strategies for dealing with linguistic barriers.

• Assurance of non-discriminatory fee structures.

• Procedures ensuring a timely search for prospective parents awaiting a child, including the use of exchanges and other inter-agency efforts, provided that such procedures ensure that the placement of a child in an appropriate household is not delayed by the search for a same race or ethnic placement.

Counties are no longer responsible for completing and sending NCDSS individual DRR plans during the transition to a regional model.

# Special Legal Considerations (MEPA, ICWA, Mexican Heritage)

INDIAN CHILD WELFARE ACT OF 1978 (ICWA)

|Policy|Legal Basis|
|---|---|
|The Indian Child Welfare Act (ICWA) of<br>1978 established nationwide procedures<br>for the handling of Indian child placements<br>and authorized the establishment of<br>Indian child and family service programs.<br>The act requires specific actions on behalf<br>of a child who is a member of a federally<br>recognized Indian Tribe, Aleuts, or<br>members of certain native Alaskan<br>villagers. Whenever it is suspected that a<br>child may fit into any of these populations,<br>the procedures outlined in this Act must<br>be followed.<br>Nothing in ICWA is to be construed as<br>preventing the emergency removal of an<br>Indian child to prevent imminent physical<br>damage or harm to that child(ren). If a<br>county child welfare worker believes that<br>a child(ren) is in imminent danger, the<br>same procedures are followed as in any<br>other emergency removal.|ICWA specifies that tribal courts have exclusive jurisdiction of child(ren) who reside on the reservation. If<br>the child(ren) is a ward of a tribal court, but does not reside on a reservation, the jurisdiction of the case<br>must be transferred to the tribal court. In any action leading to a foster care placement or in any<br>termination of parental rights action affecting an American Indian child who does not reside on the<br>reservation, the parents, guardian or custodian of the child(ren) may petition for transfer of jurisdiction<br>to a tribal court.<br>At any time during proceedings of a foster care placement the American Indian custodian and American<br>Indian tribe have the right to intervene in the proceedings at any time. Tribal courts have the same<br>authority as any state court and any decisions made by them that follow the ICWA guidelines have the<br>effect of any other court decision.<br>N.C.G.S. § 7B-505(c)<br>(http://www.ncleg.net/EnactedLegislation/Statutes/HTML/BySection/Chapter_7B/GS_7B-505.html) was<br>added in 2013 to provide changes to the placement of child(ren) while in non-secure custody. It enacted<br>a new subsection to expand types of placements available to a child(ren) in non-secure custody by<br>identifying individuals who may not be relatives but have a substantial relationship with the child(ren).<br>These individuals are defined as "non-relative kin". It also gives additional placement options for<br>American Indian child(ren) who are members of a state recognized tribe by defining non-relative kin to<br>include members of state or federally recognized tribes regardless of the relationship with the child(ren).<br>One purpose of this change is to allow placement of child(ren) from state recognized tribes with<br>American Indian families when in their best interest, given that state tribes are not protected by ICWA.<br>This change also encourages these placements be made at the onset of the case and only when the<br>placement is in the child(ren)'s best interest.<br>The Act does not apply to a placement based on an act which, if committed by an adult would be<br>deemed a crime (as in any situation in which a child was adjudicated delinquent and placed in foster care<br>or a group home), or upon an award, in a divorce proceeding, of custody to one of the parents.<br>MEPA-IEP specifically provides that it has no effect on theIndian Child Welfare Act of 1978.|

|Indian: An Indian is defined as any person who is a member of an Indian tribe, or who is an<br>Alaskan Native and a member of a Regional Corporation, as defined in the Alaska Native<br>Claims Settlement Act.<br>Indian Child: An Indian child means any unmarried person who is under 18 and is either (a) a<br>member of an Indian tribe; or (b) is eligible for membership in an Indian tribe and is the<br>biological child of a member of an Indian tribe. Tribes determine their own standards for<br>membership eligibility.<br>Indian Tribe: Any Indian tribe, band, nation, or other organized group of Indians recognized<br>as eligible for the services provided to Indians by the Secretary of the Interior because of<br>their status as Indians, including any Alaskan Native villager as defined in section 3(c) of the<br>Alaska Native Claims Settlement Act.<br>Indian Child's Tribe: An Indian child's tribe is defined as (a) the Indian tribe in which an<br>Indian child is a member or eligible for membership or (b) in the case of an Indian child who<br>is a member of or eligible for membership in more than one tribe, the Indian tribe with<br>which the Indian child has the most significant contacts.<br>Indian Reservation: Indian country as defined in Section 1151 of Title 18, United States Code<br>and any lands, not covered under such section, title to which is either held by the United<br>States in trust for any Indian tribe or individual subject to a restriction by the United States<br>against alienation.<br>The Act applies to Indian child custody proceedings and includes:<br>•<br>County child welfare custody, when the parent or custodian cannot have the child<br>returned on demand (as in Voluntary Placement Agreements), but where parental<br>rights have not been terminated;<br>•<br>In termination of parental rights proceedings;<br>•<br>In pre-adoptive and adoptive placements; and<br>•<br>Proceedings regarding juvenile court assigned custody or guardianship of the<br>person of the juvenile.|Cherokee Family Support Services is the agency of the<br>Eastern Band of the Cherokee that handles the cases<br>that involve the Indian Child Welfare act.<br>If there is belief that the child(ren) is a Cherokee Indian<br>child, the county child welfare services agency can<br>contact Cherokee Family Support Services at P.O. The notice must include the following information:<br>•<br>The name of the Indian child(ren) and tribal affiliation;<br>•<br>Name and address of the petitioner and petitioner's attorney;<br>•<br>Location, mailing address, and telephone number of the court;<br>•<br>Statement of the right of the Indian custodian and tribe to intervene and petition<br>for transfer to tribal court;<br>•<br>Statement that if the parent or Indian custodian is unable to afford counsel, the<br>court will appoint counsel;<br>•<br>Statement that the parent, custodian, or tribe may request 20 days to prepare for<br>the proceeding;<br>•<br>Statement of the potential legal consequences of an adjudication on future<br>custodial rights of the parent or Indian custodian; and|In any action leading to a child(ren) entering the<br>custody of a county child welfare services agency or in<br>any termination of parental rights action affecting an<br>Indian child(ren) who does not reside on the<br>reservation, the parents, guardian, or custodian of the<br>child(ren) may petition for transfer of jurisdiction to a<br>tribal court.<br>During proceedings of a Permanency Planning case, the<br>Indian custodian and Indian tribe have the right to<br>intervene in the proceedings at any time. Tribal courts<br>have the same authority as any state court and any<br>decisions made by them that follow the ICWA<br>guidelines have the effect of any other court decision.<br>Parents and Indian custodians have the right to a court<br>appointed lawyer in custody proceedings whenever<br>indigence is a factor; the court may also appoint an<br>attorney for the child to ensure their interests are<br>protected.|

# Special Legal Considerations (MEPA, ICWA, Mexican Heritage)

MEXICAN HERITAGE

Legal Basis

A Memorandum of Agreement was established on March 30, 2017 between the Consulate General of Mexico in North Carolina and the Government of the State of North Carolina through the Department of Health and Human Services, Division of Social Services. This agreement recognizes the significance of preserving the cultural traditions and values of child(ren) with Mexican heritage. The purpose of this agreement is to ensure that child(ren) and their families are afforded the opportunity to receive necessary services beneficial to them. The services guarantee the protection offered by the Vienna Convention, Bilateral Convention, and all other applicable treaties and laws. This agreement provides specific details for child welfare agencies when considering securing custody of a child who has Mexican heritage. It is imperative that the identification of Mexican heritage is explored throughout the longevity of the case.

|Protocol|Guidance|
|---|---|
|Upon assuming legal custody of a child, county child welfare agencies must inquire as to<br>whether the child has any Mexican parentage.<br>Ongoing efforts to identify Mexican parentage must continue throughout the life of the case.||
|NOTIFICATION TO THE MEXICAN CONSULATE<br>County child welfare agencies must notify the Mexican Consulate in writing of the following:<br>•<br>When the county child welfare services agency identifies a Mexican minor in its<br>custody; or<br>•<br>When a parent or custodian of the Mexican minor requests that the Mexican<br>Consulate be notified.<br>The written notification must be made within 10 working days of the minor entering agency<br>custody. If the county child welfare services agency learns later that the juvenile has Mexican<br>parentage, notification must be sent without delay to the appropriate parties.<br>County child welfare agencies must notify the Mexican Consulate and provide additional<br>information:<br>•<br>When a parent or custodian of a Mexican American minor has requested that the<br>Consulate be notified; or|Counties should provide notice to the Mexican<br>Consulate regarding court hearings involving Mexican<br>minors, so the Consulate may attend these hearings.|

## Documentation

Definitions

Documentation: Case documentation is comprised of all information in the case file. Documentation is critical in child welfare work as it establishes the basis for all decision making, including the critical decision to file a petition for removal of a child(ren) from their parent's care. Documentation includes, but is not limited to:

• Narrative (written by county child welfare worker to capture actions and activities completed)

• North Carolina child welfare forms and other forms. Examples include but are not limited to:

o Intake form (DSS-1402) o Risk Assessment (DSS-5230) o Assessment (DSS-5010) with case decision o Family Assessment of Strengths & Needs (DSS-5229) o Safety Assessment (DSS-5231) o In-Home Services Home Visit Record o Monthly Permanency Planning Contact Record (DSS-5295) (5236)

• Documents from service providers and collaterals. Examples include are but not limited to:

o Criminal reports o Medical records o School records o Treatment plans

• Court reports and court orders.

Case File: Includes all case documentation and provides a way to manage and organize the documentation.

Narrative: The case notes written by the county child welfare worker to describe activities and actions performed on a case.

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|Documentation is critical in child welfare<br>work.<br>Documentation, starting at the point where<br>a report is received, must include but is not<br>limited to:|**CASE FILES**<br>The county child welfare services agency should develop a consistent organizational format to be used<br>in all cases. A consistent, organized format allows the county child welfare worker to locate necessary<br>information readily; helps new county child welfare workers become familiar with their assigned cases<br>more quickly; and enables child welfare supervisors to review cases more easily.|

County child welfare workers' subjective thoughts or reactions do not belong in the case<br>narrative.<br>Assessment and/or Decision Narrative<br>During many interactions with a family, the county child welfare worker will have to assess the<br>situation to determine if action is required. One of the most important aspects of the county child<br>welfare worker's job is to monitor family situations and assess the safety and/or risk to child(ren).<br>Assessment should be based on professional knowledge and experience, not opinion. Referring to the<br>case example above, the worker will assess if the child(ren) are at risk in the care of Mr. Jones who<br>appears to be under the influence of alcohol. Several factors will be considered (age and maturity of<br>the child(ren), the presence of anyone else in the home or due home shortly, the history regarding Mr.<br>Jones's actions when under the influence, etc.).<br>Assessment or decision narrative is captured:<br>•<br>When a situation requires an immediate response and<br>•<br>For decisions made in meetings or during case staffing with the agency supervisor.<br>The case narrative about a case staffing should cover what decisions were made; who was involved in<br>the decision making; what information, condition, or factors the decision was based on; and the basis<br>for all decisions. When decisions are made during a CFT meeting, the same information will be<br>documented within the form used to facilitate and document the meeting; additional case narrative<br>may not be needed.<br>North Carolina has developed forms that can be used in place of case narrative to support assessment<br>and decision making, including but not limited to:<br>•<br>Safety Assessment (DSS-5231)<br>•<br>Permanency Planning Review (DSS-5241)<br>•<br>CFT Safety Planning form (pre-petition form)<br>•<br>Case decision portion of the Assessment Documentation tool (DSS-5010)|

# Human Trafficking

|Policy|Legal Basis|
|---|---|
|A child who is sold, traded, or exchanged for sex or labor is an abused<br>and neglected juvenile, regardless of the relationship between the<br>victim and the perpetrator.<br>Child welfare agencies must identify, document in case records, and<br>determine appropriate services for child(ren) and youth who are<br>believed to be, or at risk of being, victims of human trafficking.<br>**Required Notifications and Verifications**<br>Please refer to Intake, Assessments, In-Home, and Permanency<br>Planning manuals for instructions related to required notifications,<br>verifications, and screening tools for missing, runaway, and abducted<br>children.|Public Law 106-386 Victims of Trafficking and Violence Protection Act of<br>2000, Division A- Trafficking Victims' Protection Act (TVPA) of 2000 defines<br>severe forms of trafficking in persons and commercial sex act and outlines<br>U.S. Government efforts to protect victims of trafficking.<br>Public Law 113-183- Preventing Sex Trafficking and Strengthening Families<br>Actincludes provisions to improve identification of and assistance for<br>child(ren) who are victims or at risk of trafficking, system response for<br>runaways from foster care, and normalcy for youth in foster care.<br>Public Law 114-22- Justice for Victims of Trafficking Actamended the<br>definition of "child abuse" under the Victims of Child Abuse Act of 1990 to<br>include human trafficking and the production of child pornography;<br>amended the Child Abuse Prevention and Treatment Act to require states<br>to develop and implement protocols and procedures to identify and assess<br>reports involving child(ren) known or suspected to be victims of human<br>trafficking; and clarified that a child is considered to be a victim of "child<br>abuse and neglect" and of "sexual abuse" if the child is identified by a state<br>or local agency as being a victim of sex trafficking or victim of severe forms<br>of trafficking in persons.<br>Public Law 117-348- Trafficking Victims Prevention and Protection<br>Reauthorization Act of 22reauthorizes and amends the Trafficking Victims<br>Protection Act (TVPA). Amendments include additional communication and<br>details when title IV-E agencies report missing or abducted children to law<br>enforcement and the National Center for Missing and Exploited Children<br>(NCMEC).<br>N.S.G.S. §7B-101provides the definitions of an abused or neglected<br>juvenile to determine a county child welfare services agency's authority to<br>intervene.<br>N.C.G.S. Chapter 14 Article 10A Human Traffickingdefines what actions<br>by an offender equate to criminal human trafficking.|

## Human Trafficking

Definitions

Federal Law

The Trafficking Victims Protection Act (22 U.S.C. 7102) defines

"severe forms of trafficking in persons":

• Sex trafficking in which a commercial sex act is induced by force, fraud, or coercion or in which the person induced to perform such an act has not attained 18 years of age; or • The recruitment, harboring, transportation, provision, or obtaining of a person for labor or services using force, fraud, or coercion for subjection to involuntary servitude, peonage, debt bondage, or slavery.

"commercial sex act" as any sex act because of which anything of value is given to or received by any person.

State Law

N.C. G.S. 14-43.11 Human Trafficking

A person commits the offense of human trafficking when that person (i) knowingly or in reckless disregard of the consequences of the action recruits, harbors, transports, provides, or obtains by any means another person with the intent that the other person be held in involuntary servitude or sexual servitude or (ii) willfully or in reckless disregard of the consequences of the action causes a minor to be held in involuntary servitude or sexual servitude.

N.C. G.S. 14-43.10(a)(3) Involuntary Servitude - The term includes the following:

• The performance of labor, whether for compensation, or whether or not for the satisfaction of a debt; and • By deception, coercion, or intimidation using violence or the threat of violence or by any other means of coercion or intimidation.

N.C. G.S. 14-43.10(a)(5) Sexual Servitude - The term includes the following:

• Any sexual activity as defined in G.S. 14-190.13 for which anything of value is directly or indirectly given, promised to, or received by any person, which conduct is induced or obtained by coercion or deception or which conduct is induced or obtained from a person under the age of 18 years; or • Any sexual activity as defined in G.S. 14-190.13 that is performed or provided by any person, which conduct is induced or obtained by coercion or deception or which conduct is induced or obtained from a person under the age of 18 years.

N.C.G.S. 7B-101(1) Abused Juveniles.

Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

<!-- page: 282 -->
## Human Trafficking

Definitions

Any juvenile less than 18 years of age (i) who is found to be a minor victim of human trafficking under G.S. 14-43.15 or (ii) whose parent, guardian, custodian, or caretaker: (a.) Inflicts or allows to be inflicted upon the juvenile a serious physical injury by other than accidental means; (b.) Creates or allows to be created a substantial risk of serious physical injury to the juvenile by other than accidental means; (c.) Uses or allows to be used upon the juvenile cruel or grossly inappropriate procedures or cruel or grossly inappropriate devices to modify

behavior; (d.) Commits, permits, or encourages the commission of a violation of the following laws by, with, or upon the juvenile; first degree rape, as

provided in N.C.G.S. §14-27.2; rape of a child by an adult offender, as provided in N.C.G.S. §14- 27.2A; second degree rape as provided in N.C.G.S. §14-27.3; first degree sexual offense, as provided in N.C.G.S. §14-27.4; sexual offense with a child by an adult offender, as provided in N.C.G.S. §14-27.4A; second degree sexual offense, as provided in N.C.G.S. §14-27.5; intercourse and sexual offenses with certain victims; consent no defense, as provided in N.C.G.S. §14-27.31 and N.C.G.S. §14- 27.32; unlawful sale, surrender, or purchase of a minor, as provided in N.C.G.S. §14-43.14; crime against nature, as provided in N.C.G.S. §14-177; incest, as provided in N.C.G.S. §14-178 and N.C.G.S. §14-179; preparation of obscene photographs, slides, or motion pictures of the juvenile, as provided in N.C.G.S. §14-190.5; employing or permitting the juvenile to assist in a violation of the obscenity laws as provided in N.C.G.S. §14-190.6; dissemination of obscene material to the juvenile as provided in N.C.G.S. §14-190.7 and N.C.G.S. §14-190.8; displaying or disseminating material harmful to the juvenile as provided in N.C.G.S. §14-190.14 and N.C.G.S. §14-190.15; first and second degree sexual exploitation of the juvenile as provided in N.C.G.S. §14-190.16 and N.C.G.S. §14-190.17; promoting the prostitution of the juvenile as provided in N.C.G.S. §14- 205.3(b); and taking indecent liberties with the juvenile, as provided in N.C.G.S. §14-202.1, regardless of the age of the parties; or (e.) Creates or allows to be created serious emotional damage to the juvenile. Serious emotional damage is evidenced by a juvenile's severe

anxiety, depression, withdrawal, or aggressive behavior toward himself or others; (f.) Encourages, directs, or approves of delinquent acts involving moral turpitude committed by the juvenile; or (g.) Commits or allows to be committed an offense under N.C.G.S. §14-43.11 (human trafficking), N.C.G.S. §14-43.12 (involuntary servitude),

or N.C.G.S. §14-43.13 (sexual servitude) against the child.

N.C.G.S. 7B-101(15) Neglected Juvenile.

Any juvenile less than 18 years of age (i) who is found to be a minor victim of human trafficking under G.S. 14-43.15 or (ii) whose parent, guardian, custodian, or caretaker does not provide proper care, supervision, or discipline, or who has been abandoned; or who is not provided necessary medical care; or who is not provided necessary remedial care; or who lives in an environment injurious to the juvenile's welfare; or who has been placed for care or adoption in violation of the law. In determining whether a juvenile is a neglected juvenile, it is relevant whether that juvenile lives in a home where another juvenile has died because of suspected abuse or neglect or lives in a home where another juvenile has been subjected to abuse by an adult who regularly lives in the home.

# Human Trafficking

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
||There may be times when it is not clear that a child is victim of human trafficking.<br>County child welfare workers must be aware of the known risk factors and<br>indicators of human trafficking.<br>Risk factors:<br>•<br>History of running away or getting kicked out of home;<br>•<br>History of homelessness or housing instability;<br>•<br>History of sexual abuse;<br>•<br>History of physical abuse;<br>•<br>History of sexual offense;<br>•<br>History of delinquent or reckless behavior (involvement with law enforcement<br>or juvenile justice);<br>•<br>History of neglect or basic needs not having been met;<br>•<br>History of alcohol or substance use disorder;<br>•<br>Current or past involvement in the child welfare system;<br>•<br>History of depression/mood disorder;<br>•<br>Exposure to domestic violence;<br>•<br>Family instability;<br>•<br>Excessive absences from school<br>•<br>Identifies as lesbian, gay, bisexual, or transgender (LGBT);<br>•<br>Has disabilities, especially intellectual disability;|

# Human Trafficking

|Safety Considerations<br>County child welfare workers must collaborate with human<br>trafficking victim organizations and advocates to address the<br>unique circumstances and safety issues for child(ren) who are<br>victims of human trafficking.<br>Determining and Utilizing Appropriate Resources<br>When a county child welfare services agency has an open CPS-<br>A, CPS In-Home Services, or Permanency Planning case where<br>trafficking of the child(ren) is suspected or confirmed, the<br>county child welfare worker must provide appropriate|• Immigration status;<br>• Poverty;<br>• Unemployment;<br>• Lack of transportation.<br>Indicators:<br>• Visible signs of abuse such as unexplained bruises, cuts, marks;<br>• Fear of person accompanying them;<br>• Wearing new clothes of any style or getting hair or nails done with no financial<br>means to do this independently;<br>• Exhibits hyper-vigilance or paranoid behavior;<br>• A young person with a tattoo which he or she is reluctant to explain;<br>• Frequent or multiple sexually transmitted diseases, STIs, or pregnancies;<br>• Truancy or tardiness from school; and<br>• Unaccounted for times, vagueness concerning whereabouts, and/or<br>defensiveness in response to questions or concerns.<br>The following risk factors, indicators, and vulnerabilities should be considered for<br>foreign nationals:<br>• History of trauma, including civil unrest or prolonged community violence;<br>• Social isolation; and<br>• Lack of legal status (documentation).<br>These lists may not be inclusive of all risk factors, indicators, and vulnerabilities.<br>Immediate safety issues may include, but are not limited to:<br>• Access of the trafficker to the child(ren);<br>• Child(ren) or youth's lack of safe housing or a safe place to stay;<br>• Safety issues in the home of the parent, guardian, custodian, or caretaker;<br>and<br>• Risk of child(ren) or youth running away.|

# Enhanced Practice for Working with Special Populations

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|INTRODUCTION<br>The effective child protective services social worker must approach every situation with sensitivity to those physical, emotional,<br>cultural, or environmental factors that make children more vulnerable to abuse or less able to communicate their fears. For the<br>purposes of this section, the term "special populations" refers to children and families who are at greater risk because of these factors.<br>The social worker has an ethical and professional responsibility to recognize their own attitudes and prejudices regarding mental and<br>physical disability, race, culture, sexual orientation, religious beliefs, economic status, homelessness, marital status, and other highly<br>charged beliefs. It is impossible to grow up in a culture without such beliefs. Failure to recognize one's own perspective can lead to<br>inaccuracy in perception and, thus, to incorrect assessments.<br>It is not the intent of this section to provide exhaustive information about each group named, nor to suggest that the identified<br>populations are an all-inclusive listing. This section is designed to refresh the knowledge of veteran staff, to increase the awareness of<br>newer social workers, and to provide direction for further study.<br>Effective interviewing strategies and techniques shall be used which are appropriate to the child's developmental level.<br>Documentation, including observations of the child, shall explain any inability to interview the child.<br>When a child is alleged to have a medical condition, disease, or illness relevant to the allegation, the agency shall consult the medical<br>provider treating the condition. This consultation shall be focused on determining the family's assertions about that medical condition,<br>or there shall be justification for why this was not done. Medical and psychological resources, such as the Child Medical Evaluation<br>Program (CMEP) be utilized, as appropriate, in the assessment of alleged victims of neglect and/or physical, sexual, or emotional abuse.<br>A Child Medical Evaluation (CME) or Clinical Assessment of Protective Parenting (CAPP) should be considered if the social worker has<br>questions about any of the following issues (this list is not intended to be all inclusive).<br>THE IMPACT OF DEVELOPMENTAL DISABILITY1<br>Developmental disabilities are physical or mental conditions that become apparent before the age of twenty-two, continue throughout<br>the individual's lifetime, and cause significant impairment in daily functioning. This term includes conditions such as<br>intellectual/developmental disability,||

1 Joann Grayson, PhD., "Child Abuse and Developmental Disabilities," Virginia Child Protection Newsletter, Volume 37, Fall, 1992.

Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

# Enhanced Practice for Working with Special Populations

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|

|motor dysfunction, vision/hearing impairments, learning disabilities, communication problems, severe emotional disturbance,<br>neurological impairments, and chronic illnesses.<br>Accessibility and Abuse<br>When evaluating the accessibility risk to a child with a developmental disability, the CPS social worker should not only complete the<br>standard Structured Decision-Making tools, but also should consider the following questions:<br>•<br>Does the disability require increased physical contact, such as assistance with toileting or bathing?<br>•<br>Does the child have sufficient information about inappropriate or abusive touch to know to report maltreatment?<br>•<br>Is the child physically able to defend him/herself if necessary?<br>•<br>Does the child have access to friends or responsible adults to whom he/she could report maltreatment?<br>Stress Factors in Parents/Caregivers of Developmentally Disabled Children<br>While some disabled children may be at reduced risk for maltreatment despite the need for more nurturance, surveillance, and<br>resources, most authors agree that the presence of a child with developmental disabilities increases the stress within the family unit.<br>Considerations for the CPS social worker should include:<br>•<br>What is the parent's response to the disability? Parents may react with guilt, anger, ambivalence, and/or sorrow. Have the<br>parents received support and guidance in dealing with these feelings? Is the parental relationship suffering due to the stress of<br>caring for a disabled child?<br>•<br>Are adequate resources available to the family? Does the family have sufficient time and money to access these resources? Is<br>the family willing to use these resources?<br>•<br>What is the impact on the non-disabled siblings? Is there inappropriate assignment of responsibilities to or lack of nurturing for<br>the other children?||

# Enhanced Practice for Working with Special Populations

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|•<br>Do the parents/caregivers have realistic expectations of each child? Children with lesser degrees of impairment are thought to<br>be at higher risk for unrealistic expectations. Mildly impaired children may not be identified as disabled and their behavior may<br>be attributed to "willfulness."<br>Parental Vulnerabilities<br>Parents of developmentally disabled children are likely to be at higher risk to maltreat their children if their abilities to cope are<br>impacted by factors such as intellectual/developmental disability, mental illness, substance abuse, poverty, unemployment,<br>inadequate knowledge of or experience with child-rearing practices, and/or immaturity.<br>CPS social workers should weigh these factors in the overall assessment of the child's safety and risk of harm and the need for services<br>and resource development with the family.<br>It is known that child abuse and neglect can**cause**a full range of disabilities, ranging from mild to severe. In a 1981 study, Nora<br>Baladerian concluded that twenty-five per cent of all disabilities were caused by physical abuse or neglect. In 1991, this same<br>researcher estimated that between 32,000 and 80,000 children become disabled each year as a result of abuse or neglect. Two limited<br>but independent studies in 1971 indicated that eye disorders were present in forty percent of hospital samples of battered children,<br>suggesting strongly that abuse can result in many children with visual disabilities or blindness. Whenever a disability could have been<br>caused by maltreatment, the social worker should consider medical evaluations for the alleged victim child.<br>Deteriorating Relationships<br>Prolonged hospitalizations, institutionalization, frequent care giving by others, and early separations can interfere with parent-child<br>bonding. Problems do not end after bonding. While most children outgrow their helpless and dependent behaviors, some<br>developmentally disabled children will either be delayed in becoming capable of self-care or will never become independent.<br>Cultural Attitudes that Allow or Encourage Maltreatment<br>Historically, our society has treated disabled children as if their disability desensitized them to physical or emotional pain. A significant<br>portion of our institutionalized population consists of individuals with disabilities, suggesting a societal attitude that these individuals<br>are not harmed by institutionalization.<br>Barriers to Identification of children who are disabled that are subjected to abuse, neglect and/or dependency are:||

# Enhanced Practice for Working with Special Populations

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|•<br>Children with disabilities are more likely to be seen as unreliable reporters of abuse or neglect.<br>•<br>The client may not be able to communicate the facts in detail.<br>•<br>Symptoms of the disability may mask maltreatment; e.g.:<br>•<br>The child may be self-abusive.<br>•<br>The child may be prone to accidental injury.<br>•<br>The child may have poor judgment or impulse control and may act out inappropriately.<br>•<br>The disability may require increased touching for routine care, making identification of inappropriate touch difficult.<br>•<br>The child may be more vulnerable to attention from the perpetrator due to cognitive impairments or emotional deprivation.<br>•<br>The child may be socially isolated and may not know whom to tell.<br>•<br>The child may not have received appropriate training in sex education or prevention and may not be aware that a problem<br>exists.<br>ISSUES RELATED TO INTELLECTUAL/DEVELOPMENTAL DISABILITIES (I/DD)<br>A child with intellectual/developmental disability (I/DD) will be delayed in the mental and emotional maturation process. Depending on<br>the level of I/DD, the child may have difficulty communicating about maltreatment. The social worker will need to use interviewing<br>techniques appropriate to the mental age of the child and his/her interests. The skilled use of anatomically correct dolls, drawings, and<br>other techniques that do not require verbal skills may be useful in the assessment process. The interviewer must exercise extreme<br>caution not to suggest that any particular "answer" is sought in response to a question. The interviewer should be aware of<br>assumptions he or she may make based on the physical age of the child with I/DD. A physically mature, mentally delayed child may<br>have normal sexual responses combined with the judgment and insight of a much younger child.<br>A caregiver/parent with I/DD cannot be assumed to be incapable of offering minimally sufficient care. The assessment of maltreatment<br>must be based on the action or inaction of the caregiver/parent, as it is in any other situation.||

# Enhanced Practice for Working with Special Populations

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|ISSUES RELATED TO MOTOR IMPAIRMENTS<br>Any disability which necessitates increased physical contact, which limits the child's ability to defend him/herself, which limits a child's<br>social contact outside the living situation, and/or which increases the child's dependency on the caregiver for survival is a disability that<br>increases the child's risk for maltreatment. Reports involving children whose physical disabilities limit self-mobility must be investigated<br>with these aspects in mind. If routine care requires touching of the genitals, the child may have difficulty differentiating between<br>acceptable and non-acceptable touch.<br>If a child's physical disability is perceived to be the result of action or inaction by the parent/caregiver, that child may be a painful<br>reminder to that parent or caregiver of their feelings of guilt. Intervention plans should include consideration of the parent/caregiver's<br>acceptance of the child and his/her disability.<br>ISSUES RELATED TO SENSORY DISABILITY<br>A hearing-impaired child or family member may require a licensed interpreter and or visual aids to communicate about the<br>maltreatment. The interpreter, if used for court, should be acceptable to the court and must be able to interpret effectively, accurately,<br>and impartially. The North Carolina Interpreter and Transliterator Licensure Act (N.C.G.S. § 90D) requires that the interpreter provided<br>must be licensed to interpret. If a child is not able to communicate due to unfamiliarity with standard sign language, the regional office<br>for the hearing impaired will have a list of individuals who are familiar with local "home signs" and who can assist with interpretation.<br>For information about obtaining interpreter services, contact Carlotta Dixon with the Division of Social Services at 919-733-9461 or<br>carlotta.dixon@dhhs.nc.gov.<br>A vision-impaired child may not be able to relay information that relies on visual cues. Appropriate interview techniques will take the<br>child's perceptual abilities into consideration.<br>ISSUES RELATED TO LEARNING DISABILITIES<br>Children with learning disabilities frequently display a variety of problems that parents/caregivers may interpret as willfulness,<br>disobedience, or laziness. Such children may be more likely to be subjected to over discipline.<br>Children with learning disabilities or attention deficit disorder frequently have at least normal intellectual ability. Parents and<br>caregivers may have difficulty resolving their awareness of the child's abilities and his or her poor performance.||

Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

# Enhanced Practice for Working with Special Populations

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|If a child has been diagnosed with learning disabilities, it is important for the social worker to assess the parent/caregiver's<br>understanding of and response to the disability. Often a parent or caregiver can use effective means of helping the child by reinforcing<br>methods used in the classroom.<br>In assessing the strengths of the family, it is important for the social worker to determine the areas in which the child is successful by<br>his/her own standards. In these areas, the child may provide information regarding interests and learning abilities that can be tapped<br>for constructive planning. This can be done by reviewing the child's<br>Individualized Education Plan (IEP), talking with the child's therapist, teacher and other school staff who may have knowledge about the<br>child's learning abilities.<br>ISSUES RELATED TO SEVERE EMOTIONAL DISTURBANCE AND MENTAL ILLNESS<br>Emotional disturbance frequently results from maltreatment, but some disturbances are early manifestations of genetically transmitted<br>or drug-enhanced mental illness.<br>Children who are classified as developmentally disabled as a result of emotional disturbance or mental illness have usually displayed<br>behavior that is well beyond acceptable norms. Atypical emotional or behavioral conditions likely to be included in this category would<br>be:<br>•<br>Delay or abnormality in achieving expected emotional milestones, such as pleasurable interest in adults and peers, ability to<br>communicate emotional needs, and ability to tolerate frustration;<br>•<br>Persistent failure to initiate or respond to most social interactions;<br>•<br>Fearfulness or other distress that does not respond to comforting by caregivers;<br>•<br>Indiscriminate social interactions; and<br>•<br>Self-injurious or excessively aggressive behaviors.<br>Emotionally and mentally disturbed children are at higher risk of maltreatment because of the unpredictability of their responses to the<br>environment. This may increase the frustration of the caregiver or parent and may lead to abuse or neglect.||
