# Enhanced Practice for Working with Special Populations

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|IMPLICATIONS OF CHRONIC ILLNESS<br>•<br>A severe chronic illness is defined as a condition that lasts for a substantial period of time or that has effects that are<br>debilitating for a long period of time. Typically, a chronic illness interferes with daily functioning for greater than three months<br>a year or is likely to require hospitalization for more than one month a year. Examples include:<br>•<br>Congenital heart disease;<br>•<br>Short bowel syndrome;<br>•<br>Chronic renal failure;<br>•<br>Juvenile rheumatoid arthritis;<br>•<br>Leukemia;<br>•<br>Bronchopulmonary dysplasia (BPD);<br>•<br>Acquired Immune Deficiency Syndrome (AIDS); or<br>•<br>Hepatitis.<br>Severe chronic illness increases the stress in the family due to financial and emotional costs, de-emphasis of other issues that may<br>impact the family, separations that interfere with family bonding and resentment. These stresses may be taken out on the child in the<br>form of neglect, abandonment, physical, emotional, or sexual abuse.||

Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

# Safe Surrender/Dismissal of Safe Surrender

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|**INTRODUCTION / LEGAL BASIS**<br>During the 2023 legislative session, the Infant Homicide Prevention Act was amended (SB 20 [SL 2023-14]). This act decriminalizes<br>the abandonment of an infant who is less than thirty days old under certain circumstances and modified some of the procedures<br>when conducting CPS assessments.<br>Safe Surrender allows a parent to surrender their newborn to a designated safe person and walk away without fear of criminal<br>prosecution. Safe surrender is legal, provided that the infant is 30 days old or less and unharmed.<br>Conducting the assessment of a safely surrendered child entails using the same System of Care principles that have been<br>discussed throughout this manual.<br>A website dedicated to information regarding Safe Surrender can be found at**https://www.ncdhhs.gov/assistance/pregnancy- **<br>**services/safe-surrender**<br>**A. Legal Basis**<br>"§ 7B-520. Purpose; limitations.<br>(a) Purpose. - The purpose of this Article is to protect newborn infants by providing a<br>safe alternative for a parent who, in a crisis or in desperation, may physically abandon or harm<br>his or her newborn and to provide information for the parent regarding the parent's rights and<br>alternatives.<br>(b) Limitations. - The provisions of this Article apply exclusively to safely surrendered<br>infants as defined in G.S. 7B-101(19a). No person or agency shall act under the provisions of<br>this Article if it is determined that any of the following are true:<br>(1) A surrendered infant is reasonably believed to be more than 30 days old.<br>(2) The infant shows signs of abuse or neglect.<br>(3) There is reason to believe the individual surrendering the infant was not the<br>infant's parent.<br>(4) At the time the infant was surrendered, there was reason to believe the parent<br>intended to return for the infant.||

# Safe Surrender/Dismissal of Safe Surrender

| "§ 7B-521. Persons to whom infant may be surrendered.<br>The following individuals shall, without a court order, take into temporary custody an infant<br>reasonably believed to be not more than 30 days of age that is voluntarily delivered to the<br>individual by the infant's parent who does not express an intent to return for the infant:<br>(1) A health care provider, as defined under G.S. 90-21.11, who is on duty or at<br>a hospital or at a local or district health department or at a nonprofit<br>community health center.<br>(2) A first responder who is on duty, including a law enforcement officer, a<br>certified emergency medical services worker, or a firefighter.<br>(3) A social services worker who is on duty or at a local department of social services.<br>If the above criteria are not met, the county shall screen the report for abandonment, and any additional maltreatment concerns if<br>reported, with an Immediate response time.<br>SPECIAL CONSIDERATIONS IN INTAKE / SCREENING<br>The report shall be documented on the CPS Structured Intake Report form DSS-1402 or CWIS regardless of the residence of the<br>individual who received the infant.<br>The county that receives a report that an infant 30 days old or younger has been safely surrendered shall complete the Intake<br>process. All requirements for the completion of the Intake process shall remain the same.<br>• The county in which the infant is located shall conduct the assessment.<br>• The report is to be accepted as a result of the dependency allegations. The report is eligible to be assigned as a<br>Family Assessment at the county's discretion.<br>• If other allegations exist the report shall be screened based on all of the allegations.<br>• The report shall be assigned with an IMMEDIATE RESPONSE time in accordance with N.C.G.S.§7B-302(a).<br>CPS ASSESSMENT<br>The CPS Assessment of a safely surrendered infant does not alter any of the requirements to complete a CPS assessment. If the<br>assessment worker determines the case is a legal Safe Surrender, they are not required to search for and engage the surrendering<br>parent as part of confidentiality and anonymity provided by the Safe Surrender law. The county child welfare agency must ensure<br>the Notice of Publication occurs within 14 days of the legal Safe Surrender. Per Senate Bill 425, after notice by publication has been | |

# Safe Surrender/Dismissal of Safe Surrender

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|**DISMISSAL OF A SAFE SURRENDER**<br>**INTRODCUTION/LEGAL BASIS**<br>§ 7B-527. Rights of surrendering parent.<br>(a) Right to Regain Custody. - Prior to the filing of a termination of parental rights petition<br>under Article 11 of this Subchapter, a surrendering parent has the right to contact the<br>county department of social services where the infant was surrendered and request the<br>infant's return to his or her custody. The director shall treat any such request as a report<br>of neglect and comply with the provisions of G.S. 7B-302.<br>**SPECIAL CONSIDERATIONS FOR INTAKE/SCREENING**<br>The report shall be documented on the CPS Structured Intake Report form DSS-1402 or CWIS.<br>The county that assessed the initial report of Safe Surrender of the infant shall complete the Intake process.<br>All requirements for the completion of the Intake process shall remain the same. If the initial assessment of<br>the Safe Surrender has not been completed, the dismissal will be considered a new open report on the initial<br>Safe Surrender Assessment.<br>• <br>The report is to be accepted as a Family Assessment with a 72 hour Response time.<br>**CPS ASSESSMENT**<br>The process for a Safe Surrender Dismissal case will be different from a typical assessment. The assigned<br>worker shall begin by conducting a pre-planning conference with the permanency worker assigned to the<br>initial Safe Surrender case. The primary goal of the pre-planning conference will be to discuss any needs of<br>the child and special considerations for their care to be utilized in the assessment of the surrendering parent.<br>The resource placement for the infant must be notified of the request to dismiss the Safe Surrender after the<br>pre-planning conference.<br>The assessment worker shall conduct a face-to-face visit with the surrendering parent in the home where<br>they would be caring for the child. The worker shall interview all members of the household. Once the<br>assessment worker has interviewed all members of the household, the assessment is considered initiated.<br>The assessment worker shall coordinate with the permanency worker to see the child and communicate with<br>theplacementprovider/s regarding the development of the assessment.|Best practice would include:<br>--Participation of the supervisor/s of the<br>workers in the pre-planning conference.<br>--The permanency worker's<br>participation in the initial home<br>visit/initiation of the case.<br>--Both the assessment and permanency<br>workers presence during the initial visit<br>with the child and placement<br>provider/s.|

Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

<!-- page: 297 -->
# Safe Surrender/Dismissal of Safe Surrender

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|The assessment worker must inquire about, and include in case documentation, a detailed account of the<br>circumstances under which the parent surrendered the infant AND current circumstances of the request to<br>dismiss the Safe Surrender. This is in addition to assessment of safety of the home and the surrendering<br>parent's capacity to care for the child. The assessment worker must inquire about any information regarding<br>the non-surrendering parent, if still unknown by the Department.<br>Criminal and CPS history checks for parent/s and household members must be included in the assessment, as<br>standard in other assessment cases.<br>If at any point in the assessment additional allegations of maltreatment are reported or identified in regards to<br>the surrendering parent and other children in their care, the assessment worker will address those concerns<br>like any other open assessment and document on the Safety Assessment efforts to address danger indicators.<br>**Considerations for Completing the Safety Assessment**<br>When completing the initial Safety Assessment in a request to dismiss a Safe Surrender case, the assessment<br>worker must consider minimal standards of safety. Surrendering parents must not be held to any additional<br>standards such as licensing for foster homes.<br>If there are no identified safety concerns the Safety Assessment must be marked Safe. The dismissal of the<br>Safe Surrender isnota danger indicator on its own. The worker should conference with their supervisor, and<br>permanency worker and supervisor to begin a plan to return the child to the surrendering parent immediately,<br>and no more than 24 hours after this determination.<br>If the Safety Assessment identifies any danger indicators and can be marked, Safe with a Plan, the assessment<br>worker must document diligent efforts to provide supportive services to the parent to alleviate any safety<br>concerns throughout the course of the assessment. The assessment worker must consider and assess any<br>Temporary Safety Providers indicated by the parent, just as they would in other assessment cases.<br>In cases where the surrendering parent is actively demonstrating unsafe behaviors and removal would be<br>indicated if the child was in the home, the worker must file a petition and request non-secure custody.|<br> <br>Workers should balance the requirement<br>to obtain information from the parent<br>with compassion and empathy for the<br>complexity associated with a parental<br>decision like Safe Surrender. Workers<br>should embody the family-centered<br>principle of partnership that judgements<br>can wait throughout the entirety of all<br>assessments, but particularly in a request<br>for dismissal of Safe Surrender.<br>Best practice should include a CFT to<br>discuss shared parenting and other<br>supportive elements that will help<br>transition the child in the least disruptive<br>manner. This is for any transition for the<br>child back to the surrendering parent or<br>an identified TSP.|

Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

# Safe Surrender/Dismissal of Safe Surrender

|Case Decision Considerations<br>All required activities and Structured Decision-Making forms shall be completed prior to making a case<br>decision. The assessment worker and supervisor should consider the following circumstances with each case<br>decision option for Family Assessments:<br>• Child Protective Services Not Needed—Consider this case decision when there are no identified safety<br>concerns and the child is able to return safely to the surrendering parent, physically and legally. There<br>must have only been a SAFE Safety Assessment as part of the supporting documentation. The child<br>must be returned to the surrendering parent immediately, and no more than 24 hours after this<br>determination.<br>• Service Provided, Child Protective Services No Longer Needed—Consider this case decision when<br>there were identified safety concerns during the assessment, but it is clearly documented that diligent<br>efforts to provide services were utilized and mitigated any safety concerns. The final Safety<br>Assessment must be SAFE as part of the supporting documentation.<br>• Child Protective Services Needed—Consider this case decision when identified safety concerns during<br>the assessment period have not been alleviated or able to be addressed. Assessment workers must<br>show diligent efforts to provide supportive services to the surrendering parent.<br>o If there is a transfer to In Home services, the child must return to the surrendering parent's<br>home with services and supports that are able to maintain the child safely. A case may be<br>transferred to In Home Services with an approved Temporary Safety Provider, as well.<br>o A petition may only be filed when the assessment worker can identify and document<br>substantial risk of abuse or neglect if the child were to return to the home of the surrendering<br>parent AND diligent efforts by the agency to alleviate the risk were unsuccessful AND could<br>not be completed as a part of an In Home services case.|The agency should consider minimal<br>standards of safety and risk when making<br>a case decision for the dismissal of a Safe<br>Surrender. Surrendering parents must<br>not be held to any additional standards<br>such as licensing for foster homes.|
|---|---|

Cross Function Topics

## The Impact of Cultural Diversity

Introduction and Definitions

Introduction

The field of child protective services is being challenged to respond with knowledge and sensitivity to individuals from a wide diversity of cultural backgrounds. In North Carolina, children's services caseloads represent the diversity of the state. This population is enriched with significant representation of Native Americans and a variety of ethnic and cultural groups from Asia, the Middle East, Central and Latin America, and Europe. Each culture brings its own values, customs, beliefs and language.

North Carolina Department of Health and Human Service's Division of Social Services (NC DHHS | DSS) remains committed to the safety, permanence and wellbeing needs of children and families, while ensuring the rights of children, biological parents, foster and adoptive parents are protected as outlined in the Multiethnic Placement Act (MEPA) and the Interethnic Provisions (IEP) of 1996 relating to the race, color, or national origin of the child and/or family involved.

The North Carolina Division of Social Services and county departments of social services are prohibited from discriminating for reasons of race, ethnicity, color, national origin, sex (including on the basis of sexual orientation and on the basis of gender identity), religion, age, disability, and/or political beliefs. This includes when working with youth, families, parents, caregivers, kinship providers, adoptive parents and licensed foster parents.

Definitions

Culture is described as "languages, customs, beliefs, rules, arts, knowledge and collective identities and memories developed by members of all social groups that make their social environments meaningful."

Race is a social construct reflective of the physical characteristics of individuals. While ethnicity represents the shared characteristics of individuals which may include "ancestry, language or culture." Child welfare professionals should engage children and families mindful of cultural implications which makes each individual unique. Maintaining levels of equality, (treating everyone the same) compared to equity (different levels of engagement in efforts of achieving fairness with regards to individual conditions) may be a challenge for child welfare professionals but can be mitigated by increased knowledge development which result in behavioral indicators representative of culturally competent social work practices. Discrimination, biased treatment of individuals based on race, color or national origin, is in direct violation to the Multiethnic Placement Act (MEPA) and the Interethnic Provisions (IEP) and social work practices. Cultural competence involves the ability to "understand, communicate and effectively interact with people across cultures." Child welfare professionals in North Carolina will demonstrate culturally competent practices when engaging with children and families.

# The Impact of Homelessness

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|When a child is a member of a homeless family, the social worker is sometimes confronted with the responsibility for determining<br>whether or not a homeless child is being abused or neglected or is dependent. In this situation, it is critical to determine whether or not<br>a child's basic needs are being met, whether or not the parent is able to reasonably protect the child from harm, and whether or not<br>temporary care with relatives, kin or others might help the parent to locate employment and permanent housing.<br>Assessment of Abuse/Neglect of Homeless Families<br>•<br>Does the child have living arrangements that are reasonably safe and sanitary, or is the environment clearly injurious due to<br>illicit activities, physical endangerment, and/or unhealthful conditions?<br>•<br>Does the child have reasonable outlets for appropriate socialization with peers?<br>•<br>Does the child have untreated medical problems?<br>•<br>Is the school-aged child enrolled in school?<br>•<br>Does the family have access to minimal sanitary facilities, including working toilets, bathing facilities, and laundry facilities?<br>•<br>Is the child under age twelve supervised by a responsible adult? Are responsible adults available in the event of an emergency?<br>•<br>Does the child indicate emotional or physical damage resulting directly or indirectly from homelessness?<br>•<br>Are alternate, stable living arrangements readily available to the child?<br>•<br>Could this situation meet minimum standards of care with available community resources? If so, is the parent/caregiver willing<br>to enroll the child in these resources?<br>Could the worker and family collaborate with Work First to address the family's issues?||

# The Impact of Drug and Alcohol Abuse

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|Prenatal Exposure<br>North Carolina child protective services laws do not address abuse or neglect of the unborn child. The definition of a juvenile includes<br>unemancipated children from birth to eighteen years. However, federal law requires reports of children born with a positive toxicology<br>for illegal substances must be accepted for CPS assessment to develop a protection plan. While the prenatal drug exposure does not<br>constitute neglect per se, the DSS has a responsibility to assure that the living environment will not be injurious to the newborn. Some<br>of the issues to be considered during the assessment, as well as during the provision of CPS In-Home Services, are:<br>•<br>Are other children living in the home? Are siblings well cared for? Are immunizations current? Do siblings attend school on a<br>regular basis?<br>•<br>What is the infant's medical condition? Are there special care needs? How medically fragile is the child? To what extent is the<br>child irritable or lethargic? Is there special equipment or medication needs?<br>•<br>What medical follow-up is needed for the child? Does the child need to be tested for HIV?<br>•<br>What is the parent's pattern and history of drug use? What drug(s) were taken? How often? Is either parent using drugs now?<br>What is the method of use? Are other household members suspected to be involved in drug activity? Is there a history of<br>violence within the household?<br>•<br>What is the parent's history of drug treatment? Is the mother in treatment now? Is she considered to be compliant with<br>treatment?<br>•<br>Does the parent have physical, intellectual, or emotional problems that would impact his or her ability to care for the child?<br>•<br>Does the parent exhibit appropriate parenting skills and seem responsive to the infant's needs? Did the mother have prenatal<br>care? Are her expectations and perceptions realistic?<br>•<br>What is the parent's age and school/work history?||

Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

# The Impact of Drug and Alcohol Abuse

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|•<br>Have there been previous allegations of abuse, neglect, or dependency?<br>•<br>What supports are available? Are there responsible family members or friends who can assist the parent in caring for the child?<br>Does the parent have established supports within the community; i.e., church or community group?<br>•<br> What are the environmental conditions of the home? Is the home clean? Are utilities working? Are there safety hazards? Is<br>there evidence of preparation for the infant's arrival?<br>Research suggests that at least eleven percent of babies born in this country have been prenatally exposed to illegal drugs. The overall<br>rate of drug use by pregnant women does not seem to be significantly related to social class, income, or ethnicity.<br>Addictive Drugs and Alcohol and the Impact on Abusive and Neglectful Behavior<br>The continued use of addictive drugs and alcohol by the parent places the child at increased risk for neglect, abuse, and dependency.<br>Users may display poor judgment due to their decreased ability to evaluate the reality of their situation. They may not be conscious<br>that they are neglecting their children's needs. They may be focused on their own desire for more drugs or alcohol while placing the<br>needs of the child at a lower priority. Alcohol and drug use decrease self-control and frequently lead to violence in the environment. If<br>the use of these substances results in intoxication, the child may be left without effective supervision for extended periods of time.<br>**CPS In-Home Services**<br>Breaking a drug addiction is difficult. Denial and relapses are common. Structure and accountability are essential. Therefore,**use**the<br>Structured Decision-Making tools and the Family Services Agreements**throughout the life of the case.**Be sure to address the need for<br>medical care appointments, access and cooperation with health care resources, participation in drug treatment, routine drug screens,<br>and visitation planning if the child is placed out of the home. Whenever working with a child and family, address the child wellbeing<br>issues.||

# Responding to Reports of Methamphetamine Exposure

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|
|---|---|
|North Carolina continues to be confronted with methamphetamine (sometimes shortened as "meth") problems that are unmatched by<br>other illegal drugs in the past. Recognizing that there are other drugs that are produced in laboratories, due to the extreme volatility<br>and dangers, methamphetamine production poses to North Carolina communities, this policy section focuses on methamphetamine<br>laboratories. Methamphetamine is a synthetic narcotic that is inexpensive and easy to make with ingredients that are relatively easy to<br>obtain (although on January 15, 2006, the North Carolina General Assembly passed one of the toughest laws in the country restricting<br>the sales of cold medicines that contain pseudoephedrine, a primary precursor ingredient in methamphetamine production). The<br>chemicals, production process and the waste generated by the production of methamphetamine in laboratories pose very serious<br>dangers to public safety and to the environment. Some of these dangers include toxic poisoning, chemical and thermal burns, fires and<br>explosions. During the production of methamphetamine (known as "cooking" or "the cook") one pound of methamphetamine can<br>generally yield five to seven pounds of toxic waste with this waste being introduced into the environment by burning or dumping.<br>These laboratories continue to pose very real dangers to the communities in which they are located. The majority of methamphetamine<br>laboratories discovered have been located in the western part of the state. However, since 2001 there has been a gradual progression<br>in the number of discovered laboratories eastward across the state. In 1999, there were only nine confirmed methamphetamine<br>laboratories in North Carolina. That number rose to 16 in 2000 (a 78% increase), 34 in 2001 (a 113% increase from the previous year),<br>98 in 2002 (a 188% increase from the previous year) and 177 in 2003. That is a 1,867% increase in the number of laboratories<br>discovered within a four-year period and clearly underscores the increase of public awareness in the dangers of methamphetamine<br>production in North Carolina. During the 1999-2003 timeframe, as the number of confirmed laboratories continued to grow, North<br>Carolina also witnessed an increase in the size of the laboratories that were raided. "Super labs" are laboratories that produce ten or<br>more pounds of methamphetamine at a time.<br>Nationally, children have been found in approximately 25-30% percent of raided laboratories. The children who live in and around<br>methamphetamine laboratories have a high risk of harm due to their developmental nature, the abuse and neglect that their<br>parents/caretakers and others who frequent the home inflict on them and their inability to protect themselves. The children in these<br>homes are also exposed to serious toxicities and dangers that could have long-term effects on their health and development. The long-<br>term effects of child exposure to methamphetamine production continues to be an area of intense study.<br>Representatives from several county and state agencies came together as a work group to address the issues of methamphetamine<br>laboratories and safety for the children and the professionals that respond to these sites. The North Carolina Division of Social Services<br>has taken the lead in writing policy and protocol with the help of this work group. Due to the safety risks to children, county child<br>welfare agency staff responding to these situations, and the communities in which these laboratories are located, it is imperative that||

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance|

|guidelines and procedures for responding to methamphetamine laboratories are in place. It is also very important that everyone have<br>clear role definitions in regard to responding to a methamphetamine laboratory site that involves children. The North Carolina General<br>Assembly appropriated funding for policy development and training for county child welfare workers. Because of the seriousness of the<br>situation, the 2003 legislative session, the General Assembly passed House Bill 1510 and House Bill 1536 that imposes stiffer penalties<br>for possession of precursor substances and manufacturing methamphetamine in the presence of a person under the age of 18.<br>**Purpose**<br>Responding to a suspected methamphetamine laboratory where children are involved requires a coordinated approach involving a<br>multidisciplinary team to ensure everyone's safety. Those who make methamphetamine most often use methamphetamine, making<br>them prone to aggression, extreme paranoia, visual and tactile hallucinations, depression, irritability, and "crashes." These side effects<br>make the user's behavior unpredictable. Methamphetamine is a powerful stimulant and produces physiological changes similar to the<br>"fight or flight response" and, thus, can trigger violent tendencies in the users. Methamphetamine users have been known to use<br>weapons, explosives, traps and surveillance equipment to protect and keep their operations secret.<br>The purpose of a multidisciplinary protocol is to provide local professionals with specific procedures for response to situations where<br>there are drug-endangered children because of methamphetamine laboratories or other drug production, trafficking and abuse. The<br>Division strongly recommends that Memoranda of Agreement or Memoranda of Understanding among these local multidisciplinary<br>teams be developed, reviewed and updated regularly (i.e., annually) to formalize roles and relationships at the local level.<br>Implementing a Drug Endangered Children (DEC) protocol that has been developed with local community requirements ensures that<br>children who may be at risk for exposure to methamphetamine production and the risks inherently found in methamphetamine<br>laboratories receive protection, advocacy and support. This protocol also ensures that law enforcement investigations provide the best<br>opportunity for prosecution, while ensuring the safety of local professionals (social workers, first responders, etc.).<br>This policy is designed to outline the process for county child welfare agency responses to methamphetamine laboratories when<br>children are involved, as well as provide guidance around developing the local multidisciplinary protocol. While the dangerous nature of<br>responding to methamphetamine laboratories requires some very different responses by county child welfare agencies (responses that<br>may seem contrary to the usual practices in child welfare), many of the usual policies and procedures that apply across child welfare will<br>remain the same. In the following policy, special attention will focus on the unusual requirements in responding to methamphetamine<br>laboratories, while referring to the existing child welfare policy when appropriate.||

<!-- page: 325 -->
## Safe Sleep

Safe Sleep and Nighttime Parenting

The North Carolina Division of Social Services recognizes that infants require developmentally appropriate parenting to ensure they are able to grow and advance during one of the most fragile times of their lives.

The purpose of this document is to provide local county child welfare workers guidance on assessing and ensuring safe sleep practices are being followed in the home until the child reaches one year of age. The following safe infant sleep recommendations applies to infants from birth to 12 months of age. This includes preterm infants' home from the Neonatal Intensive Care Unit (NICU) and infants with reflux. It also provides resources to educate parents and caretakers on how to individualize implementation of infant safe sleep practices within their homes.

Definitions - Terminology Glossary

Nighttime Parenting: The acknowledgement that caring for an infant requires the parent/caregiver to be responsive to the infant's needs at night, not just during the daytime. This is especially true for infants who wake throughout the night. This can be challenging for parents/caregivers and requires extra thought, support, and preparation to ensure the safe care of infant, including the practice of safe sleep.

Safe Sleep for Infants

Prior to the first year of life, infants need anywhere from 12 to 17 hours of sleep daily1. Unsafe sleep practices are a leading cause of death for North Carolina infants. Many of these deaths could have been prevented. This means that determining infant safety must include ensuring the physical environment is equipped to accommodate this need. It also means that child welfare workers must assess the caretaker's capacity to provide suitable arrangements and parenting that keep infants safe particularly when sleeping. Knowledge of state and local resources available to assist infants is paramount to supporting families with practicing safe sleep.

Families often have misinformation and/or do not follow infant safe sleep practices and child welfare workers should consider engaging parents and caretakers in the discussion of infant safe sleep by:

• Ask open-ended questions to encourage honest more in-depth conversations. • Show respect and understanding of individual family's beliefs, needs, and concerns. Allow them to share their family's perspective on safe sleep.

Cross Function Topics (Nov 2025) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

<!-- page: 326 -->
## Safe Sleep

• Probe for questions or concerns regarding the recommendations. Engaging the family in open dialogue can allow the worker to continue to assess and work to develop an agreeable safe sleep plan for the family. • Expect resistance or questions based on past experience, social and family influence, and cultural practices. It is helpful to remember that families may receive differing information from perceived credible sources. Workers should not engage in a conflict with the family, but work to negotiate a safe sleep plan everyone can agree on. • Work together with the family to reduce the risk of unsafe sleep practices for each unique family/caregiver. This may require each family member/caregiver to have a separate sleep plan.

SAFE SLEEP PRACTICES Infants and children under the age of one should always be placed to sleep on a firm and flat mattress in a safety approved crib, bassinet, or portable crib. The sleep space should only include a mattress and a fitted sheet. No other items should be in the sleep space, including blankets, pillows, toys, bumper pads, and/or sleep positioners. It is very dangerous for an infant to sleep in an inclined seat, adult bed, recliner, or sofa. The infant should not sleep on the same sleep space with another person. Infants can share a room with the caregiver but should not share a sleep space. Parents who bring their infant into the bed for feeding must remove all soft items and bedding from the area. The infant should be placed on their back in their own sleep space when finished.

The infant should be placed on their back for every nap and at night—never on their side or stomach. An infant may roll to their side or back on their own, but they should always be placed to sleep on their back. At night, the caretaker should be providing care in a space that allows them to remain awake while providing care and ensure that the infant is returned to a safe sleep space. It is recommended that infants who are still waking multiple times to feed be kept in the room close to the caretaker's bed, but on a separate sleep surface designed for infants making it easier to feed, comfort, and watch the baby at night.

It is also recommended that the caretaker set a timer to ensure that the baby is placed back in the safe sleep space in case they fall asleep. The caretaker should avoid couches and armchairs for feeding or caring for infants when there is a concern they might fall asleep. It may be helpful for a caretaker to consider feeding the child in a dining chair to assist with remaining awake. In cases where there are multiple caregivers taking turns providing care to the infant can help to ensure that the infant makes it back to a safe sleep space each time. Be sure that anyone who provides care for your infant follows safe sleep recommendations.

Any smoking or vaping use should be done outside of the home and away from the infant. Smoke exposure has been shown to increase the risk of infants dying of sleep-related causes, including SIDS. The caretaker should change clothes and wash hands prior to touching the infant again. The care of an infant should only be provided by a sober parent/caregiver. Substances include alcohol, marijuana, legal and illegal drugs and medications. This can also include over the counter cold and sleep aid medications that can impair a parents' capacity to provide care for the infant. Substance use can make parents/caretakers sleepy and less aware creating difficulties in their ability to care for an infant. This makes it harder to engage in safe sleep practices and sleeping with an infant very dangerous.

|**CPS INTAKE**<br>When a report includes an infant and the reporter is aware of the infant's sleep space, the intake worker must<br>document the infant's sleep space, how they are placed to sleep, and if the infant sleeps alone (not sharing the same<br>sleep surface with anyone). If the reporter has the answer to these questions, it will allow the assessment worker to<br>better prepare for the initial meeting with the family. It is required to document any concerns regarding substance<br>use by caretakers. Additionally, inquiring about how substance use (legal or illegal, treatment or recreational) affects<br>the caretaker's ability to ensure the infants' safety must also be thoroughly screened and documented. The intake<br>worker must request information including which substance(s) is being used, the level of use, and whether their<br>ability to provide care for the infant has been impacted.<br>**CPS FAMILY AND INVESTIGATIVE ASSESSMENTS**<br>When an accepted assessment includes an infant, safe sleep practices must be addressed with the caretakers. When<br>information included in the report alleges unsafe sleep practices the assessment worker should bring resources and<br>be prepared to provide educational information, such as the practice guide to the family and assist them in<br>developing a safe sleep plan. The practice guide included in the Child Welfare Manual can be used to help workers<br>prepare for and provide education to families. The worker should use this time to identify and build the Safety<br>Network which can be a useful tool in helping the family enlarge the number of supports they have in providing care<br>for an infant. While engaging the Safety Network the worker should ensure that each member of the network<br>understands the importance of maintaining the safe sleep plan that has been developed.<br>When completing a thorough CPS Assessment that addresses safe sleep it must include:||

# Safe Sleep

| • Observation of where the infant sleeps. This should include all spaces that the caretaker places the infant to<br>sleep.<br>• Demonstration by the caretaker of how to position the infant for sleep. The infant should be placed on a firm,<br>flat surface and on their backs to sleep each time. Child welfare workers should assist caretakers displaying<br>difficulty with positioning with education and demonstration as necessary. Please refer to the practice guide<br>for additional resources.<br>• Identification of all substance use activity, including tobacco. Substance use that can impair the caretaker's<br>ability to provide care for the infant, must be discussed and thoroughly documented. Additionally, a plan<br>must be made to ensure the safety of the infant while the parent or caretaker's ability to parent is impaired.<br>If there are allegations an infant has been affected by substance use, a Plan of Safe Care (POSC) must be<br>developed which would include a plan to keep the infant safe when a parent's ability is impaired by using<br>substances. In cases where parents are using over the counter medications such as cold medicines or sleep<br>aids as prescribed when it impairs their capacity to provide care, a POSC should not be developed. However,<br>the worker should work with the family to ensure there is a plan for the infant's care while under the<br>influence of the medications and it should be documented as a part of the safe sleep plan on the Safety<br>Assessment.<br>• A plan that ensures that the infant will be placed in a safe sleep environment every time they sleep, to<br>include naps and at night. In cases where the family does not have a designated safe sleep space and the<br>county or a community agency provides Pack N Plays, they should be provided to the family. The family can<br>also be asked to empty out a dresser drawer or laundry basket and put it on the floor without heavy items in<br>it and place the child there.<br>• A strategy that addresses housing inconsistencies when a parent or caretaker is homeless or transient. In<br>situations where a family does not have stable housing it is of the utmost importance that each visit includes<br>a thorough assessment of where the infant is sleeping. It may require additional planning with the family at<br>each transition to ensure that infant safe sleep is being practiced.<br>There must be documentation that includes all the elements listed above. The NC Safety Assessment (DSS-5231)<br>must include the actions to address any concerns identified regarding safe sleep on page seven in the Temporary<br>Parental Safety Agreement. The use of safe sleep practices and the plan to address safe sleep must be assessed at<br>each follow up visit to the home. The DSS-5231 must be updated to include any plans to address any newly identified<br>safe sleep concerns as they arise during the assessment. This must include any new safety concerns as well as<br>ongoing concerns regarding a family's use of safe sleep. In cases where a Temporary Safety Provider is identified they<br>must provide a physical environment in compliance with and agree to parent consistent with infant safe sleep. This<br>must also be thoroughly documented. | |

# Safe Sleep

| IN-HOME SERVICES<br>When a case has been found Services Needed and there are infants within the family, a safe sleep plan must be<br>included as part of the In-Home Family Services Agreement (DSS-5239). There must be a continued assessment of<br>infant safe sleep practices throughout the life of the In-Home case and at every visit until the child reaches the age of<br>one. This must be documented monthly on the North Carolina Monthly In-Home Contact Record (DSS-5236). All<br>caretakers providing care of the infant should be provided with education as needed and as new questions arise.<br>PERMANENCY PLANNING<br>Birth parents, kinship caretakers, and foster and adoptive parents must be educated on infant safe sleep practices to<br>ensure that they are equipped with the most current information. It is critical that child welfare workers providing<br>foster care through adoption services confirm that homes where infants are placed are in adherence with safe sleep<br>practices. Foster Care and Adoption workers must document the infant safe sleep space and practices until the infant<br>is one year of age. All safe sleep discussions and any practice concerns should be documented on the North Carolina<br>Monthly Permanency Planning Contact Record (DSS-5295) at least monthly. Foster Home Licensing Workers are<br>required to do quarterly visits to ensure a safe environment and that licensing standards are met which includes a<br>safe sleep space and practice. It is also vitally important that infants have a safety approved sleep space (crib,<br>bassinet, or portable crib) that meets current standards and requirements prior to the infant being placed in the<br>home. | |
